Summary [SOURCE: Page_7]

In many developing countries, poor water quality poses a major threat to human health [SOURCE: Page_7]

and the lack of access to clean drinking water and adequate sanitation continue to be a major [SOURCE: Page_7]

brake on social and economical development. Urbanization and untreated domestic and [SOURCE: Page_7]

industrial wastewater are significant sources of organic carbon (OC) and faecal bacteria in [SOURCE: Page_7]

aquatic ecosystems. This is particularly problematic in developing countries where efficient [SOURCE: Page_7]

wastewater treatment is lacking and where human populations are rapidly increasing, becoming [SOURCE: Page_7]

more urban and increasingly industrialized. Waterborne pathogens and OC from wastewater are [SOURCE: Page_7]

particularly susceptible to shifts in water flow and quality and the predicted increases in rainfall [SOURCE: Page_7]

and floods due to climate change will only exacerbate the problems of contamination. It is [SOURCE: Page_7]

therefore imperative that we have an understanding of the distribution and the factors that [SOURCE: Page_7]

control the distribution and dispersion of water borne pathogens. The Red River is the second [SOURCE: Page_7]

largest river in Viet Nam and constitutes the main water source for a large percentage of the [SOURCE: Page_7]

population of North Viet Nam. This thesis presents the results from observations and modeling [SOURCE: Page_7]

of both faecal indicator bacteria (FIB) and dissolved and particulate organic carbon (DOC and [SOURCE: Page_7]

POC) in the Red River basin, North Viet Nam. The objective of this work was to obtain [SOURCE: Page_7]

information on the numbers of two FIB (Escherichia coli and total coliforms (TC)) and OC in [SOURCE: Page_7]

the Red River and then to model these variables in order to investigate scenarios of the system [SOURCE: Page_7]

on the 2050 horizon when the population in the area is estimated to have doubled. [SOURCE: Page_7]

For E.coli and TC, the results from 10 stations along the Red River showed that TC [SOURCE: Page_7]

numbers reached as high as 39,100 colony forming units (CFU) 100 ml-1, values that are [SOURCE: Page_7]

considerably higher than the clean water limits set by QCVN 02:2009/BYT of the Viet Namese [SOURCE: Page_7]

Government (50 CFU 100 ml-1 for informal domestic water quality). Significant seasonal [SOURCE: Page_7]

differences were found for FIB with numbers being higher during the wet season. E.coli and TC [SOURCE: Page_7]

6 [SOURCE: Page_7]

die-off rates ranged from 0.01 d-1 to 1.33 d-1 and were significantly higher for free bacteria than [SOURCE: Page_8]

for total (free + particle attached) bacteria, suggesting that particle attachment provided a certain [SOURCE: Page_8]

level of protection to E.coli and TC in this highly turbid river system. This data, along with [SOURCE: Page_8]

other data collected from a range of sources on TC numbers was then modeled using the [SOURCE: Page_8]

Seneque/Riverstrahler model in order to investigate the dynamics and seasonal distribution of [SOURCE: Page_8]

E.coli and TC in the Red River (Northern Viet Nam) and its upstream tributaries. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_8]

although many studies have been published on the use of models to assess water quality through [SOURCE: Page_8]

fecal contamination levels, the vast majority of this work has been conducted in developed [SOURCE: Page_8]

countries and similar studies from developing countries in tropical regions are lacking. The [SOURCE: Page_8]

results of the model show that, in general, the overall correlations between the simulated and [SOURCE: Page_8]

observed values of TC follow a 1: 1 relationship. They also show that TC numbers are affected [SOURCE: Page_8]

by both land use in terms of human and livestock populations and by hydrology (river [SOURCE: Page_8]

discharge). The importance of diffuse sources of TC over point sources in this system was [SOURCE: Page_8]

demonstrated, especially in the upstream part of the Red River. The scenario, based on the [SOURCE: Page_8]

predicted changes in future demographics and land use in the Red River basin for the 2050 [SOURCE: Page_8]

horizon, showed only a limited increase of TC numbers compared with the present situation at [SOURCE: Page_8]

all station. This was particularly the case in Ha Noi even though the population is expected to [SOURCE: Page_8]

double by 2050. DOC and POC concentrations were also measured and modeled along the Red [SOURCE: Page_8]

River. The model results reflected the importance of land use, discharge and the dominance of [SOURCE: Page_8]

non-point sources over point sources in this network. Indeed, as for E.coli and TC, the [SOURCE: Page_8]

concentrations observed reflected the large amounts of industrial effluent, agricultural runoff, [SOURCE: Page_8]

and domestic sewage that are discharged into the surface water of this river system. The model [SOURCE: Page_8]

also allowed determining the net ecosystem metabolism in terms of OC respiration over the [SOURCE: Page_8]

whole delta. It was found that the OC inputs to the Red River and the resulting heterotrophic [SOURCE: Page_8]

respiration of this OC resulted in a system that was a strong CO$_2$ source. Recognizing and [SOURCE: Page_8]

7 [SOURCE: Page_8]

understanding the link between human activities, natural process and microbial functioning and [SOURCE: Page_9]

their ultimate impacts on human health are prerequisites for reducing the risks to the exposed [SOURCE: Page_9]

populations. This work in tropical systems has been based on the application of a model [SOURCE: Page_9]

developed on temperate environment after checking its applicability or appropriateness of the [SOURCE: Page_9]

biogeochemical mechanisms for tropical environments. This thesis provides some new [SOURCE: Page_9]

information on E.coli and TC and on OC in the Red River, Viet Nam as well as providing a base [SOURCE: Page_9]

for discussion with decision makers on the future management of wastewater in the Red River [SOURCE: Page_9]

basin. [SOURCE: Page_9]

Keywords: Red River, Faecal Indicator Bacteria, Organic Matter, Seneque/Riverstrahler model, [SOURCE: Page_9]

human impacts [SOURCE: Page_9]

8 [SOURCE: Page_9]

Tóm tắt [SOURCE: Page_10]

Ở các nước đang phát triển, ô nhiễm nước đặt ra mối đe dọa lớn đối với sức khỏe con [SOURCE: Page_10]

người và thiếu nước sạch và vệ sinh môi trường vẫn tiếp tục là vấn đề chính cho sự phát triển. [SOURCE: Page_10]

Đô thị hóa và nước thải sinh hoạt và công nghiệp không được xử lý là nguồn cung cấp đáng kể [SOURCE: Page_10]

carbon hữu cơ (OC) và vi khuẩn vào các hệ sinh thái thủy sinh. Điều này đang là vấn đề đặc biệt [SOURCE: Page_10]

quan trọng ở các nước đang phát triển, nơi hiệu quả xử lý nước thải còn yếu kém và nơi dân số [SOURCE: Page_10]

đang gia tăng nhanh chóng, với tốc độ đô thị hoá và công nghiệp hóa tăng cao. Ô nhiễm vi sinh [SOURCE: Page_10]

và OC từ nước thải ảnh hưởng tới dòng chảy và chất lượng nước, đồng thời với gia tăng lượng [SOURCE: Page_10]

mưa và lũ lụt do biến đổi khí hậu sẽ làm các vấn đề ô nhiễm trầm trọng thêm. Như vậy, bắt buộc [SOURCE: Page_10]

chúng ta nên có sự hiểu biết về phân bố và các yếu tố ảnh hưởng tới sự phân bối và phát tán của [SOURCE: Page_10]

các tác nhân gây bệnh trongnguồn nước. Sông Hồng là con sông lớn thứ hai tại Việt Nam và là [SOURCE: Page_10]

nguồn cung cấp nước chính cho bộ phận lớn dân cưở miền Bắc Việt Nam. Luận án này trình [SOURCE: Page_10]

bày các kết quảthu được từ những quan trắc thực tế và kết quả mô phỏng từ mô hình về các chỉ [SOURCE: Page_10]

tiêu vi khuẩn chỉ thị phân (FIB) và cacbon hữu cơ dạng hòa tan và dạng hạt (DOC và POC) [SOURCE: Page_10]

trong lưu vực sông Hồng, miền Bắc Việt Nam. Mục đích của nghiên cứu này là để thu được [SOURCE: Page_10]

những thông tin về FIB và OC trên hệ thống sông Hồng và sau đó nghiên cứu ứng dụng mô hình [SOURCE: Page_10]

mô phỏng các thông số này theo các kịch bản năm 2050 khi dân số ở khu vực này được ước tính [SOURCE: Page_10]

tăng gấp đôi. [SOURCE: Page_10]

Về FIB, kết quả quan trắc tại 10 trạm dọc theo sông Hồng cho thấy giá trị FIB đạt tới [SOURCE: Page_10]

39.100 MPN 100 ml-1, cao hơn rất nhiều lần so với giới hạn cho phép về chất lượng nước sinh [SOURCE: Page_10]

hoạt (50MPN 100 ml-1 cho nguồn cung cấp nước sinh hoạt theo QCVN02: 2009/BYT của [SOURCE: Page_10]

Chính phủ Việt Nam). Có sự khác biệt đáng kể theo mùa đối với FIB, trong đó các giá trị cao [SOURCE: Page_10]

hơn đáng kể đã được quan sát thấy trong mùa mưa. Tốc độ chết của FIB dao động từ 0,01 ngày-1 [SOURCE: Page_10]

đến 1,33 ngày-1, trong đó tốc độ chết của FIB tự do cao hơn đáng kể so với FIB tổng số (tự do [SOURCE: Page_10]

9 [SOURCE: Page_10]

+ gắn kết), điều này cho thấy dạng vi khuẩn gắn kết cung cấp một mức độ bảo vệ nhất định cho [SOURCE: Page_11]

FIB trong hệ thống song có độ đục lớn. Kết quả này cùng với các số liệu khác thu thập từ nhiều [SOURCE: Page_11]

nguồn khác nhau về FIB sau đó được mô hình hóa nhờ sử dụng mô hình Seneque / Riverstrahler [SOURCE: Page_11]

để điều tra về động học và phân bố theo mùa của FIB ở sông Hồng (Bắc Việt Nam) và các [SOURCE: Page_11]

nhánh chính thượng nguồn. Mặc dù nhiều nghiên cứu đã được công bố về việc sử dụng các mô [SOURCE: Page_11]

hình để đánh giá chất lượng nước thông qua mức độ ô nhiễm phân, nhưng phần lớn các nghiên [SOURCE: Page_11]

cứu này đã được tiến hành ở các nước phát triển và thiếu các nghiên cứu tương tự từ các nước [SOURCE: Page_11]

đang phát triển ở các vùng nhiệt đới. Các kết quả của mô hình chỉ ra rằng, nhìn chung, các mối [SOURCE: Page_11]

tương quan tổng thể giữa các giá trị mô phỏng và giá trị quan trắc của FIB theo mối quan hệ tỉ lệ [SOURCE: Page_11]

1: 1. Kết quả của mô hình cũng chỉ ra rằng giá trị FIB trong nước sông đang chịu ảnh hưởng bởi [SOURCE: Page_11]

cả hai yếu tố là tình hình sử dụng đất, liên quan tới dân số và số lượng gia súc –gia cầm được [SOURCE: Page_11]

chăn nuôi trong lưu vực, cũng như yếu tố thủy văn của hệ thống sông (lưu lượng nước sông). [SOURCE: Page_11]

Như vậy, mức độ quan trọng của nguồn thải phát tán so với nguồn thải điểm cung cấp FIB trong [SOURCE: Page_11]

hệ thống sông Hồng đã được chứng minh. Kết quả mô phỏng kịch bản, dựa trên sự thay đổi [SOURCE: Page_11]

trong tương lai về dân số và sử dụng đất trong lưu vực sông Hồng năm 2050, cho thấy giá trị [SOURCE: Page_11]

FIB tăng rất ít so với kết quả mô phỏng cho thời điểm hiện tại ở tất cả các trạm, điều này là đặc [SOURCE: Page_11]

biệt đối với trường hợp tại trạm Hà Nội, khi mà dân số dự kiến sẽ tăng gấp đôi vào năm 2050. [SOURCE: Page_11]

Hàm lượng DOC và POC cũng được đo đạc và mô phỏng cho các vị trí dọc theo sông [SOURCE: Page_11]

Hồng. Các kết quả mô hình phản ánh mức độ quan trọng của tình hình sử dụng đất, lưu lượng [SOURCE: Page_11]

nước và nguồn thải phát tán hơn so với nguồn thải điểm trong mạng lưới sông Hồng. Cũng như [SOURCE: Page_11]

đối với FIB, hàm lượng OC cũng phản ánh ảnh hưởng của nước thải công nghiệp, nông nghiệp [SOURCE: Page_11]

và nước thải sinh hoạt được thải trực tiếp vào nguồn nước mặt của hệ thống sông này. Mô hình [SOURCE: Page_11]

này cũng cho phép xác định các quá trình chuyển hóa của mạng lưới sinh thái về mặt trao đổi [SOURCE: Page_11]

OC trong toàn bộ vùng đồng bằng. Nguồn cung cấp đầu vào của OC cho sông Hồng và kết quả [SOURCE: Page_11]

của hô hấp dị dưỡng của các OC này đã tạo ra một nguồn CO$_2$ lớn trong hệ thống sông. [SOURCE: Page_11]

10 [SOURCE: Page_11]

Nhận biết và hiểu được mối liên hệ giữa các hoạt động của con người, quá trình tự nhiên, [SOURCE: Page_12]

hoạt động của vi sinh vật và các tác động cuối cùng của chúng đến sức khỏe con người là điều [SOURCE: Page_12]

kiện tiên quyết cho việc giảm rủi ro cho các người dân tiếp xúc với nguồn nước ô nhiễm. Những [SOURCE: Page_12]

nghiên cứu như vậy cho vùng nhiệt đới này đã được tiến hành dựa trên việc áp dụng mô hình [SOURCE: Page_12]

được xây dựng và phát triển cho vùng ôn đới sau khi kiểm tra khả năng áp dụng hoặc phù hợp [SOURCE: Page_12]

của nó theo các cơ chế sinh địa hóa cho môi trường nhiệt đới. Luận án này cung cấp một số [SOURCE: Page_12]

thông tin mới về FIB và OC ở sông Hồng, Việt Nam cũng như cung cấp một cơ sở khoa học cho [SOURCE: Page_12]

các nhà hoạch định chính sách về quản lý nước thải trong hệ thống sông Hồng trong tương lai. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Từ khóa: Red River Delta, Vi khuẩn chỉ thị phân, Chất hữu cơ, mô hình Seneque/Riverstrahler, tác [SOURCE: Page_12]

động của con người. [SOURCE: Page_12]

### 1.1.    Human activities, microbial pathogens and organic carbon

Rivers are the major source of fresh water for industry, agriculture, domestic and leisure [SOURCE: Page_16]

use. However, the conversion of natural landscapes to agriculture and increasing urbanization and [SOURCE: Page_16]

industrialization has lead to drastic changes in water quality in many of the World’s rivers [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Vorosmarty et al., 2010). This problem is particularly pressing in developing countries where the [SOURCE: Page_16]

rapid, recent industrialization and urbanization has lead to dramatic decreases in water quality [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Kumar et al., 2014). Moreover, the consequences of human activities on water quality are all the [SOURCE: Page_16]

more critical in these regions of the world where wastewater treatment facilities are often [SOURCE: Page_16]

overloaded or inexistent and many people are exposed to illness and death through the use of [SOURCE: Page_16]

unclean water (UNICEF/WHO, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_16]

Increasing urbanization, industrialization, agriculture and plantation forestry have been all [SOURCE: Page_16]

been linked to reduced water quality and ecological degradation in the Red River, Viet Nam [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Trinh et al., 2007; Le et al., 2010; Luu et al., 2012). Moreover, increases in rainfall and floods [SOURCE: Page_16]

due to climate change are expected to further exacerbate these problems by increasing the [SOURCE: Page_16]

transport of land-produced contaminants from land into the river. This, combined with the rapid [SOURCE: Page_16]

shifts in land use that this tropical region is experiencing and the increasing urbanization and [SOURCE: Page_16]

demand for clean water and sanitation mean that it is essential to understand the sources and [SOURCE: Page_16]

controlling factors of contaminants in this and other tropical aquatic systems. One such way of [SOURCE: Page_16]

obtaining an understanding of these factors is to use biogeochemical and hydrological modeling [SOURCE: Page_16]

coupled with in situ and laboratory based measurements. [SOURCE: Page_16]

1.1.1 Aims and scientific questions of the thesis [SOURCE: Page_16]

The polluted water of Red River poses a threat to the health and livelihoods of local people [SOURCE: Page_16]

15 [SOURCE: Page_16]

along the river in North Viet Nam. This thesis aims to provide information on some of the links [SOURCE: Page_17]

between faecal indicator bacteria (FIB), organic carbon (OC), land use and hydrology in the Red [SOURCE: Page_17]

River and Delta (North Viet Nam) using both experimental and modeling techniques. The work [SOURCE: Page_17]

carried out aimed to identify the mechanisms that determine the transfer of FIB and OM within [SOURCE: Page_17]

the hydrographic network, from the upper basin down to the sea, taking into account the influence [SOURCE: Page_17]

of human activities and of climate change in its watershed. Therefore the first objective of the [SOURCE: Page_17]

thesis was to investigate the seasonal variability of two FIB – Escherichia coli and Total [SOURCE: Page_17]

Coliforms, qnd of DOC and POC concentrations in the Red River and its delta by identifying the [SOURCE: Page_17]

environmental factors controlling the abundance of these microbes, determining their die-off rates [SOURCE: Page_17]

as well providing information on the carbon dynamics in this river system. The second goal was [SOURCE: Page_17]

to construct a dataset on TC, DOC, POC concentrations in domestic, industrial and agricultural [SOURCE: Page_17]

sources in the Red River drainage network. This data, along with that collected during the survey [SOURCE: Page_17]

work, were then used for implementing the existing SENEQUE/Riverstrahler model on the Red [SOURCE: Page_17]

River to calculate TC and OC dynamics in the drainage network. The model was then used to [SOURCE: Page_17]

estimate the influence of the point and non-point sources and environmental conditions on the [SOURCE: Page_17]

retention or elimination of TC, organic matter and suspended solids in the Red River drainage [SOURCE: Page_17]

system and to examine scenarios of what might occur in 2050. [SOURCE: Page_17]

1.1.2 Structure of the thesis [SOURCE: Page_17]

This PhD thesis contains 5 chapters of which 2 are data chapters written in form of [SOURCE: Page_17]

scientific articles. This, the first chapter provides a short, general introduction to the thesis. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Chapter 2 gives a detailed description of the study area with background information on the Red [SOURCE: Page_17]

River and its Delta and on the physical constraint data required for the modeling approach, such [SOURCE: Page_17]

as the geomorphology, geology and lithology and also hydro-meteorology (temperature, rainfall 16 [SOURCE: Page_17]

and hydrology). A detailed description of the Seneque/Riverstrahler model is also provided. The [SOURCE: Page_18]

first section of Chapter 3 presents an introduction to FIB and the specificities of developing [SOURCE: Page_18]

countries. The second section then presents the results of the work on the seasonal variability of [SOURCE: Page_18]

E.coli and Total Coliform numbers and die-off rates in the Red River basin, North Viet Nam. [SOURCE: Page_18]

This section is published in the journal “Scientific Reports”. The third section of this chapter [SOURCE: Page_18]

presents and discusses the results on modeling of FIB with the Seneque/Riverstrahler model. This [SOURCE: Page_18]

chapter is in submission “Environmental Monitoring and Assessment” (submitted the 12th [SOURCE: Page_18]

January 2016). The following chapter (Chapter 4) starts with a short introduction to OC in [SOURCE: Page_18]

aquatic environments; the second section then presents the work on OC degradation and the [SOURCE: Page_18]

modelling of OC and CO$_2$ fluxes in the Red River system. This article is in preparation for [SOURCE: Page_18]

submission to the journal “Biogeochemisty” in the summer of 2016. Chapter 5 is the final [SOURCE: Page_18]

chapter of this thesis and provides some general conclusions and perspectives on this work on [SOURCE: Page_18]

FIB and organic carbon in the Red River system. A complete list of references and three [SOURCE: Page_18]

appendices are also given in Chapters 6 and 7, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_18]

2.1 Study site [SOURCE: Page_21]

2.1.1 Water resources in Viet [SOURCE: Page_21]

Nam [SOURCE: Page_21]

Viet Nam is located in South East [SOURCE: Page_21]

Asia. It is bordered to the North [SOURCE: Page_21]

by China, to the west by Laos [SOURCE: Page_21]

and Cambodia and to the east by [SOURCE: Page_21]

the Eastern Sea (Fig. 2.1). The [SOURCE: Page_21]

country has one of the highest [SOURCE: Page_21]

population densities in the region [SOURCE: Page_21]

(273 people km-2). It ranks 3rd in [SOURCE: Page_21]

South East Asia, just after the [SOURCE: Page_21]

Philippines with 307 people km-2 [SOURCE: Page_21]

and Singapore with 7,486 people [SOURCE: Page_21]

km-2. Moreover, Viet Nam’s [SOURCE: Page_21]

population is continuing to grow Figure 2.1: Map of Viet Nam. The major cities and islands rapidly and is estimated to reach are noted. From the Maps of the World website. 126 million by 2040. Given [SOURCE: Page_21]

population growth, it can be [SOURCE: Page_21]

anticipated that the environment in Viet Nam will be subject to increasingly intense pressures and [SOURCE: Page_21]

that conservation of the environment and the services it provides will be increasingly difficult. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Viet Nam has a dense network of rivers, 2,360 rivers of more than 10 km long with several [SOURCE: Page_21]

20 [SOURCE: Page_21]

much longer ones such as the Red and Mekong Rivers. This network includes many trans- [SOURCE: Page_22]

national rivers that have their source in other countries (Table 2.1). Indeed, around two thirds of [SOURCE: Page_22]

Viet Nam’s water resources originate from outside the country, making Viet Nam dependant on [SOURCE: Page_22]

water resource decisions made in upstream countries. Surface water in Viet Nam comes from a [SOURCE: Page_22]

total catchment area of 1,167,000 km^2 and the surface water potential is estimated at 835 billion [SOURCE: Page_22]

m^3 per year with the largest proportion in the Mekong delta region in the south of the country, [SOURCE: Page_22]

followed by the Red River (Sông Hồng in Viet Namese) delta region in the North (Fig. 2.2). [SOURCE: Page_22]

In Viet Nam extensive data on surface water quality is lacking. However, the information [SOURCE: Page_22]

available reveals rising biological and chemical pollution levels in downstream sections of the [SOURCE: Page_22]

major rivers (Berg et al., 2007; Trinh et al., 2007; Le et al., 2010; Luu et al., 2012; Navarro et al., [SOURCE: Page_22]

2012; Ziegler et al., 2013; Ozaki et al., 2014). The upstream water quality of most rivers remains [SOURCE: Page_22]

good, while downstream pollution mainly from urban areas (human and urban waste) and [SOURCE: Page_22]

industries affects the water quality (Berg et al., 2007; Navarro et al., 2012; Ozaki et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_22]

The rapid economic and demographic growth that Viet Nam is experiencing is increasing the [SOURCE: Page_22]

demand for clean water as well as increasing the amount of wastewater that needs to be treated. [SOURCE: Page_22]

Indeed, in the context of global change and economic development, it is obvious that any socio- [SOURCE: Page_22]

economic development is closely linked to the need for water of an adequate quality. [SOURCE: Page_22]

2.1.2 Red River Basin [SOURCE: Page_24]

The Red River is a transboundary river basin that flows through Viet Nam, China and Laos. [SOURCE: Page_24]

The total basin area is around 156,000 km^2 of which around 55 % are in Viet Nam (Table 2.1). A [SOURCE: Page_24]

small part is located in Laos (1100km^2, or 0.65%) and with the remaining 44% located in China. [SOURCE: Page_24]

The basin is delimited between latitudes 20°23 'to 25°30' North and longitudes 100°00 to 107°10’ [SOURCE: Page_24]

East. To the north the basin borders with the Yangtze River basin, to the East the Thai Binh basin, [SOURCE: Page_24]

to the west with the Mekong River basin and the Ma River, and to the south with the Gulf of [SOURCE: Page_24]

Tonkin. The length of the Red River in Viet Nam is about 328 km making it the second largest [SOURCE: Page_24]

river (after the Mekong River) in the country. [SOURCE: Page_24]

2.1.2.1 Topography [SOURCE: Page_25]

The topography of Red River basin slopes from northwest to southeast. Mountainous [SOURCE: Page_25]

terrain to the East and North dominates the upper catchment area and tends to decrease in a [SOURCE: Page_25]

northwest – southeast direction with an average altitude of 1,090 m. The mountainous region on [SOURCE: Page_25]

the border between Viet Nam and Laos has many mountains above 1,800m such as Pu Si Lung [SOURCE: Page_25]

(3,076m), Pu Den Dinh (1,886m), Pu San Sao (1,877m). This range also separates the watersheds [SOURCE: Page_25]

of the Red River and Mekong River systems. Within the Red River basin, Hoang Lien Son [SOURCE: Page_25]

mountains divide the Da and Thao Rivers, two of the tributaries of the Red River. It is in this [SOURCE: Page_25]

range that the Fansipan peak (3,143m), the highest mountain in Viet Nam, is found. The Tay Con [SOURCE: Page_25]

Linh mountain that peaks at 2,419m divides the Thao and the Lo Rivers, the second and third [SOURCE: Page_25]

tributaries of the Red River. [SOURCE: Page_25]

The average altitude of the river basin is high. The slopes vary between 6 and 8.5 degrees [SOURCE: Page_25]

but can be quite steep such as in the Ngoi Thia (23 degrees) or Suoi Sap (25 degrees) streams and [SOURCE: Page_25]

the Thao, Da and Lo River basins have an average altitude of 547m, 965m, and 884m, [SOURCE: Page_25]

respectively (Le, K.L., 2009). The Lo River has the highest slope (1.8 mkm-1), then the Da River [SOURCE: Page_25]

with 1.5 mkm-1, with the Thao River having the lowest slope (1.2 mkm-1). [SOURCE: Page_25]

2.1.2.2 Climate [SOURCE: Page_25]

The Red River Basin is influenced by the Asian tropical monsoon. The North East monsoon [SOURCE: Page_25]

occurs from November to April bringing cooler, dryer weather. The South West monsoon occurs [SOURCE: Page_25]

from May to October and weather during this period is warmer and much more humid. Wind [SOURCE: Page_25]

direction also generally depends on the orientation of the valley. It can vary from mainly from the [SOURCE: Page_25]

west or northwest during the summer in the Da river basin to south-southeast in the Lo River [SOURCE: Page_25]

24 [SOURCE: Page_25]

Basin. The average wind speed about 1-1.5ms-1 but these values can reach 40ms-1 during storm [SOURCE: Page_26]

and cyclone events. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Figure 2.3a: Monthly air [SOURCE: Page_26]

temperature in 2013 for a [SOURCE: Page_26]

selection of cities in the [SOURCE: Page_26]

Red River basin. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Figure 2.3b: Monthly precipitation for the same stations in 2013. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Figure    2.3c:    Monthly [SOURCE: Page_26]

relative humidity (%) for [SOURCE: Page_26]

the same stations in 2013. [SOURCE: Page_26]

25 [SOURCE: Page_26]

Temperature tends to increase gradually from upstream to downstream (Figure 2.3a). [SOURCE: Page_27]

Highest temperatures usually occur in May with values of up 37 – 41°C observed in Son La. [SOURCE: Page_27]

Lowest temperatures usually occur in from October to January throughout the basin. Minimums [SOURCE: Page_27]

of – 0.2°C have been observed in Son La and occasionally snow can fall in the city of Sa Pa in the [SOURCE: Page_27]

mountainous province of Lao Cai. [SOURCE: Page_27]

The annual radiation in the Red River Basin varies between 100 - 200 Kcal cm-2 yr-1 [SOURCE: Page_27]

(average 60 to 80 Kcal cm-2 yr-1). It is lowest in January and February when total radiation is 5 - 8 [SOURCE: Page_27]

Kcal cm-2month-1 and highest in July. In the summer, the radiation balance is relatively uniform [SOURCE: Page_27]

across the basin. In winter, the difference is higher with the radiation levels varying with altitude. [SOURCE: Page_27]

This means that annual Hanoi (5m AMSL) has 72.5 Kcal cm-2 yr-1 but in Sapa (1570m AMSL) [SOURCE: Page_27]

the radiation balance was only 44.7 Kcal cm-2 yr-1. [SOURCE: Page_27]

The monsoonal climate means that two distinct seasons are found. The rainy season usually [SOURCE: Page_27]

lasts 5 months from June to October. Overall, rainfall is high but unequally distributed and varies [SOURCE: Page_27]

between 1,200 – 2,000 mm, with an average of 1,800 mm yr-1 (Le, 2009). The distribution of [SOURCE: Page_27]

rainfall in the basin depends heavily on the topography (Fig. 2.2b). For example, Bac Quang, [SOURCE: Page_27]

located in middle of the Lo river basin, has rainfall of up to 5,499mm yr-1. However, the cities [SOURCE: Page_27]

located behind the mountains such as Yen Chau, Son La, Nghia Lo have much lower rainfall [SOURCE: Page_27]

(1,200mm to 1,600mm yr-1). In the plains, average annual rainfall varies from 1,400mm to [SOURCE: Page_27]

2,000mm. [SOURCE: Page_27]

The average relative humidity in the basin is high and values from 80% - 90% are common [SOURCE: Page_27]

(Fig. 2.3c). The first maximum occurs in February - March due to drizzly weather in late winter. [SOURCE: Page_27]

The second maximum occurs around July - August when temperatures and rainfall are highest. [SOURCE: Page_27]

The driest periods occur in May - June and around October - November period corresponding to [SOURCE: Page_27]

the beginning and the end of the rainy season. [SOURCE: Page_27]

26 [SOURCE: Page_27]

The Red River basin average annual evaporation varied between 730 - 980 mm yr-1 in Thai [SOURCE: Page_28]

Nguyen, 560 – 1,050 mm yr-1 in the Midlands, and 700 - 990 mm yr-1 in the Plains. Total average [SOURCE: Page_28]

evaporation, determined over the period 1958 – 2006, at Son La is 932,8mm and 803,4mm at [SOURCE: Page_28]

Thac Ba (Tran, 2007; Vu, 2009 ). [SOURCE: Page_28]

2.1.2.3 Hydrology [SOURCE: Page_28]

Red River system has three major tributaries: Da, Thao and Lo rivers. All three of these [SOURCE: Page_28]

rivers originate from Yunnan (China) and then flow into Viet Nam. The Thao River (named the [SOURCE: Page_28]

Nguyen River in China) originates in the Dali Lake at an altitude of 2,000 m at Wei Son in [SOURCE: Page_28]

Yunnan Province, China. It then flows in a Northwest to Southeast direction and enters Viet Nam [SOURCE: Page_28]

in the Bat Xat district, Lao Cai province. It then receives water from the Da River at Trung Ha [SOURCE: Page_28]

and Lo River at Viet Tri before flowing into the Red River delta. The Thao River is considered [SOURCE: Page_28]

mainstream of the river and the part of the river from Viet Tri to Ba Lat is known as the Red [SOURCE: Page_28]

River. [SOURCE: Page_28]

The Red River delta has a network of interlacing canals and arroyos. It has several [SOURCE: Page_28]

distributaries including the Duong and Luoc Rivers that flow into the Thai Binh River and the Tra [SOURCE: Page_28]

Ly, Dao and Ninh Co Rivers. The Red River flows in the Gulf of Tonkin at Ba Lat, as well as [SOURCE: Page_28]

through the Tra Ly, Lach Giang and Day Rivers. [SOURCE: Page_28]

The Da River, known as the Ly Tien River in China, originates in the high mountains of [SOURCE: Page_28]

Yunnan province and flows in a Northwest to Southeasterly direction before entering Viet Nam at [SOURCE: Page_28]

Ka Long Commune in Muong Te district, Lai Chau province. It then flows through Dien Bien, [SOURCE: Page_28]

Son La and Hoa Binh provinces before joining with the Thao River at Trung Ha. The Da River is [SOURCE: Page_28]

1,010 km long, has a catchment area of 52,900km^2 of which 570 km and 26,800km^2 are in Viet [SOURCE: Page_28]

Nam. [SOURCE: Page_28]

The Lo River also has its source in the high mountains of over 2,000 maltitude in the 27 [SOURCE: Page_28]

Southwest Yunnan province, China. In China, the Lo River is known as the Ban Long River. It [SOURCE: Page_29]

flows in a Northwest to Southeasterly direction before entering Viet Nam in Vi Xuyen District, [SOURCE: Page_29]

Ha Giang province before flowing through Tuyen Quang, Phu Tho, Vinh Phuc provinces and [SOURCE: Page_29]

emptying in the Thao River at Viet Tri. The mainstream of the Lo River is 470 km long and the [SOURCE: Page_29]

total basin area is 39,000 km^2, of which 275 km and 22,600 km^2 are in Viet Nam. [SOURCE: Page_29]

The average total flow of the Red River system is about 127 km^3, which of 48.7 km^3 [SOURCE: Page_29]

(38.3%) enters from China and Laos, 55.1 km^3 (43.4%) from the Da River, 25.6 km^3 (20.2%) [SOURCE: Page_29]

from the Thao River and 33.3 km^3 (26.2%) from the Lo River (Le et al., 2007). The maximum [SOURCE: Page_29]

and minimum flows for some selected gauging stations are given in Table 2.2. Due to [SOURCE: Page_29]

distributaries in the delta, discharge at the Hanoi station is 40% lower than that at Son Tay, which [SOURCE: Page_29]

is located immediately downstream from the confluence of the three main sub-basins (Le et al., [SOURCE: Page_29]

2010). [SOURCE: Page_29]

River discharge also varies seasonally as a consequence of seasonal differences in rainfall. [SOURCE: Page_29]

The annual flood season in the middle and upper rivers often begins in May – June and ends in [SOURCE: Page_29]

September - October. Downstream flooding occurs from June to October. Some rivers in the [SOURCE: Page_29]

highlands of Son La, Moc Chau (Da River Basin) the flood season extends from July to October. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Flood flows represent around 70-80% of the annual flow and flow in June to August or July to [SOURCE: Page_29]

September are consistently high, accounting for about 50-65% of the annual flow. July and [SOURCE: Page_29]

August are generally the months with the highest average monthly flow, and these months alone [SOURCE: Page_29]

account for 15-30% of annual flow. During the dry season flow accounts for 20-30% of annual [SOURCE: Page_29]

flow. The lowest discharge occurs between January and April when flow accounts for less than [SOURCE: Page_29]

10% of the annual flow. [SOURCE: Page_29]

28 [SOURCE: Page_29]

2.1.2.4 Demography [SOURCE: Page_30]

Administratively, the Red River basin covers 25 provinces with a population of 32 million [SOURCE: Page_30]

people (estimates for 2013) including the capital city of Hanoi and large port city of Hai Phong. [SOURCE: Page_30]

The Red River basin has the largest population density in Viet Nam (Table 2.3). The delta [SOURCE: Page_30]

provinces are most densely populated with the major cities of Hanoi (2,087 people km-2); [SOURCE: Page_30]

BacNinh (1,354 people km-2); Hai Phong (1,260 people km-2) and Hung Yen (1,244 people km-2; [SOURCE: Page_30]

data 2013)(GSO, 2013). In contrast, the mountainous provinces are less densely populated e.g. [SOURCE: Page_30]

YenBai 112 people km-2; Hoa Binh 175 people km-2. At present, about half of the inhabitants in [SOURCE: Page_30]

the Red River basin live in rural areas with the rest living in cities, towns and townships. [SOURCE: Page_30]

However, the process of urbanization is accelerating as is the rural exodus and the population [SOURCE: Page_30]

density in the urban areas is expected to rapidly increase. [SOURCE: Page_30]

29 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Generally, the educational and health conditions in the Red River basin are low, especially [SOURCE: Page_31]

in the mountainous provinces such as Lao Cai, Yen Bai, Bac Kan, Bac Giang. Health related [SOURCE: Page_31]

infrastructure is lacking and access to adequate sanitation is limited in these provinces. The delta [SOURCE: Page_31]

provinces such as Vinh Phuc, Bac Ninh, Ha Nam, Ninh Binh have much higher economic growth [SOURCE: Page_31]

and the health and education conditions are considerably better. This is particularly true in the Ha [SOURCE: Page_31]

Noi metropolitan area which is the cultural center of the country. The midland plains of the Red [SOURCE: Page_31]

River, where the capital city of Hanoi is located, also house the scientific, political and [SOURCE: Page_31]

administrative services of the country. However, sanitary facilities such as wastewater treatment [SOURCE: Page_31]

and garbage collection and treatment are still very low even in urban areas (e.g. Ha Noi) (Fig [SOURCE: Page_31]

2.4). 30 [SOURCE: Page_31]

Figure 2.4: Scheme of wastewater routing in Hanoi city [SOURCE: Page_32]

In the center of Hanoi City, the drainage system is a combined system without [SOURCE: Page_32]

separation of runoff, domestic and industrial wastewater. In 2013, total wastewater discharged [SOURCE: Page_32]

in this city averaged 794,466 m^3 per day (Huan et al., 2014). The wastewater treatment plant at [SOURCE: Page_32]

the Yen So Park receives the wastewater flows from the Kim Nguu and Set Rivers and from Yen [SOURCE: Page_32]

So Park. This plant is designed to treat a maximum of 200,000 m^3 wastewater per day, including [SOURCE: Page_32]

125,000 m^3 day-1 from the Kim Nguu river, 65,000 m^3 day-1 from the Set river, and an additional [SOURCE: Page_32]

of 10,000 m^3 day-1 from sewer systems in the city (Figure 2.4). These rivers are heavily polluted [SOURCE: Page_32]

by wastewater discharged directly from Hanoi City. [SOURCE: Page_32]

31 [SOURCE: Page_32]

2.1.2.5 Economy and land use [SOURCE: Page_33]

The Red River Delta is one of eight economic regions formed within the Worlds’ major [SOURCE: Page_33]

river basins. It is characterized by rapid population growth, urbanization and industrialization, [SOURCE: Page_33]

intensive agriculture all of which have negatively affected water quality. Indeed, the Red River [SOURCE: Page_33]

Delta has been identified as one of the regions that will be most severely affected by climate [SOURCE: Page_33]

change and human activities in the future (Chaudhry and Greet, 2008; UNCCD, 2008). The Red [SOURCE: Page_33]

River Delta, along with the Mekong River Delta is a key economic and agricultural region in Viet [SOURCE: Page_33]

Nam. [SOURCE: Page_33]

The economy of the region is based on industry, services, agriculture, forestry and fisheries. [SOURCE: Page_33]

With 22.8% of the national population in 2011, this region contributed 676.9 billion Viet Nam [SOURCE: Page_33]

Dong (25 billion USD); accounting for 28.4% of the GDP. [SOURCE: Page_33]

The most significant industrial zones are located in Hanoi, Vinh Phuc, Bac Ninh, Hung [SOURCE: Page_33]

Yen, Hai Duong and Hai Phong provinces and the main sea ports are found Hai Phong and [SOURCE: Page_33]

Quang Ninh. Industry is mainly metallurgy, chemicals, construction materials along with food [SOURCE: Page_33]

processing and consumer goods production. Agricultural production is strongly based on irrigated [SOURCE: Page_33]

and non irrigated crops and aquaculture. About 760,000ha are used for crop cultivation (mainly [SOURCE: Page_33]

rice production) and for planted forests and about 120,000 ha are used for aquaculture. The [SOURCE: Page_33]

production of hydroelectricity is important in the larger reservoirs (Hoa Binh, Thac Ba, Son La, [SOURCE: Page_33]

Tuyen Quang). [SOURCE: Page_33]

32 [SOURCE: Page_33]

Table 2.4: Structure of economic sector for Viet Nam and the Red River delta provinces. Values are given as a percentage of the gross domestic production (GDP, USD). [SOURCE: Page_34]

Relative percentage of each activity (%) [SOURCE: Page_34]

Tourism is also an important economic activity in several of the provinces (Hanoi, Tam [SOURCE: Page_34]

Coc, Bich Dong, Trang An – Ninh Binh, Ha Long – Quang Ninh). However, the economic [SOURCE: Page_34]

structure of the sector is shifting as a function of the increase in the proportion of industry and [SOURCE: Page_34]

construction at the cost of agriculture, forestry and fisheries. [SOURCE: Page_34]

Around 15% of the countries rice production occurs in the Red River Delta (Rutten et al., [SOURCE: Page_34]

2014). However, recently large areas of rice paddies have disappeared as a consequence of the [SOURCE: Page_34]

construction of housing and factories. Indeed, land use patterns in Viet Nam are expected to [SOURCE: Page_34]

change dramatically in the future as a consequence of several global and local processes that [SOURCE: Page_34]

interact at various scales and domains (Rutten et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_34]

33 [SOURCE: Page_34]

Along with climate change, other key drivers affecting land use in Viet Nam are [SOURCE: Page_35]

technological change, population growth and international trade. Economic development and [SOURCE: Page_35]

structural change will lead to considerable changes in land use with expansion of planted forests [SOURCE: Page_35]

and urbanization at the expense of rice paddies, mangroves and other non‐production forests, and [SOURCE: Page_35]

shrub lands. This is directly related to the specific development trajectory of Hanoi and the [SOURCE: Page_35]

surrounding industrial areas. Between 1999 and 2009, the Red River Delta has witnessed a very [SOURCE: Page_35]

high pace of industrial activity that led to an expansion of urban land throughout the region. The [SOURCE: Page_35]

new industrial areas are predominantly located in suburban areas at a distance of about 70-140 [SOURCE: Page_35]

km from Hanoi. [SOURCE: Page_35]

2.2 Methods [SOURCE: Page_36]

2.2.1 Sampling strategy and laboratory analysis [SOURCE: Page_36]

2.2.1.1 Experimental work [SOURCE: Page_36]

From January 2013 to December 2014 monthly field surveys took place to collect water [SOURCE: Page_36]

samples for a series of water quality measurements along the Red River. Ten stations (from Yen [SOURCE: Page_36]

Bai- upstream of the Red River- to Ba Lat - downstream of Red River) were chosen. The main [SOURCE: Page_36]

purpose of these surveys was to determine both seasonal (dry and rainy season) and year-to-year [SOURCE: Page_36]

variations of water quality on the Red River. The ten selected stations were: [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Yen Bai that is representive of water quality upstream of the Red River after receiving [SOURCE: Page_36]

water from China. [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Hoa Binh, characterizing the water quality of the Da River before receiving water from [SOURCE: Page_36]

the Red River. [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Vu Quang, characterizing the water quality of the Lo River before receiving water from [SOURCE: Page_36]

the Red River. [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Son Tay, located just after the confluence of the Da and Lo Rivers to the Red River, [SOURCE: Page_36]

represents water quality of the Red River after receiving water from the Da and Lo River. [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Hanoi station, representative for water quality at mid river. [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Gian Khau, characterizing the water quality of the Day River after receiving water from [SOURCE: Page_36]

the Red River. [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Quyet Chien, representative for water quality of the Tra Ly River before discharging of [SOURCE: Page_36]

the Red River. [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Nam Dinh, representative for water quality of the Dao River after receiving water from [SOURCE: Page_36]

the Red River. 35 [SOURCE: Page_36]

- Truc Phương, representative for water quality of the Ninh Co River after receiving water [SOURCE: Page_37]

from the Red River. [SOURCE: Page_37]

- Ba Lat, illustrating the water quality of the Red River downstream before discharging to [SOURCE: Page_37]

the Sea. [SOURCE: Page_37]

2.2.1.2 In-situ measurements [SOURCE: Page_37]

During the monthly sampling campaigns, surface samples were collected in a can of 1.5 [SOURCE: Page_37]

liter (30 cm below the surface of the river) and no preservative was added (Fig. 2.5). The water [SOURCE: Page_37]

samples were kept at 4°C to 10°C in an icebox before treatment, during transportation to the [SOURCE: Page_37]

laboratory. [SOURCE: Page_37]

Figure 2.5: Sampling river water on the Red River. [SOURCE: Page_37]

Other physical-chemical parameters were measured directly in river water as: temperature (°C), pH, conductivity (μS cm-1), salinity (%0), turbidity (NTU), TDS (total dissolved solids – 36 [SOURCE: Page_37]

mgl-1), TSS (total suspended solids – mgl-1) and dissolved oxygen (DO, mgO$_2$ l-1) were measured using WQC-22A (TOA, Japan) and Hach (USA) probes. [SOURCE: Page_38]

2.2.1.3 Laboratory treatment [SOURCE: Page_38]

Upon return to the laboratory, all samples were treated immediately to avoid any changes [SOURCE: Page_38]

during storage. The filtration was realized with a Gelman Science filter (Pall) equipped with a [SOURCE: Page_38]

manual pump. Samples were filtered through: [SOURCE: Page_38]

- Whatman GF/F paper-filter (glass micro-fiber filters 0.7μm) previously burned at 500 ° C [SOURCE: Page_38]

(4 hours) for dissolved nutrient analyses as nitrogen (nitrite, nitrate and ammonia), phosphorus [SOURCE: Page_38]

(phosphate), and for DOC. For the determination of SS and particulate organic carbon POC on [SOURCE: Page_38]

the filter, GF/F filter-papers were pre-weighted. [SOURCE: Page_38]

After treatment, all samples were contained in disposable sterile polyethylene flasks except [SOURCE: Page_38]

for DOC that was stored in brown glass bottles. The samples were stored frozen. Samples were [SOURCE: Page_38]

prepared in duplicate for analysis in Laboratory of Environment (Institute of Natural Products [SOURCE: Page_38]

Chemistry – INPC, Hanoi). [SOURCE: Page_38]

2.2.1.4 Laboratory analysis [SOURCE: Page_38]

Suspended solid (SS) values were determined as the weight of material retained on the [SOURCE: Page_38]

Whatman GF/F filters per volume unit after drying the filter for 2 h at 120°C. POC was [SOURCE: Page_38]

determined on the same burned pre-weighed filters, as was used for the TSS determination. DOC [SOURCE: Page_38]

concentration was measured by a Shimadzu TOC-VE analyzer. Nutrients (N, P, Si) were [SOURCE: Page_38]

spectrophotometrically determined on a Drell 2800 (HACH, USA) in the laboratory according to [SOURCE: Page_38]

APHA (2012) methods. [SOURCE: Page_38]

FIB numbers (free and attached) were measured by a direct count method using Petriﬁlm E. [SOURCE: Page_38]

coli/Coliform Count (EC) plate which contain Violet Red Bile (VRB) nutrients(APHA, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_38]

37 [SOURCE: Page_38]

2.2.2 Seneque/Riverstrahler model [SOURCE: Page_39]

In order to integrate the measurements obtained on the spatial and temporal distribution of [SOURCE: Page_39]

both FIB and organic carbon concentration into a general vision of the dynamics of these [SOURCE: Page_39]

components within the entire river system, we have made use of an existing modelling tool, [SOURCE: Page_39]

already adapted to the Red River basin (Le et al., 2010; Le et al., 2015), the [SOURCE: Page_39]

Seneque/Riverstrahler Model. We here present the principle of this model, as well as the [SOURCE: Page_39]

functioning of the software used to pilot the model. [SOURCE: Page_39]

2.2.3 Principles of the Riverstrahler model [SOURCE: Page_39]

The SENEQUE/Riverstrahler model (Billen et al., 1994; Billen and Garnier, 1999; Garnier [SOURCE: Page_39]

et al., 1999) is a biogeochemical model (RIVE) of in-stream processes imbedded within a GIS [SOURCE: Page_39]

interface (SENEQUE), providing a generic model of the biogeochemical functioning of whole [SOURCE: Page_39]

river systems (from 10 to > 100000 km^2), designed to calculate the seasonal and spatial variations [SOURCE: Page_39]

of water quality (Ruelland et al., 2007; Thieu et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_39]

The basic version of RIVE involves 29 variables describing the physicochemical and [SOURCE: Page_39]

ecological state of the system (Fig 2.6). [SOURCE: Page_39]

These include nutrient, oxygen, suspended matter, dissolved and particulate nonliving [SOURCE: Page_39]

organic carbon concentrations, and algal, bacterial and zooplankton biomasses. Most of the [SOURCE: Page_39]

processes that are important in the transformation, elimination and/or immobilization of nutrients [SOURCE: Page_39]

during their transfer within the network of rivers and streams are explicitly calculated, including [SOURCE: Page_39]

algal primary production, aerobic and anaerobic organic matter degradation by planktonic as well [SOURCE: Page_39]

as benthic bacteria with coupled oxidant consumption and nutrient remineralisation, nitrification [SOURCE: Page_39]

and denitrification, and phosphate reversible adsorption onto suspended matter and subsequent [SOURCE: Page_39]

38 [SOURCE: Page_39]

sedimentation. [SOURCE: Page_40]

Figure 2.6: A schematic representation of the RIVE model of biogeochemical processes in aquatic [SOURCE: Page_40]

systems. State variables include: DIA, diatoms; GRA, green algae; ZOO, zooplankton; BAC, [SOURCE: Page_40]

heterotrophic bacteria; HD$_1$,2,3, rapidly, slowly and non hydrolysable dissolved organic matter; [SOURCE: Page_40]

HP$_1$,2,3, rapidly, slowly and non hydrolysable particulate organic matter; OXY, oxygen; [SOURCE: Page_40]

NH$_4$,ammonium; NH4ads, ammonium adsorbed onto the sediment; NO$_3$, nitrate;NIT, nitrifying [SOURCE: Page_40]

bacteria; PO$_4$, ortho-phosphate; PIP, particulate inorganic phosphorus; BIP, benthic inorganic [SOURCE: Page_40]

phosphorus; Dsi, dissolvedsilica; BSi, biogenic detritic silica; BBSi, benthic biogenic silica;SS, [SOURCE: Page_40]

suspended sediments; SED, deposited sediments. (From Thouvenot-Korppoo et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_40]

The basic assumption behind the model is that basic biological and physico-chemical 39 [SOURCE: Page_40]

processes involved in the functioning of the river system are the same from headwaters to [SOURCE: Page_41]

downstream sectors, while the hydrological and morphological constraints controlling their [SOURCE: Page_41]

expression differ largely along an upstream–downstream gradient as do the constraints exerted by [SOURCE: Page_41]

the inputs of terrigenous material. [SOURCE: Page_41]

The model describes the drainage network of the river system as a combination of basins, [SOURCE: Page_41]

represented as a regular scheme of confluence of tributaries of increasing stream order, each [SOURCE: Page_41]

characterized by mean morphologic properties, connected to branches, represented with a higher [SOURCE: Page_41]

spatial resolution. The framework of the drainage network is built as a combination of three kinds [SOURCE: Page_41]

of objects (Fig. 2. 7): [SOURCE: Page_41]

- Upstream basins are idealized their complex drainage network structure as a regular [SOURCE: Page_41]

scheme of confluence of tributaries of increasing stream-order, its having the same mean [SOURCE: Page_41]

morphological characteristics. [SOURCE: Page_41]

- Branches are represented as major rivers, with a spatial resolution of 1 km of the detailed [SOURCE: Page_41]

and realistic geographical. [SOURCE: Page_41]

- Reservoirs are represented as mixed reactors, connected either to branches at a defined [SOURCE: Page_41]

position or to all stream-order rivers in a basin. [SOURCE: Page_41]

The hydrological constraints, i.e. the flow of water within each object, are deduced from [SOURCE: Page_41]

measured discharge data at a number of key stations in the river network and expressed in terms [SOURCE: Page_41]

of surface runoff and base flow for each elementary sub-basins, based on the recursive filter of [SOURCE: Page_41]

Eckardt (2005). The climatic constraints are set by the seasonal variations of temperature (by [SOURCE: Page_41]

streamorder) and light intensity. The other constraints consist of point sources (the discharge of [SOURCE: Page_41]

wastewater from human settlements) and diffuse sources (characterized by the concentration of [SOURCE: Page_41]

each variable associated with the superficial and base flow components of the discharge issued [SOURCE: Page_41]

from each and use classes of the watershed). The calculations of water quality in the drainage [SOURCE: Page_41]

40 [SOURCE: Page_41]

network are made at a ten days resolution. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Figure 2.7: The three kinds of objects taken into account in the representation of the drainage [SOURCE: Page_42]

network by the Riverstrahler model: basins, branches and reservoirs (Ruelland et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_42]

41 [SOURCE: Page_42]

The principles and data requirements of the Riverstrahler model are summarized in Fig 2.8. [SOURCE: Page_43]

Figure 2.8: Principles of the calculation of water quality by the Riverstrahler model [SOURCE: Page_43]

(Ruelland et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_43]

2.2.3.1 The SENEQUE interface [SOURCE: Page_43]

The GIS interface SENEQUE, allows the user to run the Riverstrahler model for a definite [SOURCE: Page_43]

project. It first allows the explicit delimitation the part of the basin concerned, and the degree of [SOURCE: Page_43]

resolution required in its representation in terms of objects (branches and basins) combination. It [SOURCE: Page_43]

then allows the testing of defined scenarios through the fixing of anthropogenic and hydrological [SOURCE: Page_43]

constraints. The GIS files used are under Arc/Info or ArcView format however all other files are [SOURCE: Page_43]

simple text files, conferring a large versatility in the dialog with other programs or applications. [SOURCE: Page_43]

The results of the calculations are provided as seasonal variations of discharge or concentration of [SOURCE: Page_43]

any variable at one station (either at the outlet of the tributary of a specified stream-order for a 42 [SOURCE: Page_43]

basin, or at a specified kilometric position for a river branch) or as the longitudinal variations of [SOURCE: Page_44]

discharge or concentration along a river branch at a specified time. A cartographic representation [SOURCE: Page_44]

of the variable (with an adjustable color code) over all basins and branches of the project at a [SOURCE: Page_44]

given time period can also be produced. Furthermore, the possibility exists to automatically [SOURCE: Page_44]

compare the calculation results with measured data when these are stored in the database and the [SOURCE: Page_44]

results from several scenarios of the same project can also be compared simultaneously (Fig 2.9). [SOURCE: Page_44]

Figure. 2.9: Schematic representation of the functionalities of the GIS interface of the [SOURCE: Page_44]

SENEQUE software (Ruelland et al., 2007) [SOURCE: Page_44]

2.2.3.2     Spatial structure of the Red River as used in this thesis [SOURCE: Page_44]

For the purpose of modelling FIB and organic carbon in the lower tributaries of the Red [SOURCE: Page_44]

River, where we have concentrated our measurements, the spatial resolution adopted for running [SOURCE: Page_44]

the Riverstrahler model is rather coarse, with a focus on the course of the 3 main branches of the 43 [SOURCE: Page_44]

river system (Da, Lo, Thao/Hong), and most of the remaining drainage network represented as [SOURCE: Page_45]

idealized basins (Fig 2.10). The two major dams located on the Da and in the Lo basins are taken [SOURCE: Page_45]

into account by considering the morphology (increased width and minimum depth) of the [SOURCE: Page_45]

corresponding stretches of river. In this representation, our sampling stations correspond to the [SOURCE: Page_45]

following kilometric point (pK) of the different branches as indicated in Table 2.6. [SOURCE: Page_45]

Figure 2.10: ‘Decoupage’ of the Red River drainage network as used for the modeling runs in this [SOURCE: Page_45]

thesis. [SOURCE: Page_45]

44 [SOURCE: Page_45]

3 Faecal indicator bacteria [SOURCE: Page_47]

46 [SOURCE: Page_47]

3.1 Faecal indicator bacteria [SOURCE: Page_48]

3.1.1 Introduction and definition [SOURCE: Page_48]

Microbiological contamination of water supplies is a globally important problem and poor [SOURCE: Page_48]

water quality is a major brake on development. The World Health Organization (WHO) estimates [SOURCE: Page_48]

that almost 2 million deaths annually are due to the consumption of contaminated water (WHO, [SOURCE: Page_48]

2012). River water subject to wastewater contamination is often used for washing of clothes and [SOURCE: Page_48]

food utensils and for bathing and even cooking (Bain et al., 2014). This is true for urban and peri- [SOURCE: Page_48]

urban areas where population densities are high (Ashbolt, 2004; Bain et al., 2014) as well as in [SOURCE: Page_48]

rural areas where water supplies are often informal and therefore unregulated. In Asia over 40% [SOURCE: Page_48]

of rural drinking water sources are contaminated as compared to only 12% in urban areas. Access [SOURCE: Page_48]

to clean water is therefore a problem faced by both urban and rural populations in developing [SOURCE: Page_48]

countries. [SOURCE: Page_48]

Considering the known risks associated with the consumption of sewages contaminated [SOURCE: Page_48]

water, it is critical to identify the factors that control the persistence and dissemination of these [SOURCE: Page_48]

microbial pathogens. By increasing the knowledge based on the dynamics of the water borne [SOURCE: Page_48]

pathogens in tropical ecosystems we will be able to reduce the risks associated with the use of [SOURCE: Page_48]

untreated water. Moreover, understanding the links between human activities, natural process and [SOURCE: Page_48]

biogeochemical functioning and their ultimate impacts on human health are prerequisites for [SOURCE: Page_48]

efficient water resources management. However, the data required to adequately understand and [SOURCE: Page_48]

interpret these links is often missing or of poor quality in many developing countries (Rochelle- [SOURCE: Page_48]

Newall et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_48]

Developing countries are faced with a double problem. Often no adequate structures exist [SOURCE: Page_48]

for long term monitoring of water borne pathogens in the environment due to economic [SOURCE: Page_48]

47 [SOURCE: Page_48]

constraints and secondly, very little knowledge exists on the distribution of these microbes in [SOURCE: Page_49]

tropical environments. In order to detect these waterborne pathogens at limited cost, Faecal [SOURCE: Page_49]

Indicator Bacteria (FIB) are used as a proxy for pathogenic bacteria. The term FIB describes the [SOURCE: Page_49]

range of bacteria that inhabit the gastrointestinal tract of homeothermic animals and includes [SOURCE: Page_49]

Escherichia coli and the faecal coliforms, Enterococcus spp., all of which are found in faecal [SOURCE: Page_49]

material. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Fecal coliforms (FC) and E.coli (EC) have been used as the standard indicator of recent [SOURCE: Page_49]

fecal contamination in temperate regions (e.g. Servais et al., 2007; Ouattarra et al., 2013). In [SOURCE: Page_49]

tropical ecosystems they have also been used to monitor contamination levels despite reports that [SOURCE: Page_49]

free-living coliforms may be indigenous to some tropical waters and can’t be distinguished from [SOURCE: Page_49]

those from a fecal source (Carillo et al., 1985; Jiminez et al., 1989). Indeed, Rochelle-Newall et [SOURCE: Page_49]

al (2015) noted that our ability to culture FIB (eg, E.coli and faecal coliforms) and the ability of [SOURCE: Page_49]

these FIB to become naturalized in the environment are two of the most important factors for [SOURCE: Page_49]

deciding whether or not we can estimate correctly FIB and the pathogens for which they are [SOURCE: Page_49]

indicators. Ideally, methods that monitor faecal contamination without having to rely on culturing [SOURCE: Page_49]

techniques are required. The use of specific biomarkers for fecal contamination such as stannols [SOURCE: Page_49]

is one such option (Solecki et al., 2011; Jeanneau et al., 2012). However, while these methods [SOURCE: Page_49]

have the advantage that they are culture-independent and species specific (chicken, pig or [SOURCE: Page_49]

human); they have the disadvantage that they require considerable analytical technologies that are [SOURCE: Page_49]

often lacking in developing countries (Rochelle-Newall et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_49]

Today, the most commonly measured bacterial indicators are Total coliforms, Faecal [SOURCE: Page_49]

Coliforms, and Escherichia coli. Total coliforms are a group of bacteria that are widespread in [SOURCE: Page_49]

nature, which are found in the soil, in many environments water, in human feces or animal [SOURCE: Page_49]

manure, submerged wood and in other places outside the human body. Thus, the usefulness of [SOURCE: Page_49]

48 [SOURCE: Page_49]

total coliforms as an indicator of fecal contamination depends on the extent to which the bacteria [SOURCE: Page_50]

species found (Willden, 2006). Fecal Coliforms are a form of of total coliform bacteria, and as its [SOURCE: Page_50]

name implies, it originates from fecal matter. E. coli is a species of fecal coliform bacteria that is [SOURCE: Page_50]

specific to fecal material from humans and other warm-blooded animals. Leclerc et al. (2001) [SOURCE: Page_50]

proposed that the use of E. coli is the best indicator of the presence of pathogenic bacteria and [SOURCE: Page_50]

fecal contamination. In environmental waters, several studies have reported significant [SOURCE: Page_50]

correlations between indicators of fecal pollution. For example, Donze (2004) reported that there [SOURCE: Page_50]

was a significant positive relationship between total coliforms and E. coli (r = 0.59, N = 30, p < [SOURCE: Page_50]

0.001). Byamukama et al (2000), in a study from the Nakivubo channel, Uganda, that all [SOURCE: Page_50]

microbiological parameters (total, fecal coliforms and E. coli) were significantly correlated. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Wilkes et al (2009) also found, in a comparative study on the presence and concentration of [SOURCE: Page_50]

several pathogenic and indicator bacteria in the surface water of a Canadian river, that significant [SOURCE: Page_50]

correlations were found total coliforms and E. coli. It therefore seems that although it is [SOURCE: Page_50]

preferable to used E.coli as an indicator of recent fecal contamination, given the correlations [SOURCE: Page_50]

between the coliforms and E.coli, that in the absence of E.coli numbers, faecal and total coliform [SOURCE: Page_50]

numbers can be used to indicator the presence of possible faecal contamination. [SOURCE: Page_50]

49 [SOURCE: Page_50]

3.1.2 Primary sources of FIB [SOURCE: Page_51]

Figure. 3.1: Conceptual diagram of the factors influencing FIB in developing countries. From [SOURCE: Page_51]

Rochelle-Newall et al., 2015. [SOURCE: Page_51]

The microbiological quality of rivers is primarily controlled by human and animal activities [SOURCE: Page_51]

in the watershed. Humans, livestock and wild animals are all primary sources of faecal [SOURCE: Page_51]

contamination (Fig. 3.1) although human faecal waste has the highest risk of waterborne disease, [SOURCE: Page_51]

since the probability of human pathogens being present is highest. In urban areas, faecal [SOURCE: Page_51]

microorganisms are mainly brought to aquatic environments through the discharge of treated and [SOURCE: Page_51]

untreated domestic and industrial wastewaters (Servais et al., 2007b). Point sources (outfall of [SOURCE: Page_51]

50 [SOURCE: Page_51]

wastewater treatment or industrial plants and open sewer outlets) are often a major source of [SOURCE: Page_52]

pollution in urbanized and industrialized catchments. Servais et al. (2007b) showed that in a large [SOURCE: Page_52]

urbanized watershed (Seine, France), the input of fecal microorganisms from non-point sources is [SOURCE: Page_52]

much lower than the inputs from point sources. These authors also examined the links between [SOURCE: Page_52]

land use and FIB concentrations. They looked at surface runoff and leaching under three types of [SOURCE: Page_52]

land use: forest areas, cultivated areas and grassland areas and found that small streams draining [SOURCE: Page_52]

pastures were significantly more contaminated (around 1000 FC 100ml-1) than those draining [SOURCE: Page_52]

forests or cultivated areas (around 100 FC 100ml-1). [SOURCE: Page_52]

In temperate regions with industrial scale agriculture, the microbial loading potential from [SOURCE: Page_52]

point sources, such as storage facilities and feedlots, and from non-point sources, such as grazed [SOURCE: Page_52]

pastures and rangelands, can be substantial (Table 3.1). Muirhead et al. (2005) in an experimental [SOURCE: Page_52]

study of surface runoff from cowpats found E. coli concentrations of 1 x 106 MPN 100ml-1 and [SOURCE: Page_52]

other workers have reported concentrations of up to an order more. High numbers of E. coli from [SOURCE: Page_52]

animals and wildlife can reach surface waters in agricultural watersheds where direct excretion [SOURCE: Page_52]

and runoff of fecal material from manure can enter waterways (Crowther et al., 2002; Kloot, [SOURCE: Page_52]

2007; Vidon et al., 2008a). [SOURCE: Page_52]

In developing countries and particularly in rural areas, agriculture is less intensive, [SOURCE: Page_52]

wastewater treatment is often absent and non-point sources tend to predominate and the primary [SOURCE: Page_52]

source of FIB is faecal matter generated by domestic and wild animals. For example, Ribolzi et [SOURCE: Page_52]

al. (2011) and Causse et al. (2015), working in rural Laos, found that E. coli concentrations were [SOURCE: Page_52]

below 1 MPN 100ml-1 in the upper areas of the watershed indicating a very low background level [SOURCE: Page_52]

of contamination that was probably caused by wildlife. However, as the density of poultry and [SOURCE: Page_52]

humans settlements increased in the downstream areas, values of up 230 MPN 100ml-1 were [SOURCE: Page_52]

found. Other non-agricultural sources of microbial pollution in rural watersheds include failing [SOURCE: Page_52]

51 [SOURCE: Page_52]

septic systems and latrine overflows during periods of heavy rain, all of which can create health [SOURCE: Page_53]

problems in the downstream human populations. However, as a consequence of their dispersion, [SOURCE: Page_53]

these non-point sources of microbial pollution are inherently more difficult to identify [SOURCE: Page_53]

andcharacterize than point sources. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Table 3.1: Faecal coliforms and E. coli numbers in some primary sources. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Bacteria             No.Bacteria/    Source                           Reference 100ml Faecal coliform      107             Fresh cow pats                   Thelin and Gifford (1983) 5 10              30 days old cow pats             Kress    &     Gifford 104             100 days old cow pats            (1984) as cited in Muirhead      et    al. (2005) E. coli              107             From fresh cow pats              Muirhead      et    al. (2005) 3   7 10 - 10         Sheep and beef                   Collins et al. (2005) [SOURCE: Page_53]

3.1.3 Secondary sources of FIB [SOURCE: Page_53]

FIB and the water borne pathogens for which they are an indicator are particularly [SOURCE: Page_53]

susceptible to shifts in hydrology and water quality (Vidon et al., 2008b; Cho et al., 2010; Chu et [SOURCE: Page_53]

al., 2011). Stormwater discharges are a major cause of rapid deterioration in surface water [SOURCE: Page_53]

quality. Storm events increase turbidity, suspended solids, organic matter and faecal [SOURCE: Page_53]

contamination in rivers and streams, although the microbiological quality of stormwater varies [SOURCE: Page_53]

widely and reflects human activities in the watershed (Ribolzi et al., 2011; Causse et al., 2015; 52 [SOURCE: Page_53]

Ekklesia et al., 2015). Geldreich (1991) reported that stormwater in combined sewers can have [SOURCE: Page_54]

more than 10-fold higher thermotolerant coliform levels than in separate stormwater sewers. [SOURCE: Page_54]

In rural areas and in urban areas without adequate wastewater treatment or stormflow [SOURCE: Page_54]

management, one of the major pathways via which faecal contaminants enter waterways is via [SOURCE: Page_54]

overland flow. Overland flow occurs when rainfall is unable to infiltrate the soil surface and runs [SOURCE: Page_54]

over the ground, normally in rivulets. Overland flow is also the predominant means by which soil [SOURCE: Page_54]

particles and faecal contamination in soils are transported from land to surface waters. The [SOURCE: Page_54]

concentrations of FIB in overland flow are controlled by many factors such as rainfall duration [SOURCE: Page_54]

and intensity, manure application, faecal deposit age and type, adsorption to soil particles, etc. [SOURCE: Page_54]

(Blaustein et al., 2015; Rochelle-Newall et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_54]

Many authors have highlighted the low contribution of groundwater to FIB concentrations [SOURCE: Page_54]

(Jamieson et al., 2004). These low values are probably as a result of efficient soil filtering of [SOURCE: Page_54]

microorganisms in infiltrating water (Matthess et al., 1988), in contrast to overland flow [SOURCE: Page_54]

characterized by high FIB concentrations.The values of only 4 E.coli 100 ml-1 found in [SOURCE: Page_54]

groundwater of a village in Laosis far lower than the reported values of 230 000 E.coli 100 ml-1 [SOURCE: Page_54]

in overland flow during a storm downstream of a small stream (Ribolzi et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_54]

In stream sediments have also been identified as a reservoir for E. coli. Many studies [SOURCE: Page_54]

indicate that sediments harbor much higher populations of both Faecal coliforms (FC) and E. coli [SOURCE: Page_54]

than the overlying water (e.g. Rehmann and Soupir, 2009; Chu et al., 2011; Pachepsky and [SOURCE: Page_54]

Shelton, 2011). Mechanical disturbance of bottom sediments, as occurs during flood events can [SOURCE: Page_54]

cause increased E. coli concentrations in the overlying waters as a result of their resuspension [SOURCE: Page_54]

(Cho et al., 2010). Muirhead et al. (2004), during an artificial flood experiment, observed that E. [SOURCE: Page_54]

coli concentrations peaked ahead of the flow peak, consistent with the entrainment of FC into the [SOURCE: Page_54]

water column from underlying contaminated sediments by accelerating currents on the rising 53 [SOURCE: Page_54]

limb of the hydrograph. A two order of magnitude increase was observed during the event. E.coli [SOURCE: Page_55]

concentrations were correlated with turbidity over the flood event, however, when turbidity [SOURCE: Page_55]

returned to base levels between each flood, E. coli concentrations remained elevated. A similar [SOURCE: Page_55]

dynamic was observed by Ribolzi et al. (2016a),who conducted a detailed examination of E.coli [SOURCE: Page_55]

dynamics during a flood in an upland stream. By separating the groundwater and overland flow [SOURCE: Page_55]

components of the flood they were able to identify the contribution of sediments to the total [SOURCE: Page_55]

E.coli numbers in the stream. They showed that up to 75% of the E.coli in the stream were from [SOURCE: Page_55]

the sediments and not from soil runoff from the sloping lands above the stream. [SOURCE: Page_55]

3.1.4 Fate in the aquatic continuum [SOURCE: Page_55]

The fate of FIB in the environment is controlled by the bacteria strain characteristics, the [SOURCE: Page_55]

indigenous microbial community and by external environmental variables (Rochelle-Newall et al, [SOURCE: Page_55]

2015; Fig. 3.1). This latter group includes sunlight, nutrient and suspended solids concentrations, [SOURCE: Page_55]

sedimentation and resuspension rates, water temperature, pH, predation, and organic matter (OM) [SOURCE: Page_55]

concentrations. All of which influence the die-off rates and, potentially, the growth characteristics [SOURCE: Page_55]

of FIB in the non-host environment. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Although FIB are considered to be enteric bacteria and therefore adapted to a nutrient and [SOURCE: Page_55]

organic matter (OM) rich, low oxygen environment in their host there is some evidence that they [SOURCE: Page_55]

can persist in the ecosystem and particularly so in tropical soil environments (Byappanahalli and [SOURCE: Page_55]

Fujioka, 1998; Winfield and Groisman, 2003; Ishii et al., 2006; Ishii and Sadowsky, 2008). When [SOURCE: Page_55]

they are released from the host FIB are in an environment that is colder, more dilute and has [SOURCE: Page_55]

higher oxygen concentrations and much lower OM concentrations. The OM chemical quality is [SOURCE: Page_55]

probably also very different from that of the host. Fujioka and Byappanahalli (2001) have shown [SOURCE: Page_55]

that FIB have the capacity to degrade a series of carbon sources found in soils. 54 [SOURCE: Page_55]

Table 3.2: Summary of die-off rates for faecal bacteria in aquatic systems. [SOURCE: Page_56]

Bacteria            Die-off rate     Environment                                Reference (per day, d-1) E. coli             14.7–107         Hong Kong coastal waters, under (Chan                et     al., light-exposure                             2015) 0.85 –1.50       Hong    Kong      coastal   waters,   in darkness 1.3 – 5.1        Freshwater, in-situ Faecal coliform     0.0048           Manure ponds                               Panhorst et al E. coli             0.0078                                                      (2002) [SOURCE: Page_56]

Faecal coliform     0.43             Aquatic environments dark at 100C          Auer and Niehaus 0.81             Aquatic environments dark at 350C          (1993) 0.73             Natural aquatic       conditions in the dark Faecal coliform     4.24             Reservoir, exposed to sunlight             Dewedar and Bahgat (1995) [SOURCE: Page_56]

Bouvy et al (2008) working in Senegal found that it was substrate concentration rather than [SOURCE: Page_56]

temperature that controlled FIB persistence in a coastal system subject to high sewage inputs. [SOURCE: Page_56]

Garzio-Hadzick et al. (2010) observed that the survival of E.coli was higher in soils with high [SOURCE: Page_56]

OM contents and Topp et al (2003) found that survival was high in loamy soils and that this [SOURCE: Page_56]

survival increased with the addition of manure. This is often the situation in developing countries [SOURCE: Page_56]

where fresh manure is frequently used as an economical fertilizer option for both fields and in [SOURCE: Page_56]

aquaculture (e.g. Yajima and Kurokura, 2008). It is therefore probable adequate concentrations of [SOURCE: Page_56]

bioavailable carbon may contribute to FIB survival in tropical environments. [SOURCE: Page_56]

Light intensity has been identified as one of the most influential factors causing die-off of [SOURCE: Page_56]

coliforms in freshwater (Sinton et al., 2002; Chan et al., 2015). Other studies have also reported [SOURCE: Page_56]

55 [SOURCE: Page_56]
