that the inactivation of E.coli is more rapid in saline waters than in freshwaters (e.g. Troussellier [SOURCE: Page_57]

et al., 2004).E.coli survive longer in turbid conditions (Alkan et al., 1995) and the presence of [SOURCE: Page_57]

suspended solids in the water column has been shown to increase E.coli survival rates by limiting [SOURCE: Page_57]

the effects of sunlight (Milne et al., 1986). [SOURCE: Page_57]

E.coli survive longer in cold temperatures than in warm temperatures in the environment [SOURCE: Page_57]

(Medema et al., 1997). Increased mortality rates at the higher temperatures may be due to damage [SOURCE: Page_57]

to the bacterial cell components or due to increased predation (Sinton et al., 2002). However, it is [SOURCE: Page_57]

interesting to note that the effects of temperature have been reported to be less important in the [SOURCE: Page_57]

presence of light for enteric bacteria (Alkan et al., 1995), although why this might be is not clear. [SOURCE: Page_57]

An important question often addressed in the literature is related to the differences in [SOURCE: Page_57]

disappearance rates for different bacterial strains. For example, Menon et al. (2003) did not find a [SOURCE: Page_57]

significant difference in the rates of disappearance of the strains tested (E. coli, S. faecium, and S. [SOURCE: Page_57]

typhimurium). Sinton et al. (2002), on the other hand, reported a disappearance rate for E.coli that [SOURCE: Page_57]

is four times higher compared to Enteroccus spp.. The die-off rate constant for E. coli and faecal [SOURCE: Page_57]

streptococci in groundwater ranged from 0.16 to 0.36 day -1 and from 0.03 to 0.23 day -1, [SOURCE: Page_57]

respectively (Gerba and Britton, 1984). However, most of the published values have focused on [SOURCE: Page_57]

temperate systems and few have published die-off rates from tropical situations. [SOURCE: Page_57]

56 [SOURCE: Page_57]

3.2 Seasonal variability of faecal indicator bacteria numbers and die-off rates in the Red River basin, North Viet Nam (Article 1) [SOURCE: Page_58]

This chapter is published in the journal “Scientific Reports” as: [SOURCE: Page_58]

Nguyen TMH, Le TPQ, Billen G, Garnier J, Janeau J-L, Rochelle-Newall E Seasonal variability [SOURCE: Page_58]

of faecal indicator bacteria and die-off rates in the Red River, North Viet Nam. 2016. Scientific [SOURCE: Page_58]

Reports: 6, 21644; doi: 10.1038/srep^21644. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Author contributions: EJRN, QTPL, JG designed the experiments; HTMN, JLJ and QTPL [SOURCE: Page_58]

carried out the work; EJRN, HTMN, QTPL, JG interpreted the results and HTMN and EJRN [SOURCE: Page_58]

wrote the manuscript that was revised and improved by all the coauthors [SOURCE: Page_58]

57 [SOURCE: Page_58]

3.2.1 Abstract [SOURCE: Page_59]

The Red River is the second largest river in Viet Nam and constitutes the main water source for [SOURCE: Page_59]

a large percentage of the population of North Viet Nam. Here we present the results of an annual [SOURCE: Page_59]

survey of Escherichia coli (EC) and Total Coliforms (TC) in the Red River basin, North Viet [SOURCE: Page_59]

Nam. The objective of this work was to obtain information on faecal indicator bacteria (FIB) [SOURCE: Page_59]

numbers over an annual cycle and, secondly, to determine the die-off rates of these bacterial [SOURCE: Page_59]

indicators. Monthly observations at 10 stations from July 2013 - June 2014 showed that TC and [SOURCE: Page_59]

EC reached as high as 39100 cfu (colony forming units, CFU) 100 ml-1 and 15300 CFU 100 ml- 1 , respectively. We observed a significant seasonal difference for TC (p <0.05) with numbers [SOURCE: Page_59]

being higher during the wet season. In contrast, no significant seasonal difference was found for [SOURCE: Page_59]

EC. The FIB die-off rates ranged from 0.01 d-1 to a maximum of 1.13 d-1 for EC and from 0.17 [SOURCE: Page_59]

d-1 to 1.33 d-1 for TC. Die-off rates were significantly higher for free bacteria than for total (free [SOURCE: Page_59]

+ particle attached) bacteria, suggesting that particle attachment provided a certain level of [SOURCE: Page_59]

protection to FIB in this system. [SOURCE: Page_59]

58 [SOURCE: Page_59]

3.2.2 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_60]

Biological contamination of aquatic systems by water borne pathogens from untreated [SOURCE: Page_60]

wastewater and agricultural effluent is a globally important water quality problem (Ashbolt, [SOURCE: Page_60]

2004). However, it is particularly problematic in tropical regions where a large proportion of the [SOURCE: Page_60]

developing world is located. On a global scale, it is estimated that 88% of diarrheal diseases are [SOURCE: Page_60]

due to the use of unclean water sources, leading to the deaths of 1.8 million people annually, [SOURCE: Page_60]

most of whom are children in developing countries (WHO, 2012). Indeed, in many developing [SOURCE: Page_60]

countries, surface water (e.g. rivers and stagnant ponds) subject to wastewater contamination is [SOURCE: Page_60]

often used for domestic purposes as access to uncontaminated water is limited (Bain et al., [SOURCE: Page_60]

2014). Therefore, considering the high death rates as well as the large economic burden [SOURCE: Page_60]

associated with the construction and maintenance of water treatment plants, having an [SOURCE: Page_60]

understanding of the spatial distribution and temporal variability of the microbial pathogens [SOURCE: Page_60]

responsible for these diseases is essential. Furthermore, understanding the factors that control [SOURCE: Page_60]

their distribution is a prerequisite for reducing the human health risks associated with the use of [SOURCE: Page_60]

unclean water. This is particularly important in tropical areas where there is a paucity of data, [SOURCE: Page_60]

where population growth is high, and where populations are the most exposed to these [SOURCE: Page_60]

contaminants (Ashbolt, 2004; Bain et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_60]

Rivers are a major source of fresh water for irrigation, industry and domestic water [SOURCE: Page_60]

requirements. However, many tropical rivers have been adversely affected by human activities, [SOURCE: Page_60]

such as industrialization, urbanization and agricultural intensification (Broussard and Turner, [SOURCE: Page_60]

2009; Seitzinger et al., 2010). Although the chemical contamination of water bodies has been [SOURCE: Page_60]

documented in many tropical systems (Berg et al., 2007; Navarro et al., 2012), the extent of [SOURCE: Page_60]

biological contamination from untreated wastewater and animal husbandry is often unknown. 59 [SOURCE: Page_60]

This is despite the fact that detailed knowledge on the range and origin of microbial pollution is [SOURCE: Page_61]

required for watershed management in order to provide safe water for human demands. [SOURCE: Page_61]

The Red River is the second largest river in Viet Nam, after the Mekong River, and one of [SOURCE: Page_61]

the five largest rivers in East Asia (Vinh et al., 2014). Over 24 million inhabitants live inthe Red [SOURCE: Page_61]

River basin, including over 17 million people in its delta. This area is also characterized by [SOURCE: Page_61]

several large industrial zones and by a large number of craft villages that are considered as [SOURCE: Page_61]

hotspots of biological and chemical contamination (Mahanty et al., 2012). The Red River Delta [SOURCE: Page_61]

(RRD) is the second most important rice- producing area in Viet Nam and accounts for 20% of [SOURCE: Page_61]

the national production. It is also the main freshwater source for the surrounding areas as well as [SOURCE: Page_61]

being the major outlet for wastewater (Luu et al., 2010; Luu et al., 2012). According to the Viet [SOURCE: Page_61]

Nam Environment Administration Report 2012, the urban area of the RRD concentrates 24% of [SOURCE: Page_61]

the national production of domestic wastewater. It also receives the second largest proportion of [SOURCE: Page_61]

industrial wastewater in the country after that of the South East region around Ho Chi Minh [SOURCE: Page_61]

City. Despite the high proportions of wastewater that are released into the Red River on a daily [SOURCE: Page_61]

basis, little information exists in the published literature on microbial or faecal contamination [SOURCE: Page_61]

levels in this semi-tropical region. [SOURCE: Page_61]

Faecal indicator bacteria (FIB) are used to monitor faecal contamination levels and hence [SOURCE: Page_61]

the possibility of pathogens of faecal origin in soils and water in both tropical and temperate [SOURCE: Page_61]

systems (Ishii and Sadowsky, 2008; Pachepsky and Shelton, 2011). FIB is a generic term for a [SOURCE: Page_61]

range of bacteria that inhabit the gastrointestinal tract of homoeothermic animals. This group [SOURCE: Page_61]

includes Escherichia coli, Salmonella spp., Enterococcus spp., and the coliforms. We [SOURCE: Page_61]

hypothesized that FIB numbers would increase along the river length as a consequence of the [SOURCE: Page_61]

increasing industrialization and urbanization in the downstream sections. Here we present the [SOURCE: Page_61]

results of an annual survey of FIB at ten stations along the Red River, North Viet Nam. The 60 [SOURCE: Page_61]

objective of this work was to obtain information on FIB concentrations over one annual cycle [SOURCE: Page_62]

and to identify the environmental factors controlling FIB numbers and to determine their die-off [SOURCE: Page_62]

rates. [SOURCE: Page_62]

3.2.3 Materials and methods [SOURCE: Page_62]

3.2.3.1 Study site: [SOURCE: Page_62]

The Red River basin has an area of about 156 451 km^2 of which 51.2% is in Viet Nam, [SOURCE: Page_62]

47.9% in China and 0.9% in Laos (Le et al., 2007). The basin is subject to a semi-tropical [SOURCE: Page_62]

climate with two clear seasons. The wet season persists from May to October during which 80- [SOURCE: Page_62]

90% of the total annual rainfall of 1900 mm occurs (Xuan, 2010). The cooler, dry season [SOURCE: Page_62]

persists from November to April. Mean monthly temperatures are lowest in January, with June- [SOURCE: Page_62]

August being generally the hottest. In general, temperature is relatively uniform across the basin [SOURCE: Page_62]

and the mean relative humidity is greater than 80% (IMH, 1997-2004). Concomitant with the [SOURCE: Page_62]

highest rainfall, discharge and suspended load peak during August in the middle of the wet [SOURCE: Page_62]

season (Le et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_62]

Samples were collected monthly from July 2013 to June 2014at 10 stations (total of 120 [SOURCE: Page_62]

samples) in the Red River Basin. The sample sites are located on different river branches [SOURCE: Page_62]

(distributaries) of the Red River system and include, from upstream to downstream, Yen Bai [SOURCE: Page_62]

(Thao River), Hoa Binh (Da River), Vu Quang (Lo River), Gian Khau (Day river), Truc Phuong [SOURCE: Page_62]

(Ninh Co River), Quyet Chien (Tra Ly River), Nam Dinh (Dao River) and Son Tay, Ha Noi and [SOURCE: Page_62]

Ba Lat on the main axe of the Red River (Table 3.3). [SOURCE: Page_62]

61 [SOURCE: Page_62]

3.2.3.2 Sample collection [SOURCE: Page_63]

At each sample site, 1500 ml of river water was collected with a clean plastic bottle before [SOURCE: Page_63]

storage in a cooler and return to the laboratory for processing. The sample was used to measure [SOURCE: Page_63]

pH, conductivity, temperature, salinity, total suspended solids (TSS), total phosphorus (TP), [SOURCE: Page_63]

dissolved inorganic phosphate (PO$_4$), ammonia-nitrogen (NH$_3$-N) and free and attached FIB. [SOURCE: Page_63]

Die-off rates [SOURCE: Page_63]

At four stations, a second series of samples was collected in the same way for the [SOURCE: Page_63]

determination of FIB die-off rate over time. These stations were (1) Yen Bai, locatedin the [SOURCE: Page_63]

upstream main branch of the Red River known as the Thao River; (2) Ha Noi, after the [SOURCE: Page_63]

confluence of three major upstream tributaries of the Da, Thao and Lo rivers; (3) Gian Khau, a [SOURCE: Page_63]

peri-urban river system located in the Red River Delta and, (4) Truc Phuong, located in the [SOURCE: Page_63]

downstream Red River on the Ninh Co River. These four stations were chosen to give a good [SOURCE: Page_63]

representation of the land uses and population densities in the basin and to provide a good [SOURCE: Page_63]

geographical separation over the area. For each station, 750 ml of sample were incubated in [SOURCE: Page_63]

duplicate in glass bottles at in situ temperature and in the dark for five days. For the estimation [SOURCE: Page_63]

of die-off rates, samples were collected from the incubations every day during 5 days (T$_0$, T$_1$, [SOURCE: Page_63]

T$_2$, T$_3$, T$_4$, T$_5$) to determine the decrease in FIB numbers for both total and free bacteria using [SOURCE: Page_63]

the method described below. [SOURCE: Page_63]

62 [SOURCE: Page_63]

64 [SOURCE: Page_65]

3.2.3.3 Analytical methods [SOURCE: Page_66]

Temperature, pH, and total suspended solids (TSS) were measured using a water quality [SOURCE: Page_66]

probe WQC-22A (TOA, Japan) and conductivity (Cond) was determined using a conductivity [SOURCE: Page_66]

meter     (Hach,    USA)     immediately    upon    sampling.    Nutrients   (N,   P,   Si)   were [SOURCE: Page_66]

spectrophotometrically determined on a Drell 2800 (HACH, USA) in the laboratory according [SOURCE: Page_66]

to APHA (2012) methods. [SOURCE: Page_66]

FIB abundance (free and attached) was measured by a direct count method using 3M [SOURCE: Page_66]

Petrifilm™ E.coli/Coliform Count Plate (Petrifilm EC plate), which contain Violet Red Bile [SOURCE: Page_66]

(VRB) nutrients. E.coli (EC) produces beta-glucuronidase, which produces a blue precipitate [SOURCE: Page_66]

and Total coliform (TC) colonies growing on the Petrifilm EC plate produce acid and the [SOURCE: Page_66]

colonies are denoted by dark red points. This method has been validated by the APHA and is a [SOURCE: Page_66]

technique commonly used for coliform and EC counts (APHA, 2001; Harmon et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_66]

For the total counts (free + attached bacteria; ECtot and TCtot), 1ml was removed from each [SOURCE: Page_66]

sample (or incubation) after shaking to ensure an even distribution of bacteria, the sample was [SOURCE: Page_66]

then aseptically delivered to the center of a Petrifilm EC plate. The water sample was then left to [SOURCE: Page_66]

stand for 1h and a second 1ml aliquot was inoculated onto a second Petrifilm EC plate to [SOURCE: Page_66]

estimate the number of free EC (ECfree or TCfree; i.e. non-sedimented). The Petriﬁlm EC plates [SOURCE: Page_66]

were then incubated in triplicate at 37° degrees for 24 hours, using a Fukusima incubator [SOURCE: Page_66]

(Japan). The number of colonies (EC and TC) was determined using a Colony Counter CL-560 [SOURCE: Page_66]

(Sibata, Japan). To facilitate the comparison of our data with that of previously published data [SOURCE: Page_66]

and with water quality limits, we express our data as the number of colony forming units per 100 [SOURCE: Page_66]

ml (CFU 100ml-1) of sample. [SOURCE: Page_66]

65 [SOURCE: Page_66]

The number of attached EC or TC (ECatt or TCatt) is determined from the difference [SOURCE: Page_67]

between ECtot (or TCtot) and ECfree (or TCfree) as: [SOURCE: Page_67]

ECatt = (ECtot – ECfree) or TCatt = (TCtot – TCfree); [SOURCE: Page_67]

and the percent of attached EC or TC (%ECatt or %TCatt) calculated as: [SOURCE: Page_67]

%ECatt = [ECatt/ECtot] * 100% or %TCatt= [TCatt/TCtot] * 100% [SOURCE: Page_67]

The die-off rates of TC and EC were estimated by fitting an exponential equation to [SOURCE: Page_67]

bacterial abundances measured over time. The equations were expressed as first order decay in [SOURCE: Page_67]

the general form of: [SOURCE: Page_67]

Ct = Co * e(-kt) [SOURCE: Page_67]

Where Ct = is the number of EC or TC at elapsed time t, Co is the initial number of EC or [SOURCE: Page_67]

TC per ml, k is the decay constant in day-1 and t is the elapsed time in days. As in several [SOURCE: Page_67]

incubations complete die-off was observed by day 4, k is calculated for the first 4 days for all [SOURCE: Page_67]

incubations to ensure comparability between dates and stations. The k value was determined for [SOURCE: Page_67]

both the free and total fractions (e.g. ECfree, TCfree, ECtot, and TCtot). [SOURCE: Page_67]

All statistical analyses were performed with XLSTAT (v. 2014). Pearson’s correlation was [SOURCE: Page_67]

used to test the relationships between environmental variables and FIB. Wilcoxon’s non- [SOURCE: Page_67]

parametric test was used to test for significant differences between variables and the Kruskal- [SOURCE: Page_67]

Wallace test was used to test differences between stations and season as the data were non- [SOURCE: Page_67]

normally distributed even after normalization. When statistical relationships concerning FIB [SOURCE: Page_67]

number were tested, log(EC) or log(TC) was used. When a significant difference was observed, [SOURCE: Page_67]

an a posteriori Dunn’s all-pairwise test was used and significance is determined as p<0.05. [SOURCE: Page_67]

66 [SOURCE: Page_67]

3.2.4 Results [SOURCE: Page_68]

3.2.4.1 Physico-chemical variables [SOURCE: Page_68]

67 [SOURCE: Page_68]

The variability of the physico-chemical parameters observed in the ten stations for the wet [SOURCE: Page_69]

and dry seasons are presented in Figures 3.2 and 3.3. Temperature varied between 9.5 and 35°C [SOURCE: Page_69]

and was significantly higher during the wet season (p< 0.0001). Temperature was generally [SOURCE: Page_69]

highest at Ba Lat and lowest at Gian Khau. [SOURCE: Page_69]

The pH ranged from ranged 7.1 to 8.8, with the lowest values observed at the downstream [SOURCE: Page_69]

Ba Lat station and the highest at upstream Hoa Binh station, however, no seasonal differences [SOURCE: Page_69]

were observed. Salinity was almost 0 at all stations during the entire year; the only exception [SOURCE: Page_69]

was during March at the most downstream station (Ba Lat, located at the river mouth and under [SOURCE: Page_69]

tidal influence) where a salinity of 8.5 was observed (data not shown). The highest [SOURCE: Page_69]

conductivities were also observed at this station at this time (1410 µScm-1). At the other stations, [SOURCE: Page_69]

conductivity varied between 136-423 µScm-1 (Fig. 3.2) and had no significant seasonal pattern. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Concentrations of NH$_4$, PO$_4$ and TP are shown in Fig. 3.3. Ammonium varied from 0.01 to [SOURCE: Page_69]

0.6 mg N l-1 and was significantly higher during the dry season (November – April) at low [SOURCE: Page_69]

dilution and in the downstream stations (Gian Khau, Nam Dinh, Truc Phuong and Ba Lat) of the [SOURCE: Page_69]

highly populated delta area. Similar to NH$_4$, the PO$_4$ tended to be higher during the dry season [SOURCE: Page_69]

however the difference was not significant. TSS was significantly higher during the wet season, [SOURCE: Page_69]

when particle load is higher. Over all, concentrations were generally lowest at the most [SOURCE: Page_69]

downstream station Ba Lat in July and the highest at in the upstream Yen Bai station. As for [SOURCE: Page_69]

TSS, TP was significantly higher during the wet season (p<0.012). Interestingly, high [SOURCE: Page_69]

concentrations of TP were found at Yen Bai and Gian Khau during the dry season (January) [SOURCE: Page_69]

when values of up to 1.39 and 1.59 mg PO$_4$ l-1 were observed. [SOURCE: Page_69]

3.2.4.2 FIB abundance: monthly observation [SOURCE: Page_71]

The ECtot, and TCtot numbers for each season at the ten different stations are shown in Fig. [SOURCE: Page_71]

### 3.4. The number of TCtot varied between < 100 CFU 100ml-1 at Hoa Binh in January to over

39100 CFU 100ml-1 at Yen Bai in September. During the wet season, TCtot at Yen Bai, the most [SOURCE: Page_71]

upstream station, was 9783 ± 14431 (mean ± standard deviation) CFU 100ml-1 as compared to [SOURCE: Page_71]

4383 ± 3797 CFU 100ml-1 for this station during the dry season. For the other upstream [SOURCE: Page_71]

tributaries, relatively low mean TCtot numbers were observed during the wet season (1850 ± 715 [SOURCE: Page_71]

and 800 ± 789 CFU 100ml-1 for Hoa Binh and Vu Quang, respectively. The highest mean values [SOURCE: Page_71]

were found in the downstream delta stations with 6066 ± 4506, 5408 ± 5379 and 6050 ± 5469 [SOURCE: Page_71]

CFU 100ml-1 for Nam Dinh, Truc Phuong and Ba Lat, respectively. The overall pattern was [SOURCE: Page_71]

similar for the dry season, i.e. higher mean values at Yen Bai and in downstream delta stations [SOURCE: Page_71]

such as the peri-urban Nam Dinh station where a seasonal average of 5016 ± 5840 CFU 100ml-1 [SOURCE: Page_71]

was found. When the data from all of the stations were combined TCtot exhibited a significant [SOURCE: Page_71]

seasonal difference with lower numbers during the dry season as compared to the wet season [SOURCE: Page_71]

(Fig. 3.4; p= 0.042). [SOURCE: Page_71]

As for TCtot, low numbers of ECtot were found in the upstream tributaries during wet season [SOURCE: Page_71]

(283 ± 248 and 150 ± 234 CFU 100ml-1 at Vu Quang and Hoa Binh, respectively), and high [SOURCE: Page_71]

numbers were observed at Yen Bai (3108 ± 5960 CFU 100ml-1). The more anthropogenically [SOURCE: Page_71]

impacted downstream stations of the delta had high values during wet season (e.g. 1733 ± 1632 [SOURCE: Page_71]

CFU 100ml-1 at Nam Dinh). During the dry season, a similar pattern was observed with high [SOURCE: Page_71]

mean ECtot numbers at Nam Dinh (2266 ± 3007 CFU 100ml-1) and low values in the upstream [SOURCE: Page_71]

stations (167 ± 122, 83 ± 75.7 CFU 100ml-1 for Vu Quang and Hoa Binh, respectively). [SOURCE: Page_71]

Regarding the dataset as a whole, there was no significant difference between the wet and dry [SOURCE: Page_71]

70 [SOURCE: Page_71]

seasons for ECtot.ECtot numbers were significantly lower than TCtot (p<0.05) during both seasons [SOURCE: Page_72]

at all of the particle attached FIB. [SOURCE: Page_72]

The percentage of ECatt and TCatt varied greatly between stations (Fig. 3.5). For example, [SOURCE: Page_72]

the percentage of ECatt varied between 9.7 % and 100 % with a mean (± se) of 46.5 ± 2.9%. The [SOURCE: Page_72]

percentage of TCatt was also highly variable (2.7 – 100 %) with a mean 34.8 ± 1.8% for the whole [SOURCE: Page_72]

data set combined. The number of ECatt or TCatt was significantly lower than for ECfree or TCfree [SOURCE: Page_72]

when the entire dataset was examined (p<0.05). [SOURCE: Page_72]

The number of ECatt differed significantly between stations (p<0.05) and was highest at [SOURCE: Page_72]

Yen Bai (4900 CFU 100ml-1) and lowest at Hoa Binh (100 CFU 100ml-1), similar to what was [SOURCE: Page_72]

observed for the total number (free + attached). As with ECatt, the number of TCatt differed [SOURCE: Page_72]

significantly between stations. TCatt was highest at Yen Bai (13050 CFU 100ml-1) and the [SOURCE: Page_72]

lowest at Hoa Binh (< 100 CFU 100ml-1). The numbers of free and attached TC and EC were [SOURCE: Page_72]

positively correlated with each other and had correlation coefficients of 0.73 and 0.81, [SOURCE: Page_72]

respectively. This indicates that attached and free TC and EC increased concomitantly even [SOURCE: Page_72]

though the actual numbers were different. [SOURCE: Page_72]

71 [SOURCE: Page_72]

3.2.4.3 Die-off rates [SOURCE: Page_75]

Figure 3.5: Percentage [SOURCE: Page_75]

TCatt and ECatt for each [SOURCE: Page_75]

of the 10 stations. The [SOURCE: Page_75]

mean        and     standard [SOURCE: Page_75]

are        given.     Filled [SOURCE: Page_75]

The die-off rates of EC and TC calculated over 4 days are shown in Table 3.4. In general, [SOURCE: Page_75]

die-off rates for TCtot were significantly higher than for ECtot. For example, at Yen Bai in [SOURCE: Page_75]

August, the die-off rate for TCtot was 1.33 d-1 compared to 0.50 d-1 for ECtot and 1.03 d-1 and [SOURCE: Page_75]

0.43 d-1 TCtot and ECtot respectively, at Truc Phuong during February. It is interesting to mention [SOURCE: Page_75]

again the specific behavior of FIB at Yen Bai. Along with the high TC and EC numbers at this [SOURCE: Page_75]

station, TCtot die-off rates were higher than at the other three stations examined e.g. 1.33 d-1 as [SOURCE: Page_75]

compared to 0.70 d-1, 1.03 and 0.42 d-1 for Hanoi, Truc Phuong and Gian Khau, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_75]

Similarly, ECtot die-off rates at Yen Bai were also significantly higher (p<0.05) than those [SOURCE: Page_75]

74 [SOURCE: Page_75]

observed for the three other stations. However, and in contrast to TCfree, we observed a [SOURCE: Page_76]

significant station effect for ECfree with the rates found at Yen Bai and Truc Phuong being [SOURCE: Page_76]

significantly higher than those found at the two other stations, Hanoi and Gian Khau. Overall, [SOURCE: Page_76]

die-off rates were significantly higher for TCfree than for TCtot (p<0.05) when the whole data set [SOURCE: Page_76]

was examined. In contrast, the opposite was true for ECtot and ECfree (p<0.05). [SOURCE: Page_76]

3.2.4.4 Relationships between FIB and environmental factors [SOURCE: Page_76]

When the data set was regarded either as a whole or by station, EC and TC (free, attached [SOURCE: Page_76]

and total) numbers were only positively correlated with TSS and TP (p<0.05; Table 3.5). No [SOURCE: Page_76]

other significant relationship was found between EC or TC and the measured environmental [SOURCE: Page_76]

variables (pH, DO, NH$_4$, conductivity, or PO$_4$). [SOURCE: Page_76]

As for TCtot and ECtot numbers, the die-off rates of both TCfree and ECfree and TCtot and [SOURCE: Page_76]

ECtot were uncorrelated with temperature, DO, NH$_4$ or conductivity (Table 3.6). However, ECtot [SOURCE: Page_76]

die-off rate was significantly correlated with TP and TSS. No significant correlations were [SOURCE: Page_76]

observed for TCtot and TCfree and TSS. [SOURCE: Page_76]

3.2.5 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_78]

3.2.5.1 Distribution of FIB [SOURCE: Page_78]

Land use affects the abundance and distribution of FIB in both tropical (Crowther et al., [SOURCE: Page_78]

2002; Causse et al., 2015) and temperate regions (Boyer and Pasquarell, 1999; Isobe et al., [SOURCE: Page_78]

2004). It is also probably the case in the Red River system. In general, mean FIB numbers (EC, [SOURCE: Page_78]

TC) in the upstream stations (e.g. Vu Quang, Hoa Binh, Son Tay) were lower than the [SOURCE: Page_78]

downstream stations (Nam Dinh, Truc Phuong and Ba Lat). Of the ten stations investigated, the [SOURCE: Page_78]

upstream Hoa Binh station on the Da River had the lowest FIB levels. The Da River sub-basin is [SOURCE: Page_78]

primarily forest (74% of total sub-basin area) and population density is sparse (<100 people per [SOURCE: Page_78]

km-2) and this probably explains the low FIB numbers at this station. In contrast, Yen Bai on the [SOURCE: Page_78]

Thao River has the highest and most variable levels of contamination, despite being upstream. [SOURCE: Page_78]

The Thao River basin has a relatively high population density (190 people km-2) and a large [SOURCE: Page_78]

proportion of the total sub-basin area (33%) is used for agriculture and livestock grazing. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Livestock are known to be an important source of FIB contamination in streams and rivers [SOURCE: Page_78]

(Crowther et al., 2002) and this along with non-treated human wastewater from the surrounding [SOURCE: Page_78]

population may well explain the high and variable FIB numbers observed at this site during both [SOURCE: Page_78]

seasons. Furthermore, the Van Yen industrial zone which processes agricultural products is [SOURCE: Page_78]

located in the upstream Thao River and this is probably also a source of FIB. Indeed, the high [SOURCE: Page_78]

and variable FIB numbers observed during the wet season also suggest that FIB originated [SOURCE: Page_78]

mostly from diffuse runoff sources. [SOURCE: Page_78]

The higher FIB abundances observed in the downstream stations (Nam Dinh, Ba Lat and [SOURCE: Page_78]

Truc Phuong) are also probably related to the surrounding land use. These three sites are in peri- [SOURCE: Page_78]

77 [SOURCE: Page_78]

urban areas of the delta with high population densities (1200 people km-2 compared to 100-150 [SOURCE: Page_79]

people km-2 in the upstream basin(Le et al., 2007)), agriculture and industry all of which release [SOURCE: Page_79]

untreated wastewater into the system. Although the Hanoi station is located in the city and the [SOURCE: Page_79]

FIB numbers are relatively low (Fig. 3.4), they are still above the acceptable limits for [SOURCE: Page_79]

individual, non-commercial water supplies in Viet Nam (20 EC CFU 100 ml-1 and 150 TC CFU [SOURCE: Page_79]

100 ml-1) (MoH, 2009). Domestic wastewater from Hanoi city is mostly discharged into small [SOURCE: Page_79]

urban rivers as shown by the high ammonium concentrations in the To Lich and Nhue Rivers [SOURCE: Page_79]

(Luu et al., 2010; Trinh et al., 2012) and other work from the urban rivers in Hanoi city has [SOURCE: Page_79]

reported very high TC numbers. For example, TC numbers at Lien Mac, at Phu Van Bridge [SOURCE: Page_79]

(Nhue River) and at Thanh Liet Dam (To Lich river) were 7820, 9820 and 148480 MPN 100ml - 1 , respectively (Cuong, 2012) meaning that the water at these locations is considered as being [SOURCE: Page_79]

unfit even for irrigation due to its high FIB load. [SOURCE: Page_79]

3.2.5.2 Free and attached FIB [SOURCE: Page_79]

Understanding the relative numbers of attached and free FIB and their differential fate [SOURCE: Page_79]

allows us to better estimate the time these indicator organisms remain viable in the environment. [SOURCE: Page_79]

In soils and sediments, bacteria tend to be associated with particles as opposed to in the free- [SOURCE: Page_79]

state (e.g. Oliver et al., 2007) whereas in aquatic systems the percentages of particle associated [SOURCE: Page_79]

bacteria are highly variable and can range from 10% in clear waters with very low organic [SOURCE: Page_79]

particle loads to over 70% in estuaries with high particle loads (Crump et al., 1998; Lemee et [SOURCE: Page_79]

al., 2002). Suter et al. (2011) have shown that high proportions of FIB are associated with [SOURCE: Page_79]

particles (52.9% ± 20.9% and over 90% in some areas) and that, perhaps unsurprisingly, these [SOURCE: Page_79]

values were related to turbidity levels. [SOURCE: Page_79]

78 [SOURCE: Page_79]

In our work, free-living bacteria generally predominated (only 36% ± 20% of TC were [SOURCE: Page_80]

particle attached and 50% ± 26.9% of EC particle attached). However, the %ECatt and %TCatt [SOURCE: Page_80]

were highly variable (7.8% to 100% and 2.7% to 80% for ECatt and TCatt, respectively). Previous [SOURCE: Page_80]

studies from temperate systems have indicated that the attachment to particles by FIB in the [SOURCE: Page_80]

aquatic environment is influenced by various factors, including temperature, bacterial genotype, [SOURCE: Page_80]

soil particle size, organic matter, pH, ionic strength, dissolved nutrients and turbidity (Pachepsky [SOURCE: Page_80]

et al., 2006; Garcia-Armisen and Servais, 2009). We also found significant correlations among [SOURCE: Page_80]

TCatt, ECatt, TSS and TP. In one of the few articles investigating the factors controlling FIB [SOURCE: Page_80]

concentrations in the tropics, Byamukama et al. (2005) working in Uganda, also found that FIB [SOURCE: Page_80]

were correlated with TSS concentrations as we show here. The Red River system has high [SOURCE: Page_80]

turbidity levels (Le et al., 2007; Vinh et al., 2014) and this may explain the relatively high [SOURCE: Page_80]

%ECatt and %TCatt observed in this system. Moreover, attachment to particles probably plays a [SOURCE: Page_80]

strong role in controlling the transport of FIB in the system as well (Ribolzi et al., 2016a). [SOURCE: Page_80]

Particles in aquatic systems are often associated with nutrients and particulate carbon and [SOURCE: Page_80]

phosphorus in particular, both of which can be limiting substrates for bacterial growth and in [SOURCE: Page_80]

turn, can affect FIB survival in non-host environments (Rochelle-Newall et al., 2015) and in [SOURCE: Page_80]

systems with high particle loads, the TP pool is generally dominated by the particulate fraction, [SOURCE: Page_80]

as is the case here. This, along with higher carbon concentrations in the particles probably [SOURCE: Page_80]

confers a competitive advantage to the bacteria thereby enhancing their survival. However, for [SOURCE: Page_80]

the moment, little other information exists on the proportions of attached and free FIB in other [SOURCE: Page_80]

tropical water bodies and so it is difficult to compare our results with other tropical ecosystems. [SOURCE: Page_80]

Nevertheless it also appears that TSS and TP plays an important role in determining the [SOURCE: Page_80]

proportions of attached and free FIB in this highly turbid, tropical riverine system. [SOURCE: Page_80]

79 [SOURCE: Page_80]

3.2.5.3 Die-off rates [SOURCE: Page_81]

Schumacher (2003) in an in-stream incubation in the Upper Shoal Creek Basin, [SOURCE: Page_81]

Southwestern Missouri, found that fecal coliform and EC densities decreased more than 90 % [SOURCE: Page_81]

from initial densities over a 42h period. Troussellier et al. (2004) also reported rapid die-offs [SOURCE: Page_81]

with over 90% of FIB lost over a 128h period. We also observed a rapid decrease in EC and TC [SOURCE: Page_81]

over the 5 days incubation, with in many cases up 100% loss after 120h. We therefore chose to [SOURCE: Page_81]

calculate the decay constants over the first 4 days. Our TC and EC die-off rate decay constants [SOURCE: Page_81]

(k) ranged from a minimum of 0.01 d-1 to a maximum of 1.13 d-1 for EC and from 0.17 d-1 to [SOURCE: Page_81]

1.33 d-1 for TC, with a mean of 0.36 ± 0.21 d-1 for ECfree and 0.44 ± 0.23 d-1 for ECtot and 0.83 ± [SOURCE: Page_81]

0.19 d-1 for TCfree and 0.61 ± 0.28 d-1 for TCtot. These rates are similar to other studies from [SOURCE: Page_81]

temperate aquatic environments. For example, Menon et al.(2003), working in the Seine river, [SOURCE: Page_81]

France observed die-off rates of 0.19 to 0.82 x 10-3 h-1 for EC and Blaustein et al. (2013), in a [SOURCE: Page_81]

review on EC survival in a range of temperate aquatic environments reported an average rate of [SOURCE: Page_81]

0.725 ± 0.078 d-1. In one of the few articles dealing with die-off rates in sub-tropical systems, [SOURCE: Page_81]

Chan et al (2015) also found values of between 0.85 to 1.50 d-1 for the coastal water around [SOURCE: Page_81]

Hong Kong, similar to the values we report in this work. [SOURCE: Page_81]

80 [SOURCE: Page_81]

We observed higher die-off rates for TCfree than for TCtot, however, this was not the [SOURCE: Page_83]

case for EC, where die-off was significantly higher for ECtot than for ECfree. Some authors [SOURCE: Page_83]

(Davies et al., 1995; Craig et al., 2004) have shown lower decay rates for fecal bacteria [SOURCE: Page_83]

attached to sediments as compared to free fecal bacteria, as we show here. Attachment to [SOURCE: Page_83]

particles may protect FIB against grazing by small heterotrophic nanoflagellates (the main [SOURCE: Page_83]

grazers of bacteria in aquatic systems) as well as providing a micro-environment rich in [SOURCE: Page_83]

nutrients. Although, according to Sinton et al. (2002) and Chan et al. (2015), exposure to solar [SOURCE: Page_83]

radiation and predation are among the most important factors controlling die-off, although it [SOURCE: Page_83]

seems from our work that attachment to particles can also be an important factor at least for [SOURCE: Page_83]

TC. [SOURCE: Page_83]

We observed systematically higher die-off rates for TCtot than for ECtot. Why this might [SOURCE: Page_83]

be the case in this system merits some reflection. The appropriateness of using TC and EC as [SOURCE: Page_83]

indicators of faecal contamination in tropical systems has been questioned by several authors [SOURCE: Page_83]

(Nshimyimana et al., 2014). Indeed, it has been shown that E.coli may be able to persist and [SOURCE: Page_83]

even proliferate for some time in tropical freshwaters (Carillo et al., 1985; Jiminez et al., [SOURCE: Page_83]

1989; Winfield and Groisman, 2003) particularly in those with high temperatures and [SOURCE: Page_83]

elevated nutrient and organic matter concentrations. This may well explain the differences in [SOURCE: Page_83]

die-off rates between EC and TC. It may also be why die-off in ECfree was lower than that for [SOURCE: Page_83]

ECatt. Although the technique used in this work allowed us to determine the presence or [SOURCE: Page_83]

absence of TC or EC, it provides no information on the sources of the TC or EC. Therefore, if [SOURCE: Page_83]

the FIB are from different origins with different levels of adaptation to the environment, then [SOURCE: Page_83]

this may explain some of the differences between the die-off rates of free and attached TC and [SOURCE: Page_83]

EC. Nevertheless, this clearly is a hypothesis that merits further investigation in situ using [SOURCE: Page_83]

some of the newer microbial source tracking techniques (Rochelle-Newall et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_83]

82 [SOURCE: Page_83]

Many studies have indicated that seasonal variations can influence FIB die-off rates [SOURCE: Page_84]

through modifications of temperature, pH, nutrient concentration, and dissolved oxygen [SOURCE: Page_84]

(Burton et al., 1987; Flint, 1987). In this study, die-off rates were determined over the whole [SOURCE: Page_84]

year with temperatures varying between 9.5 and 35 °C and we did not observe any significant [SOURCE: Page_84]

relationship with in situ temperature. Moreover, we only observed a significant correlation [SOURCE: Page_84]

between temperature and TCfree in contrast to what has been found by other investigators [SOURCE: Page_84]

(Crowther et al., 2001; Isobe et al., 2004). We did, however, find significant differences in [SOURCE: Page_84]

FIB numbers and die-offs between the wet and dry seasons, emphasizing the dominant role of [SOURCE: Page_84]

the hydrology. Why we did not find a clear temperature relationship for FIB number or die- [SOURCE: Page_84]

off rate is not clear from the data, however, it is probably related to other environmental and [SOURCE: Page_84]

source related factors not investigated in this work. [SOURCE: Page_84]

3.2.6 Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_84]

There have been many studies on the survival of EC and other indicators of faecal [SOURCE: Page_84]

pollution in freshwater, marine and estuarine habitats in temperate ecosystems however less [SOURCE: Page_84]

information exists for tropical aquatic environments. Crane and Moore (1986) observed that [SOURCE: Page_84]

the identification of relationships between environmental and physical parameters and FIB [SOURCE: Page_84]

survival was a fundamental topic for future research in the field. Although their work dates, [SOURCE: Page_84]

and much work has been published since on temperate ecosystems, it is still the case that very [SOURCE: Page_84]

little information is available on die-off rates in tropical and sub-tropical ecosystems where [SOURCE: Page_84]

the management of FIB concentration is most needed and where the risks to human [SOURCE: Page_84]

populations are highest (Rochelle-Newall et al., 2015). In our work from the sub-tropical Red [SOURCE: Page_84]

River system in Viet Nam, we found that FIB numbers exceed Viet Namese water quality [SOURCE: Page_84]

guidelines of 20 and 150 CFU 100ml-1 for EC and TC, respectively, throughout the whole [SOURCE: Page_84]

83 [SOURCE: Page_84]

year at almost all of the 10 stations investigated. Moreover, many values exceeded 500 [SOURCE: Page_85]

colonies 100 ml-1 above which the World Health Organization considers that there is a 10% [SOURCE: Page_85]

risk of gastro-intestinal illness after one single exposure. Therefore, the use of water from [SOURCE: Page_85]

sites with high FIB numbers such as those in the downstream sites pose a real risk to public [SOURCE: Page_85]

health. We also found a significant correlation between particles (as TSS or TP) and FIB as [SOURCE: Page_85]

has been observed in temperate riverine ecosystems. However, in contrast to other studies we [SOURCE: Page_85]

did not find a significant correlation between FIB and temperature for this subtropical [SOURCE: Page_85]

environment, but instead a correlation with discharge (wet vs. dry season). Indeed, the highest [SOURCE: Page_85]

TSS concentrations and FIB numbers were found during the wet season at high discharge. It [SOURCE: Page_85]

is therefore probable that in developing countries where sanitation facilities are deficient and [SOURCE: Page_85]

where people and their livestock live in close proximity to the river, FIB contamination [SOURCE: Page_85]

mostly originates from diffuse sources (Causse et al., 2015). Nevertheless, the data presented [SOURCE: Page_85]

here, notably that of FIB numbers and their respective die-off rates, provides a base for the [SOURCE: Page_85]

application of a model that will allow the parameterization of FIB dynamics in Red River and [SOURCE: Page_85]

delta and potentially in other large river basins of the sub-tropical and tropical belt. [SOURCE: Page_85]

84 [SOURCE: Page_85]

3.3 Modeling of Faecal Indicator Bacteria (FIB) in the Red River basin, Viet Nam (Article 2): [SOURCE: Page_86]

This chapter is in submission to the “Environmental Monitoring and Assessment” as: [SOURCE: Page_86]

Huong Thi Mai Nguyen, Gilles Billen, Josette Garnier, Emma Rochelle-Newall, Olivier [SOURCE: Page_86]

Ribolzi, Quynh Thi Phuong Le. Modeling of Faecal Indicator Bacteria (FIB) in the Red River [SOURCE: Page_86]

basin (Viet Nam). [SOURCE: Page_86]

Author contributions: GB, JG, QTPL designed the experiments; HTMN carried out the [SOURCE: Page_86]

work; HTMN, GB, JG interpreted the results and HTMN wrote the manuscript that was [SOURCE: Page_86]

revised and improved by all the coauthors. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Keywords: sub-tropical watershed modeling, future scenarios, water quality, faecal coliforms, [SOURCE: Page_86]

point and non-point sources [SOURCE: Page_86]

85 [SOURCE: Page_86]

3.3.1 Abstract [SOURCE: Page_87]

Many studies have been published on the use of models to assess water quality through [SOURCE: Page_87]

fecal contamination levels. However, the vast majority of this work has been conducted in [SOURCE: Page_87]

developed countries and similar studies from developing countries in tropical regions are [SOURCE: Page_87]

lacking. Here we use the Seneque/Riverstrahler model to investigate the dynamics and seasonal [SOURCE: Page_87]

distribution of FIB in the Red River (Northern Viet Nam) and its upstream tributaries. The [SOURCE: Page_87]

results of the model show that, in general, the overall correlations between the simulated and [SOURCE: Page_87]

observed values of fecal indicator bacteria (FIB) follow a 1: 1 relationship at all examined [SOURCE: Page_87]

stations. They also show that FIB numbers are affected by both land use in terms of human and [SOURCE: Page_87]

livestock populations and by hydrology (river discharge). We also developed a possible [SOURCE: Page_87]

scenario based on the predicted changes in future demographics and land use in the Red River [SOURCE: Page_87]

system for the 2050 horizon. Interestingly, the results show only a limited increase of FIB [SOURCE: Page_87]

numbers compared with the present situation at all stations, especially in the upstream Vu [SOURCE: Page_87]

Quang station and in the urban Ha Noi station. This is probably due to the dominance of [SOURCE: Page_87]

diffuse sources of contamination relative to point sources. [SOURCE: Page_87]

86 [SOURCE: Page_87]

3.3.2 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_88]

Access to clean water is one of the primordial necessities for humans. Water used for [SOURCE: Page_88]

drinking, washing, and recreation needs to be free from biological, chemical, and physical [SOURCE: Page_88]

contaminations in order to protect human health (Palaniappan et al., 2010). However, poor [SOURCE: Page_88]

water quality continues to pose a significant threat to human health worldwide. Over twenty [SOURCE: Page_88]

years ago Tebbutt (1992) reported that in tropical regions some of the most common human [SOURCE: Page_88]

diseases are due to the use and consumption of contaminated water, and this is still the case. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Inadequate water, sanitation and hygiene are estimated to be responsible for 842,000 deaths [SOURCE: Page_88]

every year, including the death of 361,000 children under five years of age, many of which [SOURCE: Page_88]

occur in developing countries (WHO, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_88]

Contamination of surface water by faecal matter is the major mechanism by which [SOURCE: Page_88]

pathogens attain high concentrations in aquatic systems. Faecal contaminants originate from [SOURCE: Page_88]

many different sources, including wastewater from sewage treatment plants, industrial parks [SOURCE: Page_88]

and residential areas. Animal husbandry and runoff from grazing pastures are also important [SOURCE: Page_88]

sources (Crowther et al., 2002), particularly when the animals have unrestricted acces to [SOURCE: Page_88]

waterways (Collins et al., 2005; Wilcock et al., 2006). In rural areas in developing countries [SOURCE: Page_88]

wash-off from outside latrines during rain events may also be an important source of faecal [SOURCE: Page_88]

contamination to streams (Causse et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_88]

Fecal indicator bacteria (FIB) are used as a measurement of the sanitary quality of water [SOURCE: Page_88]

that is used for domestic, industrial, agriculture and leisure activities. The term FIB is used to [SOURCE: Page_88]

describe the bacterial community that is released in faecal matter of homeothermic animals [SOURCE: Page_88]

(e.g. humans and other mammals). The group includes Escherichia coli and coliforms, [SOURCE: Page_88]

Salmonella spp., Enterococcus spp., all of which are used as a proxy for detecting the presence [SOURCE: Page_88]

87 [SOURCE: Page_88]

of other pathogenicbacteria in environmental samples such as soil and water (Ishii and [SOURCE: Page_89]

Sadowsky, 2008; Byappanahalli et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_89]

Although the initial presence of these bacteria in water is due to faecal contamination, [SOURCE: Page_89]

their survival and transfer varies as a function of environmental influences such as sunlight, [SOURCE: Page_89]

temperature, competition with other bacteria, predation, and other pollutants such as agro- [SOURCE: Page_89]

chemicals (Chan et al., 2015; Rochelle-Newall et al., 2015). Moreover, mobilization and [SOURCE: Page_89]

transfer of FIB and other pathogens in an ecosystem is strongly affected by hydrology, soil [SOURCE: Page_89]

characteristics and land use in catchments (Chu et al., 2011; Ribolzi et al., 2016a; Ribolzi et [SOURCE: Page_89]

al., 2016b). [SOURCE: Page_89]

Given the strong links between hydrology and the transfer of FIB in aquatic systems, [SOURCE: Page_89]

there is a growing demand for modeling methods that allow the assessment of water quality. A [SOURCE: Page_89]

variety of models have been used to predict the distribution of FIB in streams and rivers. [SOURCE: Page_89]

Generally, they are divided into two types: the first uses a multivariate linear regression model [SOURCE: Page_89]

approach to create a link between the input variables (meteorological, hydrological, physical [SOURCE: Page_89]

chemistry, land use) and the output of FIB concentrations to predict water quality (e.g. Eleria [SOURCE: Page_89]

and Vogel, 2005; Frick et al., 2008; Nevers and Whitman, 2011; Tilburg et al., 2015). This [SOURCE: Page_89]

type of model has to be calibrated on a large set of observations and they lose their predictive [SOURCE: Page_89]

capacity outside the range of conditions covered by the calibration situations. The second type [SOURCE: Page_89]

are mechanistic (or process-based) models (e.g. Wilkinson et al., 1995; Brauwere et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_89]

They tend to describe the dynamics of FIB within the environment based on the detailed [SOURCE: Page_89]

kinetics of the processes involved. Observation data are used for validating such models (or [SOURCE: Page_89]

assessing their degree of accuracy), however, they are usually not used calibrate the kinetic [SOURCE: Page_89]

parameters, which are separately determined. These models have therefore a much higher [SOURCE: Page_89]

capacity to predict the impact of potential future changes in land use and human activities in [SOURCE: Page_89]

the watershed on water quality and FIB concentrations. 88 [SOURCE: Page_89]

The Seneque/Riverstrahler model is of this second type. It was developed for the study of [SOURCE: Page_90]

ecological function and nutrient transfer in large drainage networks in France (Billen et al., [SOURCE: Page_90]

2007), central Europe (Garnier et al., 2002) and in France (Servais et al., 2007a). Le et al. [SOURCE: Page_90]

(2010) applied the model to the Red River, North Viet Nam and showed how land use affects [SOURCE: Page_90]

the hydrology and transport of carbon, nitrogen and phosphorus in the basin. Recently, the [SOURCE: Page_90]

model has been adapted to investigate the transport of FIB in Belgium (Ouattara et al., 2013) [SOURCE: Page_90]

and in Laos (Causse et al., 2015). Indeed, this latter article is one of the few that discusses the [SOURCE: Page_90]

modeling of FIB in developing countries. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Recently, Nguyen et al. (2016) showed that concentrations of FIB in the Red River (Viet [SOURCE: Page_90]

Nam) varied between 0 and 39100 colonies 100ml-1, with significantly higher values found [SOURCE: Page_90]

during the wet season as opposed to the dry season. Throughout the year the FIB numbers were [SOURCE: Page_90]

above the acceptable limits for individual, non-commercial water supplies in Viet Nam of 20 [SOURCE: Page_90]

E.coli colonies 100 ml-1 and 150 total coliforms colonies 100 ml-1(MoH, 2009) at most of the [SOURCE: Page_90]

10 stations examined. Here we use the latest version of the FIB dynamics module developed by [SOURCE: Page_90]

Servais et al., (2007b) and Ouattara et al., (2013) to model the distribution and seasonal [SOURCE: Page_90]

dynamics of FIB in the main branch and upstream tributaries of the Red River. To do this we [SOURCE: Page_90]

supplied the model with data on the possible factors driving the input and transfer of FIB in the [SOURCE: Page_90]

Red River flowpath through agricultural and rural areas as well as across densely populated [SOURCE: Page_90]

conurbations (e.g., Ha Noi) where domestic effluents are only partially treated. We then [SOURCE: Page_90]

compared the results with monthly field measurements collected over a three years period from [SOURCE: Page_90]

January 2012 to December 2014. After validation of the model, we tested a scenario based on [SOURCE: Page_90]

predicted future changes in demographics and land use in order to evaluate their impact on FIB [SOURCE: Page_90]

concentrations in the Red River system in 2050. [SOURCE: Page_90]

89 [SOURCE: Page_90]

Figure 3.6: Map of the Red river and localization of the main hydrological and water [SOURCE: Page_91]

quality stations. [SOURCE: Page_91]

3.3.3 Material and methods [SOURCE: Page_91]

3.3.3.1 Study area [SOURCE: Page_91]

The Red River (RR; in Viet Namese: Đồng bằng sông Hồng, or Châu thổ sông Hồng) is [SOURCE: Page_91]

trans-national and the catchment covers parts of China, Viet Nam and Laos (Fig. 3.6). The Red [SOURCE: Page_91]

90 [SOURCE: Page_91]

River Delta itself (RRD) is located in the sub-tropical monsoon zone of the western coastal [SOURCE: Page_92]

Gulf of Tonkin. The Northeast monsoon occurs from November to late April and dryer, colder [SOURCE: Page_92]

weather dominates. From May to October the Southwest monsoon occurs and warmer, wetter [SOURCE: Page_92]

weather dominates. The area is subject to high rainfall that varies from about 1200 – 4800 mm [SOURCE: Page_92]

per year with an average of 1900 mm, of which 80% falls from May to October (Xuan, 2010). [SOURCE: Page_92]

From July to October, the coastal areas of the RRD are affected by low pressure systems of [SOURCE: Page_92]

varying intensity from the South China Sea that bring tropical storms and typhoons. The [SOURCE: Page_92]

coldest month is January, when temperatures can reach 10°C. [SOURCE: Page_92]

The Red River itself is the second largest river system in Viet Nam with an average rate [SOURCE: Page_92]

of flow of 3389 m^3s-1 (for the 7 years period 1997 – 2004 as quoted by Le et al., 2007). It is [SOURCE: Page_92]

formed by three large river branches (Da, Lo and Thao Rivers) and enters into the sea at Ba [SOURCE: Page_92]

Lat. The Da River has the highest discharge (about 42% of the total) and Thao River has the [SOURCE: Page_92]

lowest with only about 19 % (Le et al., 2007). Discharge of the Lo River is intermediate (25.4 [SOURCE: Page_92]

%), however, this river has the smallest catchment of the three. Peak flow occurs during July [SOURCE: Page_92]

(32% frequency), August (55%), September (7.3%), and October (4.4%) (Kok, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_92]

The RRD has a very high population density (average population density is over 1,250 [SOURCE: Page_92]

people per km^2) and industry and agriculture are the dominant economic activies. In the [SOURCE: Page_92]

upstream section, Yen Bai province has 19 industrial and craft production zones and the Hoa [SOURCE: Page_92]

Binh province has 8 economic and industrial zones. In the downstream part (e.g., Viet Tri, Ha [SOURCE: Page_92]

Noi, Hai Phong and Nam Dinh), there are many small and large industrial zones and [SOURCE: Page_92]

urbanisation is increasing. The delta region also hosts other important economic activities such [SOURCE: Page_92]

as fisheries, aquaculture, land reclamation for agriculture, harbour construction mangrove [SOURCE: Page_92]

forestry all of which can negatively affect water quality (Navarro et al., 2012; Trinh et al., [SOURCE: Page_92]

2012; Chu et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_92]

## VHHFKDSWHUVHFWLRQ ZHXVHGGDWDRQ7&IRUWKHPRGHODSSOLFDWLRQDVDUHODWLYHO\ODUJH

 [SOURCE: Page_94]

 [SOURCE: Page_94]

data set on TC exists for the Red River, whereas much less data is available on E. coli numbers [SOURCE: Page_95]

in this system. Many factors impact FIB die-off rates in tropical environments (Rochelle- [SOURCE: Page_95]

Newall et al., 2015) and some of the processes affecting the survival of bacteria in the drainage [SOURCE: Page_95]

network of the RRD have recently been studied (Nguyen et al., 2016). We used a FIB mortality [SOURCE: Page_95]

rate of 0.045 h-1 for the FIB E. coli, as proposed by Servais et al. (2007a). This value also falls [SOURCE: Page_95]

within the range observed during one year at four stations in the Red River (Nguyen et al., [SOURCE: Page_95]

2016). Sedimentation of attached bacteria (considered to represent half the FIB inputs by point [SOURCE: Page_95]

and diffuse sources) is taken into account in the model through a sedimentation velocity of 0.02 [SOURCE: Page_95]

m h-1. The mortality rate of attached FIB in the sediment compartment is set as half of that for [SOURCE: Page_95]

water column organisms (Ouattara et al., 2013) and attached FIB in the sediment are subject to [SOURCE: Page_95]

resuspension when water velocity allows sediment erosion. [SOURCE: Page_95]

3.3.3.3 Point and diffuse sources evaluation [SOURCE: Page_95]

For running the FIB module in the Seneque/Riverstrahler model, inputs of FIB from the [SOURCE: Page_95]

watershed into the drainage network have to be provided. The model distinguishes between (1) [SOURCE: Page_95]

point sources such as the discharge of domestic wastewater that is either directly released or is [SOURCE: Page_95]

treated in wastewater treatment plants (WWTPs) and (2) diffuse sources of FIB from forested [SOURCE: Page_95]

lands, croplands, grasslands, paddy rice fields and urban surfaces. [SOURCE: Page_95]

The point sources are taken into account in the model by considering all potential sources [SOURCE: Page_95]

of wastewater in the watershed based on the urban population census and industrial activities [SOURCE: Page_95]

(Le et al., 2005). A portion of the wastewater from the Ha Noi urban area is now collected and [SOURCE: Page_95]

partially treated by the Yen So infrastructures. The point sources of FIB were determined using [SOURCE: Page_95]

either the urban population (in the case of no wastewater treatment) or the treatment capacity [SOURCE: Page_95]

(in number of inhabitant equivalents) and the type of treatment for each WWTP in the Red [SOURCE: Page_95]

River watershed. [SOURCE: Page_95]

94 [SOURCE: Page_95]

therefore considered as representative of raw wastewater. The flux of TC from each WWTP [SOURCE: Page_97]

was set to 50% as attached bacteria and 50% as free FIB in the model (Ouattara et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_97]

The diffuse sources of TC are calculated on the basis of land use in each sub-basin of the [SOURCE: Page_97]

watershed. The GIS land use coverage information, taken from the data of Le et al.(2005), is [SOURCE: Page_97]

summarized in Fig. 3.7 and also shows the trends of land use changes over the last 50 years. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Data from the literature and from available monitoring programs on the concentration of FIB in [SOURCE: Page_97]

small rivers, streams and ditches draining homogeneous land cover types were used to [SOURCE: Page_97]

characterize TC numbers in surface runoff and in base flow as a function of land used (e.g. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Nguyen, 2006; VIWRR, 2008 ; Nguyen, 2012; Vu, 2012). The TC concentration assigned to [SOURCE: Page_97]

each of the land use classes was determined as the median value of all data compiled from [SOURCE: Page_97]

these empirical surveys (Table 3.8). [SOURCE: Page_97]

An alternative way of defining diffuse sources of FIB was recently proposed by Causse [SOURCE: Page_97]

et al. (2015). These authors showed a robust relationship between the total flux of EC to the [SOURCE: Page_97]

watershed soil reservoir (through grazing livestock excretion, application of manure and human [SOURCE: Page_97]

outside excretion, flux EC (FlxEC)) and the FIB concentration in surface runoff, this [SOURCE: Page_97]

relationship is of general validity whatever the dies off used: [SOURCE: Page_97]

FIB (nb l-1) = 14.6 x 10-6* [FlxFB (nb km-² day-1)] 0.78 [SOURCE: Page_97]

Where FlxFIB is flux FIB to the soil reservoir. [SOURCE: Page_97]

The work of Causse et al. (2015) was conducted in the Nam Khan drainage network in [SOURCE: Page_97]

Laos. Given the strong similarities between the two study sites in Laos and Vietnam (both are [SOURCE: Page_97]

tropical, developing countries in Southeast Asia), it is assumed in this work that the [SOURCE: Page_97]

relationship above is the same in the two systems. Moreover, and as noted by Causse et al. [SOURCE: Page_97]

(2015), it is probable that the equation above for the estimation E. coli concentration in [SOURCE: Page_97]

96 [SOURCE: Page_97]

overland works well in other similar systems, particularly those with similar landuse and [SOURCE: Page_98]

sanitation practices. The application of this relationship to the 4 main sub-watersheds of the [SOURCE: Page_98]

Red River system, considering the rate of FIB inputs to the soil through livestock and rural [SOURCE: Page_98]

human population, provides values in the same order of magnitude, (±20% on average) of the [SOURCE: Page_98]

FIB concentration as those obtained from a weighted mean of the data by land use of Table 3.8, [SOURCE: Page_98]

taking into account the distribution of land use (Table 3.9). This difference is probably due to [SOURCE: Page_98]

differences in land use between the Da and Thao Rivers. [SOURCE: Page_98]

3.3.3.4 Validation data [SOURCE: Page_98]

In order to validate the modelling results, a monthly monitoring survey was carried out [SOURCE: Page_98]

from January 2012 to July 2015 with water samples collected from the ten stations along the [SOURCE: Page_98]

Red River (see Fig. 3.6). Details on this monitoring survey and the data for the first twelve [SOURCE: Page_98]

months can be found in Nguyen et al.(2016). [SOURCE: Page_98]

3.3.3.5 Construction of the 2050 scenario [SOURCE: Page_98]

One of the objectives of this work was to examine the impact of changes in the basin on [SOURCE: Page_98]

FIB numbers under a future of global and local change in 2050. The FAO (2011) evaluated the [SOURCE: Page_98]

effects of climate change on the Red River Delta region and they predict that the annual [SOURCE: Page_98]

average temperature is expected to increase by 1.2°C to 1.3°C and precipitation increase by 3.9 [SOURCE: Page_98]

to 4.1%. However, in terms of bacterial contamination of the river system, the direct effects of [SOURCE: Page_98]

the anticipated changes on diffuse and point sources of FIB are likely to be much larger than [SOURCE: Page_98]

climate change effects. Population in the Red River basin has increased rapidly from 5x^106 [SOURCE: Page_98]

inhabitants in 1950 up to 20x^106 inhabitants in 2005 and this number is expected to have [SOURCE: Page_98]

doubled by 2050 (FAO, 2004). Moreover, most of this increase will occur in urban areas where [SOURCE: Page_98]

population will triple while the rural population, which currently represents about 50% of the [SOURCE: Page_98]

total population, is expected to stay roughly constant. For the upstream part of the watershed, 97 [SOURCE: Page_98]

we therefore considered that the number of urban inhabitants (discharging their wastewater into [SOURCE: Page_99]

the drainage network) will increase by a factor of 3. We assumed that only the agglomerations [SOURCE: Page_99]

larger than 50,000 inhabitants will have a wastewater treatment plant with a basic activated [SOURCE: Page_99]

sludge process, while the smaller villages will release their wastewater without treatment with [SOURCE: Page_99]

75% of that wastewater being recycled in agriculture (Le et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_99]

In the Ha Noi area, the population is predicted to reach 14,000,000 inhabitants in 2050, [SOURCE: Page_99]

which means a 4.7 fold increase with respect to the current population. According to the Prime [SOURCE: Page_99]

Ministers office (PMOV, 2013), the treatment capacity of the Ha Noi district sector will reach [SOURCE: Page_99]

1,896,000 m^3day-1, representing 10,600,000 inhabitants.equivalent i.e., 75% of the total [SOURCE: Page_99]

population. This value takes into account a 30% reduction with respect to current values in per [SOURCE: Page_99]

capita water consumption (reduced to 180 L day-1), as has been observed over the past decades [SOURCE: Page_99]

in Europe). We considered that 50% of this (i.e., 37.5% of the total population) will be treated [SOURCE: Page_99]

by standard activated sludge process and 50% (37.5% of the total) with further treatment [SOURCE: Page_99]

including UV disinfection. The remaining 25% of the Ha Noi population will release its [SOURCE: Page_99]

wastewater without treatment. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Regarding diffuse sources on the 2050 horizon, we took into account both the predicted [SOURCE: Page_101]

land use changes and the expected increase in livestock numbers. The former consists of a [SOURCE: Page_101]

significant increase of agricultural and urbanized surfaces at the expense of rangeland and [SOURCE: Page_101]

forested areas (Fig. 3.7). Livestock numbers are expected to increase considerably based on the [SOURCE: Page_101]

figures for livestock density (Nguyen et al., 2012) and General Statistics Office of Viet Nam in [SOURCE: Page_101]

the Red River basin(GSO, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_101]

Considering the projected increase in livestock density together with a constant rural [SOURCE: Page_101]

human population (Pham, 2014), we estimate that the flux of FIB inputs to soils will be [SOURCE: Page_101]

multiplied by a factor 12 at the 2050 horizon. Based on the approach illustrated in Table 3.10, [SOURCE: Page_101]

we recalculated the FIB concentrations assigned to surface runoff from agricultural land use [SOURCE: Page_101]

classes (Table 3.10). The concentration assigned to base flow is assumed to remain unchanged. [SOURCE: Page_101]

To further evaluate the results, we compared the average observations and the simulation [SOURCE: Page_104]

data for all stations (Fig. 3.10b). Although the averaged simulated FIB concentrations were [SOURCE: Page_104]

lower than those of the observations at Hoa Binh, and higher at Yen Bai and Vu Quang, the [SOURCE: Page_104]

differences were only statistically significant at Yen Bai and Vu Quang (p < 0.05).. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Spatially, the observed and simulated data along the main branches of the three main [SOURCE: Page_104]

tributaries were also compared (Fig. 3.11). Field FIB numbers were averaged for low [SOURCE: Page_104]

(December - March) and high (May - October) discharge periods at each station for the 2011- [SOURCE: Page_104]

2014 period as were the model calculations. In all cases, higher FIB values were observed at [SOURCE: Page_104]

higher rather than low discharge, showing the dominance of diffuse over point sources. In the [SOURCE: Page_104]

Thao River, a sharp decrease of FIB concentration occurs at the confluence of the Da River. [SOURCE: Page_104]

This is due to the much lower FIB concentrations found in the Da River than in the Thao River [SOURCE: Page_104]

which is characterized by a larger proportion of agricultural land (Fig. 3.11). [SOURCE: Page_104]

The much lower FIB concentrations in the Da River may also be due to the Hoa Binh [SOURCE: Page_104]

reservoir which considerably increases the residence time of the water masses, leading to a [SOURCE: Page_104]

strong decrease of FIB through mortality (Fig. 3.11). The same self purification of FIB in [SOURCE: Page_104]

reservoirs is observed in the Lo River downstream of the Dai Thi dam (Fig. 3.6), despite a [SOURCE: Page_104]

much larger proportion of agricultural land use in this basin (Fig. 3.11). A sharp decrease is [SOURCE: Page_104]

also observed at the confluence with the Chay river (at kp 145) on which Thac Ba reservoir is [SOURCE: Page_104]

located (Fig. 3.6; Fig. 3.11). [SOURCE: Page_104]

3.3.4.2 Model Simulation of FIB contamination at 2050 horizon [SOURCE: Page_107]

As explained above, we used the model to predict the future level of FIB for 2050. For [SOURCE: Page_107]

this scenario, FIB within the entire drainage system was calculated using the reconstructed [SOURCE: Page_107]

point and diffuse sources for the predicted 2050 situations and the hydrological constraints of [SOURCE: Page_107]

the 2012-2014 period. The result shows only a limited increase of FIB compared with the [SOURCE: Page_107]

present situation at all stations, especially in the Vu Quang and Ha Noi stations (Fig. 3.11). [SOURCE: Page_107]

This might appear surprising in view of the predicted increase of point and diffuse sources of [SOURCE: Page_107]

FIB at the 2050 horizon. However, the limited model response can be explained by two [SOURCE: Page_107]

reasons. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Regarding point sources, the predicted increase in urban population is for a large part [SOURCE: Page_107]

compensated in the scenario by an increase in wastewater treatment. For instance, although the [SOURCE: Page_107]

total population of Greater Ha Noi is expected to increase by a factor 4.7, only 25% of the [SOURCE: Page_107]

wastewater produced is expected to be discharged without treatment (whereas 100% was [SOURCE: Page_107]

discharged before the implementation of the Yen So facilities in 2014). Regarding diffuse [SOURCE: Page_107]

sources, the predicted increase in livestock numbers led us to predict a 12 folds increase of the [SOURCE: Page_107]

flux of FIB deposited on pasture and agricultural soils. Although this results in a considerable [SOURCE: Page_107]

increase of the FIB concentration of surface runoff issued from these land use classes (Table [SOURCE: Page_107]

3.11), it affects neither the contamination from forested and urbanized areas, nor the FIB [SOURCE: Page_107]

concentration from baseflow. It is therefore the combination of these two factors that explains [SOURCE: Page_107]

the lack of increase in FIB on the 2050 horizon in the Red River system. [SOURCE: Page_107]

106 [SOURCE: Page_107]

Agri land excluding rice                       132,000 [SOURCE: Page_108]

Rice fields                                    310,200 [SOURCE: Page_108]

Urbanized area                                  68,750,000 [SOURCE: Page_108]

3.3.4.3 Respective role of point and non point sources of FIB [SOURCE: Page_108]

Overall, our model results and our observational data, show a initial positive linear [SOURCE: Page_108]

relationship between FIB levels with river discharge, with however very different slopes [SOURCE: Page_108]

according to the subbasins (Fig. 3.12). This is quite different from the expected decreasing [SOURCE: Page_108]

hyperbolic relationship with discharge, often observed in case of point source dominated [SOURCE: Page_108]

systems. The fact that such a pattern is not observed any where in the Red River system, even [SOURCE: Page_108]

in the populated area of the Delta, further indicates the dominance of diffuse pathways over [SOURCE: Page_108]

point sources of FIB contamination. [SOURCE: Page_108]

This is probably a consequence of the very high discharge regime of the Red River, even [SOURCE: Page_108]

compared with the high population of the watershed. The calculation of the respective inputs of [SOURCE: Page_108]

FIB from surface runoff of the different land use classes and from urban point sources (Table [SOURCE: Page_108]

3.8) illustrates this point very well. The budget of FIB inputs and outputs also underlines the [SOURCE: Page_108]

importance of self purification in this system, as the average FIB flux at Son Tay amounts less [SOURCE: Page_108]

than 1% of the total FIB input to the upstream river system from diffuse and point sources. [SOURCE: Page_108]

3.3.4.4 Comparison with other river systems [SOURCE: Page_110]

Figure 3.13 summarises the literature values of FIB contamination downstream of large [SOURCE: Page_110]

urban areas of varying population in rivers of different mean discharge. The ratio of population [SOURCE: Page_110]

to mean river discharge (together with the presence of wastewater treatment facilities) explains [SOURCE: Page_110]

the major trends of the distribution of faecal contamination. Above a ratio of about 10 000 [SOURCE: Page_110]

inhabitants per m^3s-1, FIB contamination increases rapidly to concentrations higher than 106 [SOURCE: Page_110]

FIB L-1, which are dominated by point sources. This is the case for cities like Paris, Reims and [SOURCE: Page_110]

Troyes (Seine River, France; Servais et al., 2007a), Brussels (Zenne river, Belgium; Ouattara et [SOURCE: Page_110]

al., 2013), Nairobi (Nairobi river, Kenya; Musyoki et al., 2013), Hong Kong (DongJiang river, [SOURCE: Page_110]

China; Hong et al., 2010). Below this ratio, the contamination is much lower (although often [SOURCE: Page_110]

above sanitary thresholds) and is controlled by diffuse sources of FIB. This is the case for Ha [SOURCE: Page_110]

Noi city (as shown in this study) but also for Luang Prabang (Nam Khan River, tributary of the [SOURCE: Page_110]

Mekong River, Laos; Causse et al., 2015) and Vienna (Danube River, Austria; Hoch et al., [SOURCE: Page_110]

1996). The ratio of population to average river discharge can vary over a very large range [SOURCE: Page_110]

according to the size of the river and with that of the river draining its wastewater. It is [SOURCE: Page_110]

therefore a useful indicator of whether point or diffuse sources are the likely dominant factor [SOURCE: Page_110]

controlling FIB contamination. Note the case of Meriden (on the Quinnipiac river, USA; Mitch [SOURCE: Page_110]

## HIILFLHQWGLOXWLRQDVDFRQVHTXHQFHRIULYHUKLJKGLVFKDUJH
