3.3.5 Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_112]

Our study is one of the few on faecal contamination of surface water in a large river basin [SOURCE: Page_112]

that covers both urban and rural areas of a tropical, developing country. The [SOURCE: Page_112]

Seneque/Riverstrahler model, which combines a module describing the dynamics of FIB in a [SOURCE: Page_112]

hydro-ecological representation of the whole drainage network of a large regional river basin is [SOURCE: Page_112]

to our knowledge one of the first mechanistic models able to simulate spatial and seasonal [SOURCE: Page_112]

variations of microbial contamination (FIB concentration). The simulations of our model on [SOURCE: Page_112]

FIB concentration compare rather well with field data collected over a 3 years period in [SOURCE: Page_112]

different stretches of the hydrographic network. The Seneque/Riverstrahler model has been [SOURCE: Page_112]

shown a useful tool to explore the impact of wastewater management strategies on microbial [SOURCE: Page_112]

contamination in the rivers of the whole drainage network. We have revealed that fecal [SOURCE: Page_112]

contamination in the Red River is in the lowest range of the values found in ther literature, [SOURCE: Page_112]

thanks to its very high dilution capacity. In such river systems where sanitation is poor, diffuse [SOURCE: Page_112]

sources of contamination predominate over point ones. If the future plans at the horizon 2050 [SOURCE: Page_112]

are achieved, the fecal contamination would not increase considerably, despite a doubling of [SOURCE: Page_112]

the population and a planned considerable increase of the livestock. [SOURCE: Page_112]

111 [SOURCE: Page_112]

4 Organic carbon [SOURCE: Page_113]

112 [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.1 Organic carbon in aquatic systems [SOURCE: Page_114]

4.1.1 Introduction and definition [SOURCE: Page_114]

Figure. 4.1: Global carbon cycle. The stocks (PgC) are noted in “()”, the fluxes are [SOURCE: Page_114]

in black (PgCy-1) and the turnover times are provided as “t”. (Bianchi, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_114]

Particulate organic carbon (POC) and dissolved organic carbon (DOC) make up the total [SOURCE: Page_114]

organic carbon (TOC) pool. TOC is ubiquitous to aquatic and terrestrial ecosystems and forms [SOURCE: Page_114]

a large pool of organic carbon. TOC is the largest pool of reactive carbon in the Earths’ crust [SOURCE: Page_114]

(Fig. 4.1; Bianchi, 2011). Overall, soils contain 1,600 Pg C, sediments 1,000 Pg C and the [SOURCE: Page_114]

dissolved fraction (DOM) represents 685 Pg C in the oceans which is comparable to the [SOURCE: Page_114]

atmospheric CO$_2$ pool. The global estimate of average POC produced as biomass is 1.34 – 6.41 [SOURCE: Page_114]

g C m-2 yr-1 (Stramska, 2009) and the transport of POC and DOC from the terrestrial [SOURCE: Page_114]

environment to the oceans forms a significant link between the terrestrial pool and the oceanic [SOURCE: Page_114]

113 [SOURCE: Page_114]

sink with over 0.9 Pg yr-1 of C being transferred from the terrestrial environment to the oceans [SOURCE: Page_115]

via rivers and streams (Cole et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_115]

TOC plays an important role in ecological processes as its presence is the water column [SOURCE: Page_115]

affects light penetration, water pH, nutrient availability and the toxicity of metals (Dalva and [SOURCE: Page_115]

Moore, 1991; Kraepiel et al., 2003; Roulet and Moore, 2006). Moreover, as OC is the main [SOURCE: Page_115]

nutrition source for aquatic microbes, it also plays a fundamental role at the base of the [SOURCE: Page_115]

foodweb (Azam et al., 1983; del Giorgio and Davis, 2003; Strayer et al., 2008; Findlay, 2010). [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.1.2 Sources [SOURCE: Page_115]

Even clear water, such as that from deep lakes or from the open ocean, contains organic [SOURCE: Page_115]

carbon. DOC, the soluble fraction of the TOC pool can vary in concentration by several orders [SOURCE: Page_115]

of magnitude (0.5 – 50 mgL-1) with concentrations being higher in freshwaters than in the [SOURCE: Page_115]

oceans (Benner, 2002; Mulholland, 2003). In rivers, DOC concentrations, tend to be lowest in [SOURCE: Page_115]

arid regions (1 mgL-1), intermediate in tundra, temperate and dry tropical regions (2 - 4 mgL-1) [SOURCE: Page_115]

and the highest are found in wet tropical and taiga climates (7 - 8 mgL-1) with the global mean [SOURCE: Page_115]

being 6.3 mgL-1 (Meybeck, 1988 as cited in Mulholland, 2003). [SOURCE: Page_115]

Each year, the rivers of the world export 0.9 Pg of C annually of which 50-60% is DOC [SOURCE: Page_115]

(Cole et al., 2007; Jiao et al., 2010). This organic carbon originates from a multitude of [SOURCE: Page_115]

sources, including primary production by freshwater and marine phytoplankton, benthic [SOURCE: Page_115]

vegetation and riverine transport of eroded soils (Mulholland, 2003). Agricultural runoff and [SOURCE: Page_115]

industrial and human wastewater can also bring an important contribution to TOC in aquatic [SOURCE: Page_115]

systems. This can include domestic sewage, pulp mill effluent, discharge from sewage [SOURCE: Page_115]

treatment works, as well as other industrial waste that can contribute a significant [SOURCE: Page_115]

anthropogenic allochthonous input to the water column and sediments (Swinarski et al., 2009; [SOURCE: Page_115]

114 [SOURCE: Page_115]

Renjith et al., 2013; Thevenon et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_116]

Organic matter that originates from terrestrial sources (e.g. soils, vegetation) makes up an [SOURCE: Page_116]

important fraction of the total OM especially in small continental water bodies (Cole and [SOURCE: Page_116]

Caraco, 2001; Cole et al., 2007). In soils, TOC is most concentrated in the surface layer (1 to [SOURCE: Page_116]

20 cm depth). Under natural conditions the content of organic matter in soil is constant, [SOURCE: Page_116]

however, this equilibrium is disturbed when forests are cleared and the land is used for [SOURCE: Page_116]

agriculture (Wu et al., 2003). There is also a decline in soil organic matter when grassland in [SOURCE: Page_116]

the tropics and subtropics is transformed into cropland, or when savannahs are burned (Lal, [SOURCE: Page_116]

2000; Paustian et al., 2000; Wu et al., 2003). [SOURCE: Page_116]

Terrestrially production TOC can be transferred from the soil into the hydrosphere. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Transport of soluble and particulate organic carbon to streams, lakes, and oceans or into [SOURCE: Page_116]

groundwater usually occurs in runoff or via percolation through the soil column in the case of [SOURCE: Page_116]

groundwater (Chaplot et al., 2006; Clay et al., 2010; Dhillon and Inamdar, 2013). DOC [SOURCE: Page_116]

concentrations along overland flow paths can be an order of magnitude higher than [SOURCE: Page_116]

groundwater. For example, in the Amazon overland flow and shallow subsurface flow had 19.6 [SOURCE: Page_116]

± 1.7 mg C l–1 DOC and 8.8 ± 0.7 mg C l–1, respectively, versus 0.50 ± 0.04 mg C l–1 DOC in [SOURCE: Page_116]

emergent groundwater (Johnson et al., 2006). A similar pattern was observed in Laos where [SOURCE: Page_116]

Chaplot and Ribolzi (2014) observed 11.9 ± 0.8 mg C l−1 in overland flow (runoff) compared to [SOURCE: Page_116]

2.3 ± 0.6 mg C l−1 in groundwater. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Most DOC in free-flowing rivers without wastewater inputs is derived from vegetation [SOURCE: Page_116]

and soil organic matter, whereas in dammed rivers phytoplankton primary production can [SOURCE: Page_116]

provide a significant proportion of TOC. This autochthonous production can be the major [SOURCE: Page_116]

source of OM in lakes and is principal source of OM in the open ocean (Baines and Pace, [SOURCE: Page_116]

1991). The amount of phytoplankton production in surface water can vary depending on [SOURCE: Page_116]

latitude, climate, lake depth, clarity and nutrient levels (Lewis, 2011). However, the global 115 [SOURCE: Page_116]

estimated global net primary production per unit area for lakes is estimated to be 260 gC m -2y-1 [SOURCE: Page_117]

with a large proportion of this production coming from the tropical and subtropical latitudes [SOURCE: Page_117]

where a large number of lakes are found (Lewis, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_117]

4.1.3 Role of climate [SOURCE: Page_117]

Climate plays an important role in controlling OC concentration in rivers (Thurman, [SOURCE: Page_117]

1985). Precipitation and temperature independently control terrestrial climate and vegetation in [SOURCE: Page_117]

a watershed. Areas of high precipitation, temperatures, and net primary productivity (NPP) and [SOURCE: Page_117]

extensively weathered soils as are often found in tropical regions, tropical rivers tend to have [SOURCE: Page_117]

high DOC concentrations (Schlesinger, 1997). [SOURCE: Page_117]

Climate change is predicted to decrease the number of rainy days but increase the [SOURCE: Page_117]

average volume of each rainfall event (Bates et al., 2008). The anticipated increases in rainfall [SOURCE: Page_117]

and rainfall intensity will increase erosion, runoff and solid material transportation from land as [SOURCE: Page_117]

well as increasing discharge in rivers and streams thereby altering water quality. Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_117]

these effects on the timing and magnitude of river discharge will probably alter the delivery of [SOURCE: Page_117]

terrigenous organic carbon to the ocean (Nohara et al., 2006; Dai et al., 2009; Dai et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_117]

Consequently, the relative importance of the fluxes of terrestrial and marine organic carbon to [SOURCE: Page_117]

rivers and to the oceans will likely change, as will the processing and preservation of organic [SOURCE: Page_117]

carbon in sediments (Katsev et al., 2006). In areas where droughts are predicted to increase, the [SOURCE: Page_117]

drought–rewetting cycles may impact water quality as it enhances decomposition and flushing [SOURCE: Page_117]

of organic matter into streams (Evans et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_117]

During storm flow, DOC concentration in a river typically rises. Studies attribute this rise [SOURCE: Page_117]

to flushing of DOC-rich soil water, leaching of leaf litter from runoff, and flow of shallow [SOURCE: Page_117]

groundwater through the soil. Kaplan et al (2006) reported a positive correlation between DOC [SOURCE: Page_117]

116 [SOURCE: Page_117]

concentration and baseflow discharge, suggesting that changes from high to low baseflow [SOURCE: Page_118]

conditions were accompanied by subtle shifts in flow paths along hydrologic gradients. DOC [SOURCE: Page_118]

concentrations on the order of 10 mg C l–1 are transported by approximately 4% of stream flow [SOURCE: Page_118]

during storm events, while the remaining 96% of stream flow originates as low-DOC (~0.5 mg [SOURCE: Page_118]

C l–1) base flow (Kaplan et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_118]

Tropical rivers have a critical role in the total global fluvial carbon flux (Huang et al., [SOURCE: Page_118]

2012). Indeed, this region contributes 66.2% of freshwater outflow, 73.2% of sediment loads [SOURCE: Page_118]

and approximately 61% of terrestrial net primary production (Huang et al. 2012). However, [SOURCE: Page_118]

much of the Asian region has experienced dramatic changes in water discharge and sediments [SOURCE: Page_118]

fluxes over the past decades as a consequence of changes in land use and the damming of [SOURCE: Page_118]

rivers (Le et al., 2007; Luu et al., 2010; Vinh et al., 2014; Le et al., 2015). Forest conversion [SOURCE: Page_118]

and other changes in land use have resulted in increases in soil erosion (Valentin et al., 2008), [SOURCE: Page_118]

which have contributed to changing the inputs of carbon and nutrients to rivers and streams [SOURCE: Page_118]

(Quinn and Stroud, 2002; Williams et al., 2010). Although there is evidence that reforestation [SOURCE: Page_118]

can reduce inputs of sediments to rivers and streams (e.g. Zhang et al., 2009). Changes in [SOURCE: Page_118]

temperature and rainfall in the region have also probably impacted the rivers systems (Nguyen [SOURCE: Page_118]

et al., 2014) affecting carbon fluxes in these river systems as well as those of the associated [SOURCE: Page_118]

estuaries, coastal regions and even continental shelf areas (Chen, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_118]

4.1.4 Biodegradability of DOC [SOURCE: Page_118]

In aquatic systems, the fate of organic carbon is controlled in part by its biodegradability [SOURCE: Page_118]

(Findlay, 2003). The biodegradability of DOC in water environments is controlled by a variety [SOURCE: Page_118]

of interacting physical, chemical and biological factors and processes and is generally defined [SOURCE: Page_118]

as a function of the time required by bacteria to degrade organic carbon. The most rapidly used [SOURCE: Page_118]

117 [SOURCE: Page_118]

fraction, the labile fraction, is often produced during photosynthesis and has a turnover time of [SOURCE: Page_119]

minutes to a few days (Fuhrman, 1987; Carlson et al., 1998). The remaining DOC pool is then [SOURCE: Page_119]

divided up into: semi-labile (1.5 years), semi-refractory (20 years), refractory (16,000 years) [SOURCE: Page_119]

and ultra-refractory (40,000 years) (Hansell et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_119]

Although understanding the factors that control bioavailability is justifiable purely on an [SOURCE: Page_119]

ecological basis, in practical terms such studies are also useful as a quantitative measure of [SOURCE: Page_119]

pollution in aquatic systems. Many studies have investigated the biodegradability of organic [SOURCE: Page_119]

carbon in marine, estuarine and lacustrine ecosystems as well as in treated and untreated [SOURCE: Page_119]

wastewaters (Sondergaard and Middelboe, 1995; Stets and Cotner, 2008). For example, [SOURCE: Page_119]

Fellman et al. (2009a) found that BDOC (biodegradable DOC) in a coastal temperate rainforest [SOURCE: Page_119]

watershed ranged between 0.6 – 8.1 mgC l-1. In contrast, BDOC in Lake Taihu (China) ranged [SOURCE: Page_119]

from 0.012 ± 0.001 to 1.23 ± 0.05 mgC l-1 (Yea et al., 2015). In general, as a consequence of [SOURCE: Page_119]

microbial and photodegradation processes and to the presence of humic and fulvic acids, [SOURCE: Page_119]

BDOC in lake waters are lower than other environments, from 12.4% (Søndergaard and Worm, [SOURCE: Page_119]

2000) to 29% (Lønborg et al., 2009). In unpolluted river and sea water showed values of 20% [SOURCE: Page_119]

to 35% for an incubation time of 30 days (Servais et al., 1987). Given the strong links between [SOURCE: Page_119]

BDOC, OC, microbial respiration and CO$_2$ fluxes, it is therefore interesting to look at [SOURCE: Page_119]

bioavailability in a wide range of environments and particularly in tropical areas where less is [SOURCE: Page_119]

known at present. [SOURCE: Page_119]

118 [SOURCE: Page_119]

4.2 Organic carbon transfers in the subtropical Red River system (Viet Nam). Insights on CO$_2$ sources and sinks (Article 3) [SOURCE: Page_120]

This chapter is in preparation for submission in spring 2016 to the journal [SOURCE: Page_120]

“Biogeochemistry” as: [SOURCE: Page_120]

Huong Thi Mai Nguyen, Gilles Billen, Josette Garnier, Quynh Thi Phuong Le, Emma [SOURCE: Page_120]

Rochelle-Newall. Organic carbon transfers in the subtropical Red River system (Viet Nam). [SOURCE: Page_120]

Author contributions: GB, JG, QTPL designed the experiments; HTMN and GB carried out [SOURCE: Page_120]

the work; HTMN, GB, JG interpreted the results and HTMN wrote the manuscript that was [SOURCE: Page_120]

revised and improved by all the coauthors. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Keywords: sub-tropical watershed modeling, organic carbon, point and non-point sources, net [SOURCE: Page_120]

source [SOURCE: Page_120]

119 [SOURCE: Page_120]

4.2.1 Abstract [SOURCE: Page_121]

The Red River, draining a 169,000km² wide watershed, is the second largest river in Viet [SOURCE: Page_121]

Nam and constitutes the main source of water for a large percentage of the population of North [SOURCE: Page_121]

Viet Nam. Here we present the results of an investigation onparticulate organic carbon (POC) [SOURCE: Page_121]

and dissolved organic carbon (DOC) dynamics in the Red River basin, based on measurements [SOURCE: Page_121]

in the main branches of the river as well as on the application of a model of OC dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_121]

POC concentrations ranged from 0.24 - 5.8 mg C L-1 and DOC concentrations ranged from [SOURCE: Page_121]

0.26 - 5.39 mg Cl-1. The model RIVERSTRAHLER / SENEQUE was used to investigate the [SOURCE: Page_121]

dynamics and seasonal distribution of OC in the river. The results of the model show that, in [SOURCE: Page_121]

general, the model simulations of the temporal variations and spatial distribution in OC [SOURCE: Page_121]

concentration followed the observed trends. They also show the importance of the high [SOURCE: Page_121]

population in the watershed on the inputs of OC from surface runoff from the different land use [SOURCE: Page_121]

classes and from urban point sources. A budget of the main fluxes of OC in the whole river [SOURCE: Page_121]

network, including diffuse inputs from soil leaching and runoff, point sources from urban [SOURCE: Page_121]

centers, algal primary production and heterotrophic respiration is established based on the [SOURCE: Page_121]

model results. It shows the predominantly heterotrophic character of the river system, and [SOURCE: Page_121]

allows evaluating the flux of CO$_2$ across the river-atmosphere interface to 330 GgCyr-1, in [SOURCE: Page_121]

reasonable agreement with a few available direct measurements of CO$_2$ fluxes in the [SOURCE: Page_121]

downstream part of the river network. [SOURCE: Page_121]

120 [SOURCE: Page_121]

4.2.2 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_122]

The conversion of natural landscapes to agriculture and urban or industrial areas has [SOURCE: Page_122]

altered the quantity and composition of organic matter delivered to rivers with adverse effects [SOURCE: Page_122]

on ecosystems and society (Aitkenhead-Peterson et al., 2003; 2013). Inputs of dissolved [SOURCE: Page_122]

organic carbon (DOC) from terrestrial sources have increased through the erosion of soil [SOURCE: Page_122]

organic carbon (OC) following the introduction of agriculture, while particulate organic carbon [SOURCE: Page_122]

(POC) from internal sources such as phytoplankton primary have also changed (Jassby et al., [SOURCE: Page_122]

2002; Beman et al., 2005; Stanley et al., 2012). Moreover, in areas where runoff from [SOURCE: Page_122]

agricultural lands has high concentrations of nitrogen (N) or phosphorus (P), primary [SOURCE: Page_122]

production increases, leading to futher increases in POC and DOC concentrations (Howarth [SOURCE: Page_122]

and Paerl, 2008; Howarth et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_122]

The fate of this OC in an aquatic system is determined, in part, by its biodegradability [SOURCE: Page_122]

(Findlay, 2003). In general, biodegradability is defined as a function of the time required by [SOURCE: Page_122]

bacteria to degrade OC. Several fractions based on an estimate of the time required to [SOURCE: Page_122]

remineralise the OC pool have been proposed. The most rapidly used fraction, the labile [SOURCE: Page_122]

fraction, is often produced during photosynthesis and has a turnover time of minutes to a few [SOURCE: Page_122]

days (Fuhrman, 1987; Carlson et al., 1998). The remaining DOC pool is then divided up into: [SOURCE: Page_122]

semi-labile (1.5 years), semi-refractory (20 years), refractory (16,000 years) and ultra- [SOURCE: Page_122]

refractory (40,000 years) (Hansell et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_122]

The biodegradability of OC in aquatic environments has been broadly attributed to a [SOURCE: Page_122]

variety of interacting physical, chemical and biological factors and processes (Findlay, 2003). [SOURCE: Page_122]

Although understanding these factors and their relative importance is justifiable purely on an [SOURCE: Page_122]

ecological basis, in practical terms such studies are also useful as a quantitative measure of [SOURCE: Page_122]

pollution or of carbon stocks and fluxes in aquatic systems. Indeed, many studies have [SOURCE: Page_122]

121 [SOURCE: Page_122]

investigated the biodegradability of OC in marine, estuarine and lacustrine ecosystems as well [SOURCE: Page_123]

as treated and untreated wastewaters (Sondergaard and Middelboe, 1995; Katsoyiannis and [SOURCE: Page_123]

Samara, 2007; Stets and Cotner, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_123]

The biodegradability of OC in aquatic environments also impacts the potential carbon [SOURCE: Page_123]

dioxide (CO$_2$) fluxes across the water-air interface as microbial respiration is a major source of [SOURCE: Page_123]

CO$_2$ to the atmosphere (Richey et al., 2002; Farjalla et al., 2009). When respiratory rates [SOURCE: Page_123]

exceed CO$_2$ fixation in aquatic environments, the system is considered to become a net source [SOURCE: Page_123]

of CO$_2$, to the atmosphere, thereby contributing to the increase of this constituent in the [SOURCE: Page_123]

atmosphere (Raymond et al., 2013). The inverse is also true: when net uptake by primary [SOURCE: Page_123]

production exceeds respiration, the system is considered to be a net sink of CO$_2$. The [SOURCE: Page_123]

proportions of each process (production and respiration) depend on many factors, including the [SOURCE: Page_123]

size of the river (Findlay, 2003), its geology and hydrology (Lapierre and del Giorgio, 2012; [SOURCE: Page_123]

Maberly et al., 2013), temperature and light penetration (Lomas et al., 2002; Rochelle-Newall [SOURCE: Page_123]

et al., 2007), the relative abundances of the autotrophs and heterotrophs present (Smith and [SOURCE: Page_123]

Kemp, 1995) as well as the sources and bioavailability of the OC (Raymond et al., 2000; [SOURCE: Page_123]

Bouillon et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_123]

The use of models provides an interesting strategy for studying the stocks and fluxes of [SOURCE: Page_123]

OC in an ecosystem. There have been several studies on OC based on empirical relationships [SOURCE: Page_123]

between OC and attributes of the watershed. For example, authors have examined the [SOURCE: Page_123]

importance of soil characteristics (Aitkenhead-Peterson et al., 2003; Aitkenhead et al., 2007), [SOURCE: Page_123]

watershed dynamics (Band et al., 2001; Raymond and Saiers, 2010), size and slope of basins [SOURCE: Page_123]

(Clair and Ehrman, 1996). However, these models often contain a large number of [SOURCE: Page_123]

environmental variables, so their application to future scenarios of climate change and human [SOURCE: Page_123]

activities is difficult. [SOURCE: Page_123]

122 [SOURCE: Page_123]

There are also models that take into account streamflow characteristics and mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_124]

of particle transport making it is possible to identify and assess the behavior and the interaction [SOURCE: Page_124]

between different compartments and how they change in space and time in the river (Knapik et [SOURCE: Page_124]

al., 2015). However, many of the water quality models do not consider OC as a state variable [SOURCE: Page_124]

(Shanahan et al., 1998). This lack of representation of the various components of OC may [SOURCE: Page_124]

affect the way that the decomposition process is calculated either through a direct impact or [SOURCE: Page_124]

through an indirect impact on the overall understanding of the stocks and fluxes of OC in the [SOURCE: Page_124]

water column. In addition, the existence of incomplete databases, particularly in developing [SOURCE: Page_124]

countries, on OC stocks and fluxes in aquatic ecosystems also further hinders the development [SOURCE: Page_124]

and validation of models of water quality. [SOURCE: Page_124]

The Red River is the second largest river in Viet Nam (after the Mekong River) and one [SOURCE: Page_124]

of the five largest rivers on the coast of East Asia. The Red River Delta (RRD) is the economic [SOURCE: Page_124]

hub of Northern Viet Nam and the majority of the region's population is concentrated in this [SOURCE: Page_124]

basin. Over 30% (24 million) of the total population of Viet Nam (78 million) lives in the Red [SOURCE: Page_124]

River basin, including over 17 million people in the delta (Thanh, 2003). The Red River also [SOURCE: Page_124]

constitutes the main water source for a large percentage of the population of North Viet Nam as [SOURCE: Page_124]

well as being the major outlet for wastewater. However the rapidly increasing urbanization and [SOURCE: Page_124]

the transition of land towards more intensive agriculture and plantation forestry have all been [SOURCE: Page_124]

linked to decreasing water quality in the Red River system (Luu et al., 2010; Le et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_124]

The objective of this work was to identify the seasonal variations of organic carbon [SOURCE: Page_124]

(DOC, POC) and to determine the biodegradable fraction of DOC (BDOC) and POC (BPOC) [SOURCE: Page_124]

in the Red river over an annual cycle. Then the SENEQUE/Riverstrahler model was applied to [SOURCE: Page_124]

quantify riverine organic C dynamics in this large, subtropical river basin over the period 2009- [SOURCE: Page_124]

2014 allowing giving insight on CO$_2$ respiration, and hence balancing between autotrophy and [SOURCE: Page_124]

123 [SOURCE: Page_124]

heterotrophy in the system. [SOURCE: Page_125]

4.2.3 Material and methods [SOURCE: Page_125]

4.2.3.1 Study area [SOURCE: Page_125]

The Red River is a transboundary river basin that flows through Viet Nam, China and [SOURCE: Page_125]

Laos. The total basin area is around 169.000 km^2 of which around 86 660 km^2 (51.35 % of the [SOURCE: Page_125]

entire basin) are in Viet Nam. To the North, 81,240 km^2 (48%) is located in China with the [SOURCE: Page_125]

remaining small section (1,100 km^2 or 0.65%) in Laos (Fig.4.2). [SOURCE: Page_125]

Figure 4.2: Map of the Red River and localization of the main hydrological and water [SOURCE: Page_125]

quality stations. [SOURCE: Page_125]

124 [SOURCE: Page_125]

The Red River basin is delimited between latitudes 20°23' to 25°30' North and longitudes [SOURCE: Page_126]

100°00 to 107°10’ East. The RRD is located in the sub-tropical monsoon zone of the western [SOURCE: Page_126]

coastal Gulf of Tonkin. During the year, the northeast monsoon occurs from November to late [SOURCE: Page_126]

April when dryer, colder weather dominates. From May to October the South-West monsoon [SOURCE: Page_126]

occurs and warmer, wetter weather dominates. The area is subject to high rainfall that varies [SOURCE: Page_126]

from about 1,200 – 4,800 mm per year (IMH, 1997-2004) with an average of 1,900 mm of [SOURCE: Page_126]

which 80% falls from May to October. From July to October, the coastal areas of the RRD are [SOURCE: Page_126]

affected by low pressure systems of varying intensity that bring tropical storms and typhoons [SOURCE: Page_126]

from the South China Sea. [SOURCE: Page_126]

The Red River itself is the second largest river system in Viet Nam with an average rate [SOURCE: Page_126]

of flow of 3,389 m^3s-1 (averaged over the period 1997 - 2004). It is formed by 3 large river [SOURCE: Page_126]

branches (Da, Lo and Thao Rivers) and enters into the sea at Ba Lat. The Da River has the [SOURCE: Page_126]

highest discharge (about 42% of the total) and Thao River has the lowest, just about 19 % [SOURCE: Page_126]

(IMRR, 2010). Discharge of the Lo River is intermediate (25.4 %), however, this river has the [SOURCE: Page_126]

smallest catchment of the three. Peak flow occurs during July (32%), August (55%), September [SOURCE: Page_126]

(7.3%), and October (4.4%). [SOURCE: Page_126]

Samples for DOC, POC and TSS were collected monthly from July 2013 to July 2015 at [SOURCE: Page_126]

10 gauging stations in the Red River Basin (Fig. 4.2). The sample sites are located on different [SOURCE: Page_126]

river branches (distributaries) of the Red River system and include, from upstream to [SOURCE: Page_126]

downstream : Yen Bai (YB) on the Thao River, Hoa Binh (HB) on the Da River, Vu Quang [SOURCE: Page_126]

(VQ) on the Lo River, Gian Khau (GK) on the Day River, Truc Phuong (TP) on the Ninh Co [SOURCE: Page_126]

River, Quyet Chien (QC) on the Tra Ly River, Nam Dinh (ND) on the Dao River and Son Tay [SOURCE: Page_126]

(ST), Ha Noi (HN) and Ba Lat (BL) on the main axe of the Red River (Fig. 4.2). After [SOURCE: Page_126]

collection, the samples were returned to the laboratory in a cooler for analysis. 125 [SOURCE: Page_126]

4.2.3.2 Biodegradability [SOURCE: Page_127]

At four stations a second series of samples was collected for the determination of [SOURCE: Page_127]

biodegradable OC over time from July 2013 to July 2014. These stations were (1) Yen Bai, [SOURCE: Page_127]

located in the upstream main branch of the Red River known as the Thao River; (2) Ha Noi, [SOURCE: Page_127]

after the confluence of three major upstream tributaries of the Da, Thao and Lo rivers; (3) Gian [SOURCE: Page_127]

Khau, a peri-urban river system located in the Red River Delta and, (4) Truc Phuong, located [SOURCE: Page_127]

in the downstream Red River on the Ninh Co River. At each station, 750 ml of sample were [SOURCE: Page_127]

incubated in duplicate in glass bottles at initial in situ temperature and in the dark for 40 days. [SOURCE: Page_127]

The samples were shaken frequently throughout the incubation. Replicate samples from each [SOURCE: Page_127]

duplicate incubation were collected at 0h, 6h, 18h, 32h, 60h, 120h and 960h (40d in total) for [SOURCE: Page_127]

DOC and POC determination. The biodegradable fraction of DOC and POC (BDOC and [SOURCE: Page_127]

BPOC, respectively) was calculated by subtracting the concentrations of DOC and POC during [SOURCE: Page_127]

the incubations at time t^40d from their respective concentrations at t^0 (Servais et al., 1995; [SOURCE: Page_127]

Garnier et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_127]

The annual OC flux (106 kg year-1) was determined for the year from July 2013 to June [SOURCE: Page_127]

2014 using the formula of Verhoff (1980): [SOURCE: Page_127]

 CiQi 6        -1 FluxOC (10 kg year )         = ----------------* Qm *3600 *24 *365 * 10-9           eqn. 1  Qi [SOURCE: Page_127]

Where FluxOC is the OC flux in 106 kg year-1, Ci is the discrete instantaneous concentration [SOURCE: Page_127]

(mgC l-1), Qi is the corresponding instantaneous discharge (m^3s-1) and Qm is the mean discharge [SOURCE: Page_127]

for period of record (m^3s-1). [SOURCE: Page_127]

4.2.3.3       Analytical methods [SOURCE: Page_127]

Suspended Solids (SS) were determined after filtration of a known volume of sample on [SOURCE: Page_127]

126 [SOURCE: Page_127]

to Whatman GF/F glass fibre filters precalcinated at 550 °C for 4h and preweighed. The filters [SOURCE: Page_128]

were then dried for 2 h at 120°C and the weight of the material retained on the filter was [SOURCE: Page_128]

recorded. DOC was determined on the filtrate using a Shimadzu TOC-VE analyzer after [SOURCE: Page_128]

acidification of 30ml of sample with 35µl 85% H3PO$_4$. POC concentrations were determined [SOURCE: Page_128]

on the same filters as for the TSS determination. Filters were then weighed after calcination at [SOURCE: Page_128]

550°C for 4 h. The difference in weight after the 2h at 120°C and after calcination was [SOURCE: Page_128]

multiplied by 0.4 to providean estimation of the POC content (Garnier et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_128]

4.2.3.4 Model description [SOURCE: Page_128]

The SENEQUE/Riverstrahler model was developed for application in large drainage [SOURCE: Page_128]

networks such as the Seine River, France (Billen et al., 1994; Billen and Garnier, 1999), [SOURCE: Page_128]

Scheldt River, Belgium and the Netherlands (Billen et al., 2009; Thieu et al., 2009) and the [SOURCE: Page_128]

Danube (Garnier et al., 2002). It has also been adapted to the Red River in North Viet Nam [SOURCE: Page_128]

(Luu et al., 2010; Le et al., 2015). Although initially developed to investigate water quality and [SOURCE: Page_128]

biogeochemical functioning, it has also been adapted to take into account faecal indicator [SOURCE: Page_128]

bacteria, an important marker of wastewater inputs (Ouattara et al., 2012; Ouattara et al., 2013; [SOURCE: Page_128]

Causse et al., 2015; Nguyen et al., sub). The model uses drainage network morphology, [SOURCE: Page_128]

meteorological conditions, and land-use, point and non-point sources, to calculate geographical [SOURCE: Page_128]

and seasonal variations of the main water quality variables at a 10-day time step. Here the [SOURCE: Page_128]

model was applied to the Red River to simulate the fluxes of OC and to estimate the C [SOURCE: Page_128]

metabolism of this large river system. [SOURCE: Page_128]

The model includes a module describing the dynamics of OC as represented in Fig. 4.3 [SOURCE: Page_128]

(Garnier et al., 2008). In the model, organic matter has two fractions: particulate (HP) and [SOURCE: Page_128]

dissolved (HD). Each of which is sub-divided into 3 classes of biodegradability that are [SOURCE: Page_128]

indicated as HP$_1$, HP$_2$ (rapidly and slowly biodegradable, respectively) and HP$_3$ (refractory) [SOURCE: Page_128]

127 [SOURCE: Page_128]

for particulate stocks, and as HD$_1$, HD$_2$, HD$_3$ for the dissolved pool. The parameters used in [SOURCE: Page_129]

the model were those determined experimentally and shown to be generic by Garnier et al. [SOURCE: Page_129]

(1992), Servais et al.(1999), Menon et al. (2003), Billen and Servais (1989), Billen (1991). Our [SOURCE: Page_129]

measurements of organic matter biodegradability (see below) confirm the coherency of these [SOURCE: Page_129]

values. [SOURCE: Page_129]

The three major tributaries (the Da, the Thao and Lo Rivers) of the Red River are taken in [SOURCE: Page_129]

account explicitly in the model. Each station is denoted as a function of the distance (pK in km) [SOURCE: Page_129]

from an upstream station, namely Lao Cai, Son La, Tuyen Quang cities in the upstream section [SOURCE: Page_129]

of the Thao, Da and Lo rivers, respectively. The main stations on the Thao River are Yen Bai, [SOURCE: Page_129]

Ha Noi, Ba Lat at pK 402, 524, 652, respectively; in the Da River Hoa Binh is at pK 434; and [SOURCE: Page_129]

in Lo River Vu Quang is at pK 135. The remaining part of the drainage network is considered [SOURCE: Page_129]

as idealised Strahler basins according to the methodology of the Riverstrahler model (Ruelland [SOURCE: Page_129]

et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_129]

4.2.3.5       Point and diffuse sources evaluation [SOURCE: Page_129]

For running the OC module in the SENEQUE/Riverstrahler model, inputs of OC from [SOURCE: Page_129]

the watershed into the drainage network have to be provided. The model distinguishes between [SOURCE: Page_129]

(1) point sources such as the discharge of domestic wastewater (either directly released or [SOURCE: Page_129]

treated in wastewater treatment plants - WWTPs) (Table 4.1) Le et al. (2005) and Servais et al., [SOURCE: Page_129]

(2007b) and (2) diffuse sources of OC from forested, crop or grassland (considering paddy rice [SOURCE: Page_129]

fields separately) and urban surfaces (Table 4.2) (Baric and Sigvardsson, 2007; Chu et al., [SOURCE: Page_129]

2010; Stevenson et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_129]

4.2.3.6   Validation data [SOURCE: Page_133]

In order to validate the modelling results, data from the monthly monitoring [SOURCE: Page_133]

surveys (January 2009 to December 2014) at the ten stations along the Red River (see [SOURCE: Page_133]

Fig. 4.2) were used. [SOURCE: Page_133]

4.2.4 Results [SOURCE: Page_133]

4.2.4.1   Distributions of TSS, POC and DOC [SOURCE: Page_133]

TSS, POC and DOC concentration for the 10 sampling stations for the rainy and [SOURCE: Page_133]

dry seasons are presented in Fig. 4.4. TSS concentrations differed significantly between [SOURCE: Page_133]

seasons and stations over the sampling period (p< 0.05; Fig. 4.4) with concentrations up [SOURCE: Page_133]

to three times higher during the rainy as compared to the dry season. [SOURCE: Page_133]

POC concentrations also varied between stations and seasons with a tendency [SOURCE: Page_133]

towards higher values during the dry season when the stations were compared [SOURCE: Page_133]

individually. POC and DOC concentrations were not statistically different between [SOURCE: Page_133]

seasons, although the concentrations were more variable during the rainy season (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_133]

4.4). Overall, as TSS increased, POC also increased, reflecting the importance of POC in [SOURCE: Page_133]

4.2.4.2     Biodegradability of DOC and POC [SOURCE: Page_136]

At all four stations, the initial concentrations of DOC and POC sampled decreased [SOURCE: Page_136]

during the biodegradation incubations with a signficiant effect of season (Fig 4.5). The [SOURCE: Page_136]

concentration of biodegradable DOC (BDOC) was lowest (0.2 mgCl-1) at Yen Bai during [SOURCE: Page_136]

dry season (January 2014) and was highest (2.84 mgCl-1) at Truc Phuong during rainy [SOURCE: Page_136]

season (July 2014). BPOC, the concentration of biodegradable POC was lowest (0.02 [SOURCE: Page_136]

mgCl-1) at Ha Noi during dry season (January 2014) was highest (2.6 mgCl -1) at Gian [SOURCE: Page_136]

Khau during rainy season. Overall, the biodegradable fraction of TOC (BTOC = BPOC [SOURCE: Page_136]

+ BDOC) was 1.54 ± 1.14 mgCl-1 (33% % of the initial TOC), 1.4 ± 0.49 mgCl-1 (35 % [SOURCE: Page_136]

of the initial TOC), 2.43 ± 1.46 mgCl-1 (36 % of the initial TOC), 1.92 ± 0.88 mgCl-1 (44 [SOURCE: Page_136]

% of the initial TOC) at Yen Bai, Ha Noi, Gian Khau, Truc Phuong, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Flux calculation [SOURCE: Page_136]

The POC and DOC flux were calculated from the measured concentration at each [SOURCE: Page_136]

station in Red River over the period 2013-2014. The results are summarized in Table 4.3. [SOURCE: Page_136]

calculated from observed concentration values and daily measurement of discharge. [SOURCE: Page_136]

They show that the flux of the particulate form (POC) is equal or slightly [SOURCE: Page_136]

larger than the dissolved flux at the outlet of the Lo and Thao Rivers, while the 135 [SOURCE: Page_136]

opposite is true at the outlet of the Da River, probably as a result of sedimentation in [SOURCE: Page_137]

the Hoa Binh reservoir. In the main Hong branch, downstream from Son Tay, DOC [SOURCE: Page_137]

dominates the flux of OC. [SOURCE: Page_137]

4.2.4.3 Model simulation of seasonal and geographical variations in the Red River system under current conditons [SOURCE: Page_137]

The model was run for the five years (2009 – 2014) and compared with the observed [SOURCE: Page_137]

temporal changes in bulk OC concentration and that of its biodegradable fraction for both [SOURCE: Page_137]

POC (Fig. 4.6) and DOC (Fig. 4.7). [SOURCE: Page_137]

In general, the model simulations of the temporal variations in OC concentrations [SOURCE: Page_137]

followed the same levels as that of the in situ data measurements, although it was often [SOURCE: Page_137]

lower for POC and higher for DOC. [SOURCE: Page_137]

The spatial distribution of OC in the river branches in terms of kilometric point (pk) at [SOURCE: Page_137]

particular time of the seasonal cycle (Fig. 4.8) was also well represented by the model. For [SOURCE: Page_137]

both the Da and Thao Rivers, DOC concentrations were higher at low discharge than at [SOURCE: Page_137]

high discharge (Fig. 4.8). However, when POC concentrations were simulated; [SOURCE: Page_137]

concentrations were highest during high discharge in the Da River, whereas in the Thao [SOURCE: Page_137]

River, the opposite was found e.g. POC concentrations were highest during low discharge [SOURCE: Page_137]

(Fig. 4.8). In the downstream sector of the Thao (or Hong) River, DOC and POC [SOURCE: Page_137]

concentrations and C respiration rates increased in response to the elevated inputs of [SOURCE: Page_137]

wastewater from this densely populated sector (Fig. 4.9). In the Da River, a strong reduction [SOURCE: Page_137]

of DOC and POC concentration occured in the region of the Hoa Binh lake (Fig 4.10) [SOURCE: Page_137]

concomitant with increased C respiration rates. [SOURCE: Page_137]

4.2.4.4 Carbon metabolism in Red River [SOURCE: Page_143]

The modelling approach used offers the possibility to calculate the complete [SOURCE: Page_143]

carbon metabolism in all river stretches of the drainage network. The spatial variations of [SOURCE: Page_143]

the mean annual respiration and photosynthesis within the two main rivers of the Red [SOURCE: Page_143]

River basin, the Da River and Thao River are shown in Fig. 4.9 and 4.10, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_143]

The model calculations of mean net primary production ranged from zero to 1.44 gCm- 2 -1 d (Fig. 4.9c; Fig. 4.10c), with higher values found during spring (0.19  0.31 gCm-2d-1, [SOURCE: Page_143]

range -0.00 to 1.17 gCm-2d-1) than during summer (1.44  0.26 gCm-2d-1, range from - [SOURCE: Page_143]

0.1 to 1.45 gCm-2d-1). The values of respiration (R) were much higher and ranged from [SOURCE: Page_143]

0.34 gCm-2d-1 at Hoa Binh to 2.28 gCm-2d-1 at Yen Bai. The highest R rates (4.82 gCm- 2 -1 d ) were observed at Yen Bai during the summer. Overall, there is a general [SOURCE: Page_143]

longitudinal trend in both Thao and Da River of increasing R in the downstream [SOURCE: Page_143]

sections. The ratio of production to respiration (P: R) varied from 0.00 to 0.28, reflecting [SOURCE: Page_143]

the dominance of R over P in this system. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Taking into account the morphology (depth, width and length) of each river [SOURCE: Page_143]

stretch, we calculated the annual values of net primary production and heterotrophic [SOURCE: Page_143]

respiration from the model calculated rates for the period 2009-2014 (Table 4.4). [SOURCE: Page_143]

Primary production varied between 40.9 and 57.5 GgCyr-1 around an average annual [SOURCE: Page_143]

value of 49.1 GgCyr-1. Respiration varies from 208.5 to 270 GgCyr-1, with an average of [SOURCE: Page_143]

274.5 GgCyr-1. This shows the predominance of heterotrophic to autotrophic processes [SOURCE: Page_143]

in this system. [SOURCE: Page_143]

The average inputs of OC from point sources (e.g. urban discharge of wastewater) [SOURCE: Page_143]

represented 233 GgCyr-1. Diffuse sources of OC, calculated from annual runoff and [SOURCE: Page_143]

base flow and the associated concentrations for each land use classes, varied from 230 [SOURCE: Page_143]

142 [SOURCE: Page_143]

to 322 GgCyr-1 and average 275 GgC y-1 over the 5 year period. River export of OC at [SOURCE: Page_144]

Son Tay was estimated from calculated concentrations and discharge. It varied from [SOURCE: Page_144]

282 to 359 GgCyr-1 and was 309 GgCyr-1 on average for the 5 years period. Table 4.4 [SOURCE: Page_144]

gathers the model estimates of these different fluxes of the C cycle in the Red River [SOURCE: Page_144]

system up stream from Son Tay, which is represented in Figure 4.11. [SOURCE: Page_144]

144 [SOURCE: Page_145]

4.2.5 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_146]

4.2.5.1     Organic carbon concentration levels [SOURCE: Page_146]

The results found here are similar than those previously found for the Red River in [SOURCE: Page_146]

2008-2009 by Dang focusing more on the upstream part of the basin (Dang, 2011).The [SOURCE: Page_146]

observed and modelled DOC and POC concentrations are similar to other data from [SOURCE: Page_146]

tropical rivers (Table 4.5). For example, Ni et al. (2008) reported means DOC [SOURCE: Page_146]

concentrations of 1.67 mgC L-1 in the Pearl River and Wang et al (2012) in the Yangtze [SOURCE: Page_146]

River (1.6 – 3.3 mgC L-1). However, our values are lower than the regional means of [SOURCE: Page_146]

Huang et al. (2012) (4.93, 5.17, 4.79 mg C l-1) for the tropical and sub-tropical rivers of [SOURCE: Page_146]

the Americas, Asia and Oceania, respectively. Our POC values were also within the [SOURCE: Page_146]

range of those reported by Coynel et al (2005) for the Congo River, although they are [SOURCE: Page_146]

much lower than in the Mississippi River in the U.S. and the Godavari River in India. [SOURCE: Page_146]

In general, mean DOC and POC concentrations followed a similar trend in both the [SOURCE: Page_146]

dry and rainy seasons with lower concentrations in the upstream stations (Yen Bai, Vu [SOURCE: Page_146]

Quang, Hoa Binh), increases in the upper delta (Son Tay, Ha Noi, Gian Khau) and then [SOURCE: Page_146]

lower values at the downstream stations (Quyet Chien, Nam Dinh, Truc Phuong) and at [SOURCE: Page_146]

Ba Lat where there is a dilution by sea water. The low mean POC concentrations at Hoa [SOURCE: Page_146]

Binh and Vu Quang can be attributed to the impact of reservoirs (Son La, Hoa Binh and [SOURCE: Page_146]

Thac Ba reservoirs upstream Hoa Binh and Vu Quang stations) which are known to lead [SOURCE: Page_146]

to reduced TSS and POC concentrations in the downstream reaches (Kim et al., 2000). [SOURCE: Page_146]

Similar variations in DOC and POC concentrations have also been observed in temperate [SOURCE: Page_146]

systems where it has been proposed that the impoundment of reservoirs along rivers [SOURCE: Page_146]

increases the trapping of POC (Downing et al. 2008; Tranvik et al. 2009). Reservoirs [SOURCE: Page_146]

increase the residence time of the water, which may lead to increased biodegradation. 145 [SOURCE: Page_146]

However, during high discharge, residence time is reduced within the dammed sector [SOURCE: Page_147]

and biodegradation and sedimentation is also potentially reduced. The presence of a [SOURCE: Page_147]

number of dams along the Red River may well explain why POC and DOC values are [SOURCE: Page_147]

lower than the global averages and support the seasonal differences in the downstream [SOURCE: Page_147]

waters. [SOURCE: Page_147]

In contrast, the elevated concentrations observed in the mid-reaches are probably [SOURCE: Page_147]

due to the high levels of urbanization and industrialization in this river section. The main [SOURCE: Page_147]

rivers (To Lich, Nhue, Day) run through several densely populated regions such as Ha [SOURCE: Page_147]

Noi and Son Tay where large amounts of industrial effluent, agricultural runoff, and [SOURCE: Page_147]

domestic sewage are discharged into the surface water, resulting in increased levels of [SOURCE: Page_147]

TOC in riverine runoff (Ho and Hui, 2001). Finally, some of the highest DOC [SOURCE: Page_147]

concentrations were observed at Gian Khau. This station is located downstream of the [SOURCE: Page_147]

Van Long nature reserve which forms the largest natural wetland reserve in North of [SOURCE: Page_147]

Viet Nam. This may explain the particularly high DOC concentrations and relatively low [SOURCE: Page_147]

POC concentrations observed, as wetlands are known to play an important role in [SOURCE: Page_147]

catchment carbon dynamics (Twilley et al., 1992; Laudon et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_147]

146 [SOURCE: Page_147]

Netherlands; Bonilla-Findji et al., 2009) or at the Arbutus Lake watershed in New York [SOURCE: Page_149]

State (6-18 %; Kang and Mitchell, 2013). The difference between BDOC in the Red [SOURCE: Page_149]

River and those of the other sites is probable related to the sources of organic matter [SOURCE: Page_149]

(allochtonous – soils and domestic effluents - versus autochthonous-phytoplankton [SOURCE: Page_149]

primary production-) and other processes that influence their concentration in the water [SOURCE: Page_149]

column (Ribes et al., 1999). Due to the tight coupling between terrestrial and aquatic [SOURCE: Page_149]

ecosystems (Fellman et al., 2009b; Wang et al., 2012), and to the high suspended solid [SOURCE: Page_149]

concentration in the Red River, the majority of its organic matter is thought to be of [SOURCE: Page_149]

terrestrial rather than phytoplanktonic origin (Dixon et al., 2014). The highly significant [SOURCE: Page_149]

correlation between POC and TSS concentration in the Red River (p < 0.0001) and the [SOURCE: Page_149]

lower DOC:POC ratios points towards soil organic material as being the dominant [SOURCE: Page_149]

source of POC in riverine runoff of the Red River as opposed to being of autochthonous [SOURCE: Page_149]

origin (i.e. from in situ primary production). [SOURCE: Page_149]

The sub-tropical climate of the Red River also may play a role as it is known that [SOURCE: Page_149]

the remineralisation of DOC is strongly related to temperature due to the tight [SOURCE: Page_149]

relationship between bacterial respiration and temperature (Rivkin and Legendre, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_149]

We observed a statistically significant seasonal difference in degradability of DOC and [SOURCE: Page_149]

POC between seasons, with higher rates during the warmer, rainy season. This was [SOURCE: Page_149]

despite the fact that there was no significant seasonal difference in DOC or POC bulk [SOURCE: Page_149]

concentrations. Further variable organic matter sources or of differing hydrological [SOURCE: Page_149]

flowpaths may explain these seasonal differences (Schiff et al., 1997; Fenner et al., [SOURCE: Page_149]

2005). [SOURCE: Page_149]

In our biodegradation experiments, it is possible that given the high rates of carbon [SOURCE: Page_149]

remineralisation in some of the incubations, that the concentration of oxygen in the [SOURCE: Page_149]

incubations became depleted. However, the water samples were shaken during the 148 [SOURCE: Page_149]

incubation and this could have allowed oxygen to diffuse into the sample. Nevertheless, [SOURCE: Page_150]

this should be borne in mind. In the Red River itself, oxygen concentrations vary greatly [SOURCE: Page_150]

between season and river stage. Indeed, (LE, 2005) reported that during the rainy season, [SOURCE: Page_150]

oxygen concentrations averaged 5.5 mgO2l-1 in 2003 versus 4.5 mgO2l-1 in 2004 for the [SOURCE: Page_150]

upstream tributaries, and 6.5 mgO2l-1 in 2003 against 4.5 mgO2l-1 in 2004 for the main [SOURCE: Page_150]

branch. (DUONG, 2006) also report oxygen concentrations higher between 7.3 (6.9 – [SOURCE: Page_150]

8.1) mgO2l-1. These differences might be explained by the difference in SS that besides [SOURCE: Page_150]

limiting photosynthesis and algal growth are known to be a support for heterotrophic [SOURCE: Page_150]

bacteria which consume oxygen (e.g (LE, 2005). Moreover, our model results show that [SOURCE: Page_150]

the Red River system is a strongly heterotrophic system, which explains strong [SOURCE: Page_150]

biodegradation is indicated by low DO measured during monitoring. [SOURCE: Page_150]

4.2.5.3     Carbon budget and metabolism [SOURCE: Page_150]

Our model results, as well as our observation data, show negative linear [SOURCE: Page_150]

relationship of OC content with river discharge, although with some seasonal variability. [SOURCE: Page_150]

This is consistent with the model calculated budget of organic carbon (Fig 4.11, Table [SOURCE: Page_150]

4.4) which shows a similar contribution of diffuse pathways over OC point sources in the [SOURCE: Page_150]

drainage network as a whole. There is a quite significant contribution of point sources [SOURCE: Page_150]

from wastewater release, particularly in the populated area of the Delta and in the Great [SOURCE: Page_150]

Ha Noi urban area, but diffuse sources linked to soil runoff and leaching bring again [SOURCE: Page_150]

similar amounts of organic matter, although likely with a lower biodegradability. On the [SOURCE: Page_150]

other hand, primary production is only a modest contributor of organic carbon to the [SOURCE: Page_150]

system. [SOURCE: Page_150]

The differential relationships between DOC and POC and discharge in the Thao [SOURCE: Page_150]

and Da Rivers are probably due to shifts in the dominance of point over diffuse sources. [SOURCE: Page_150]

149 [SOURCE: Page_150]

In the Da river, the sharp increase in POC concentration occurs at the confluence with [SOURCE: Page_151]

the Nam Na River (pk 70, Fig. 4.9). Conversely, in the Thao River, a sharp decrease of [SOURCE: Page_151]

DOC and POC concentrations occurs at the confluence of the Da River (pk 450, Fig. [SOURCE: Page_151]

4.10). This is due to the much lower OC concentrations that are found in the Da River, [SOURCE: Page_151]

which is characterized by the presence of the Hoa Binh reservoir (pk 250 – pk 420, Fig. [SOURCE: Page_151]

4.10) than in the Thao River. Reservoirs, as well as trapping POC, also increase the [SOURCE: Page_151]

potential for phytoplankton blooms which can lead to increased DOC concentrations [SOURCE: Page_151]

through the production of exudates during photosynthesis (Downing et al., 2008; [SOURCE: Page_151]

Tranvik et al., 2009; Vinh et al., 2014). The model calculates however no increased [SOURCE: Page_151]

photosynthetic activity in the reservoirs due to increased depth and low light penetration. [SOURCE: Page_151]

Therefore, it is probable that DOC is originating from allochthonous, terrestrial sources [SOURCE: Page_151]

rather than from autochthonous sources. [SOURCE: Page_151]

Rivers are not just conduits for the transfer of OC from terrestrial systems to the [SOURCE: Page_151]

environment; they also can be a significant source of CO$_2$ to the atmosphere from [SOURCE: Page_151]

outgassing across the water-air interface (Richey et al., 2002; Cole et al., 2007; Farjalla [SOURCE: Page_151]

et al., 2009). Using the model, we determined the rates of P and R in the Red River over [SOURCE: Page_151]

the 2009-2014 periods. Our calculated P rates are higher than those observed in some [SOURCE: Page_151]

other tropical rivers. For example, Davies et al (2008) reported that in less contaminated [SOURCE: Page_151]

river systems rates of 0.01 gC m-2 d-1 to 0.20 gC m-2 d-1 are common. However, in [SOURCE: Page_151]

tropical and subtropical rivers with high nutrient inputs, primary production may exceed [SOURCE: Page_151]

3.84 gC m-2 d-1. The values reported here are within the range reported by Rochelle- [SOURCE: Page_151]

Newall et al (2011) for the Bach Dang estuary, one of the distributaries of the Red River. [SOURCE: Page_151]

These low values of primary production are most probably due to the high turbidities in [SOURCE: Page_151]

the Red River as it is known that turbidity, along with nutrient concentrations are known [SOURCE: Page_151]

to be a major factor controlling primary production rates in this and other aquatic 150 [SOURCE: Page_151]

systems (Fisher et al., 1988; Rochelle-Newall et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_152]

The model calculations of median respiration rates (R) 1.44 0.864 gC m-2 d-1 are [SOURCE: Page_152]

consistently higher than the rates of primary production. Moreover, the ratio of [SOURCE: Page_152]

production to respiration (P:R) varied from 0.00 to 0.28. This imbalance between P and [SOURCE: Page_152]

R, also observed in other river systems and estuaries, and particularly in tropical ones [SOURCE: Page_152]

(Richey et al., 2002; Borges et al., 2015), is explained by the high respiration rates [SOURCE: Page_152]

reflecting a high degree of allochthonous inputs to the Red River system (Sarma et al., [SOURCE: Page_152]

2009). [SOURCE: Page_152]

We compared our respiration rates with the results of direct CO$_2$ flux [SOURCE: Page_152]

measurements that were made with floating chambers at several stations in the Red River [SOURCE: Page_152]

during the wet season in 2014. Values were in the range 0.48 to 5.52 gC m-2 d-1 (Le, [SOURCE: Page_152]

unpublished data). Considering a total area of 600 km² for the river-atmosphere interface [SOURCE: Page_152]

of the whole drainage network (about 430 km² for the main branches and 170 km² for [SOURCE: Page_152]

basins), this range corresponds to 100-1200 GgC yr-1. Our model estimated respiration [SOURCE: Page_152]

rate for the whole drainage network is thus in the middle of the range of the few [SOURCE: Page_152]

available measurements of CO$_2$ efflux from Le (unpublished data). This confirms the [SOURCE: Page_152]

coherency of our model of OC of the whole Red River drainage network. [SOURCE: Page_152]

4.2.6 Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_152]

Overall, the Red River system appears to be a strongly heterotrophic system, [SOURCE: Page_152]

receiving high amounts of allochtonous organic carbon from diffuse sources (soil [SOURCE: Page_152]

leaching and erosion, with only limited biodegradability), and from wastewater inputs [SOURCE: Page_152]

(with high biodegradability). In contrast, autochtonous primary production only [SOURCE: Page_152]

151 [SOURCE: Page_152]

contributes about 10% of the total OC inputs. About half the total inputs of OC to the [SOURCE: Page_153]

system are respired and lost to the atmosphere, while the remaining part is exported to [SOURCE: Page_153]

the estuarine and coastal zone. These results further underline the importance of tropical [SOURCE: Page_153]

rivers and estuaries in the global carbon budget and highlight the need for more research [SOURCE: Page_153]

on these systems that represent 66.2 % of the total global freshwater flow (Huang et al., [SOURCE: Page_153]

2012). [SOURCE: Page_153]

5 General conclusions and perspectives [SOURCE: Page_155]

154 [SOURCE: Page_155]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_156]

5.1 General conclusions [SOURCE: Page_156]

The work presented in this thesis focuses on the observation and modeling of organic [SOURCE: Page_156]

carbon (OC) and faecal indicator bacteria (FIB) in urban and rural areas of the Red River [SOURCE: Page_156]

basin, Viet Nam. Achieving and maintaining good water quality is a challenging problem for [SOURCE: Page_156]

both developing and developed countries. Untreated domestic, agricultural and industrial [SOURCE: Page_156]

wastewaters are significant sources of OC and faecal contaminants in aquatic ecosystems, [SOURCE: Page_156]

both of which degrade water quality. This is particularly problematic in developing countries [SOURCE: Page_156]

where efficient wastewater treatment is lacking and where human populations are rapidly [SOURCE: Page_156]

increasing, becoming more urban and increasingly industrialized. Moreover, waterborne [SOURCE: Page_156]

pathogens and OC from wastewater are particularly susceptible to shifts in water flow and [SOURCE: Page_156]

quality and the predicted increases in rainfall and floods due to climate change will only [SOURCE: Page_156]

exacerbate the problems of contamination. It is therefore imperative that we have an [SOURCE: Page_156]

understanding of the distribution and the factors that control the distribution and dispersion of [SOURCE: Page_156]

water borne pathogens. [SOURCE: Page_156]

This PhD thesis research examined the FIB and OC in the Red River system. The Red [SOURCE: Page_156]

River basin in Northern Viet Nam, is the country’s most densely populated region (GSO, [SOURCE: Page_156]

2013). Red River Delta is also a major economic area and is considered the industrial heart of [SOURCE: Page_156]

Viet Nam. This data, along with a large dataset of collected data points from a range of [SOURCE: Page_156]

sources was then used to model OC and FIB in the 4 main sub- basins (Da, Lo, Thao and [SOURCE: Page_156]

Delta) of the Red River system. Indeed, although many studies have been published on the [SOURCE: Page_156]

use of models to assess water quality through faecal contamination levels, the vast majority of [SOURCE: Page_156]

this work has been conducted in developed countries and similar studies from developing [SOURCE: Page_156]

countries in tropical regions are lacking. A schematic of the work is presented in Figure 1. [SOURCE: Page_156]

155 [SOURCE: Page_156]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_157]

Figure 5.1: Overview of the thesis [SOURCE: Page_157]

Wastewater can contribute large concentrations of OC as well as faecal microorganisms [SOURCE: Page_157]

to the aquatic systems. OC concentrations can reach as high as 3000 mgL-1 and FIB can be as [SOURCE: Page_157]

high as 107-1010 MPN 100ml-1 in raw, untreated wastewater (von Sperling, 2007). In this [SOURCE: Page_157]

work, the highest values observed were 30.9 mg l-1 OC and 1.1 x 109 MPN 100ml-1. Over and [SOURCE: Page_157]

above the health aspects associated with the use of water contaminated by wastewater, such [SOURCE: Page_157]

high inputs of organic matter and microbes can also have a severe impact on the environment. [SOURCE: Page_157]

High wastewater inputs can severely degrade water quality, fundamentally alter the [SOURCE: Page_157]

biodiversity of an ecosystem (Dutto et al., 2012; Englert et al., 2013) and have important [SOURCE: Page_157]

impacts on the nutrient and carbon cycling of the impacted ecosystem (Brion and Billen, [SOURCE: Page_157]

2000; Garnier et al., 2001; Smith et al., 2011; Trinh et al., 2012; Garnier et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_157]

Moreover, organic matter is also a controlling factor of microbial activity and high [SOURCE: Page_157]

concentrations such as are found in wastewaters may also support the growth of pathogenic [SOURCE: Page_157]

156 [SOURCE: Page_157]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_158]

bacteria present in manure and sewage (Byappanahalli and Fujioka, 1998; Byappanahalli et [SOURCE: Page_158]

al., 2003; Son et al., 2011; Rochelle-Newall et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_158]

In this work, POC and TSS concentrations were significantly correlated (Pearson [SOURCE: Page_158]

correlation = 0.519, p < 0.0001), reflecting the close association between POC and suspended [SOURCE: Page_158]

solids. At the same time, ECtot and ECtot die-off rates was significantly correlated with TSS. It [SOURCE: Page_158]

has been poroposed that sediments serve as a suitable environment for faecal bacterial [SOURCE: Page_158]

survival, perhaps due to the availability of soluble organic matter (Jamieson et al., 2004; [SOURCE: Page_158]

Jamieson et al., 2005). However, at Yen Bai, although this station had the highest TSS [SOURCE: Page_158]

concentrations and the highest number of attached FIB (ECatt and TCatt), this was also the [SOURCE: Page_158]

station where the highest die-off rates were observed, which seems to contradict this idea. [SOURCE: Page_158]

Given the strong relationships between microbial metabolism and organic matter, it may well [SOURCE: Page_158]

be that one of the factors controlling the die-off of FIB is the lability of DOC and POC. This [SOURCE: Page_158]

might be particularly important during the wet season when the highest die-off rates were [SOURCE: Page_158]

observed despite there being high turbidities and high temperatures, both of which are [SOURCE: Page_158]

associated with higher bacteria growth rates (Crump and Baross, 1996; Shiah and Ducklow, [SOURCE: Page_158]

1997). Moreover, the highest DOC and POC biodegradation rates were observed during the [SOURCE: Page_158]

hotter, rainy season (Chapter 4). This relationship of increased FIB die-off rates at higher [SOURCE: Page_158]

temperatures has been observed elsewhere in temperate environments (Ishii et al., 2006; [SOURCE: Page_158]

Chahinian et al., 2012) and it was also observed in this work (Chapter 3). Interestingly, at [SOURCE: Page_158]

Gian Khau where high percentages of ECatt and FCatt were observed, die-off rates of EC and [SOURCE: Page_158]

FC were much lower. This was despite the fact that this station has lower fraction of labile [SOURCE: Page_158]

DOC and POC than at Yen Bai. Of course, it is difficult to draw any firm conclusions without [SOURCE: Page_158]

more data nevertheless it would be interesting to verify if the relative importance of organic [SOURCE: Page_158]

carbon bioavailability and temperature in determining FIB die-off rates in a controlled [SOURCE: Page_158]

laboratory experiments. 157 [SOURCE: Page_158]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_159]

The results of the model (Chapters 3 and 4) reflected the importance of land use, [SOURCE: Page_159]

discharge and the dominance of diffuse over point sources in this network for both FIB and [SOURCE: Page_159]

OC. Indeed, the concentrations observed reflected the large amounts of industrial effluent, [SOURCE: Page_159]

agricultural runoff, and domestic sewage that are discharged into the surface water of this [SOURCE: Page_159]

river system. Although, domestic and industrial wastewater (point sources) are important [SOURCE: Page_159]

sources of FIB and OC in the urban or peri-urban areas such as Ha Noi, Nam Dinh, Truc [SOURCE: Page_159]

Phuong, on the scale of the whole river basin, diffuse pathways dominated over point sources [SOURCE: Page_159]

even in the populated area of the Delta of Red River. The scenario, based on the predicted [SOURCE: Page_159]

changes in future demographics and land use in the Red River system for the 2050 horizon, [SOURCE: Page_159]

showed only a limited increase of FIB numbers compared with the present situation at all [SOURCE: Page_159]

station. This was particularly the case in Ha Noi even though the population is expected to [SOURCE: Page_159]

triple by 2050. This perhaps surprising result further underlines the difficulties that many [SOURCE: Page_159]

developing countries are facing in terms of water quality. On one side, such a result can be [SOURCE: Page_159]

considered as being relatively positive i.e. no decrease in water quality, at least in terms of [SOURCE: Page_159]

FIB loads despite large increases in population. On the other hand, it also means that water [SOURCE: Page_159]

quality (FIB and probably OC concentrations) will not improve in the future unless efforts are [SOURCE: Page_159]

made to control diffuse sources in the basin. However, the setting up and running of such a [SOURCE: Page_159]

program requires extremely large investments, strong legislation and effective education [SOURCE: Page_159]

programs. [SOURCE: Page_159]

Moreover, the high inputs of allochthonous OC from both point and diffuse sources [SOURCE: Page_159]

mean that the Red River system is a strongly heterotrophic system. The high inputs of OC to [SOURCE: Page_159]

the Red River are supporting respiration rates that exceed primary production rates by a factor [SOURCE: Page_159]

of 6. This means that the Red River is strong source of CO$_2$ to the atmosphere. Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_159]

considering the results of Chapter 3, if the scenario for 2050 of FIB is also true for OC inputs [SOURCE: Page_159]

158 [SOURCE: Page_159]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_160]

(i.e. relatively little change in the future despite large increases in the urban populations), it [SOURCE: Page_160]

appears that this system will remain a strong source of CO$_2$ to the atmosphere in the future. [SOURCE: Page_160]

5.2 Directions for future research [SOURCE: Page_160]

Recognizing and understanding the link between human activities, natural process and [SOURCE: Page_160]

microbial functioning and their ultimate impacts on human health are prerequisites for [SOURCE: Page_160]

reducing the risks to the exposed populations. The work presented in this thesis provides, to [SOURCE: Page_160]

our knowledge, some of the first, internationally published data on FIB numbers in the Red [SOURCE: Page_160]

River network. During this thesis, the measured FIB concentrations in the river water were [SOURCE: Page_160]

considerably higher than the clean water limits set by the Viet Namese Government (500 [SOURCE: Page_160]

colonies 100 ml-1 for informal domestic water supplies). While it is difficult to estimate the [SOURCE: Page_160]

number of people using these informal domestic water supplies in the Red River network, on [SOURCE: Page_160]

the scale of the country, WHO estimate that around 97.6% of the population of Viet Nam has [SOURCE: Page_160]

access to “improved” drinking water supplies (WHO, 2012). It can therefore be considered [SOURCE: Page_160]

that the health risks to the population are low, at least in terms of domestic usage. However, [SOURCE: Page_160]

during the hot, rainy season when FIB numbers and OC concentrations are higher, the number [SOURCE: Page_160]

of people bathing in the river network may be higher and the associated health risks may be [SOURCE: Page_160]

higher too. Nevertheless, it would be interesting to link this data with an epidemiological [SOURCE: Page_160]

study to determine the real health risks associated with the use of the river water for leisure or [SOURCE: Page_160]

domestic and agricultural activities. [SOURCE: Page_160]

One of the long term objectives of this thesis work is to participate in the development [SOURCE: Page_160]

of a tool that can be used to aid the construction of strategies for better water quality [SOURCE: Page_160]

management both on the local and, potentially, on the regional scale. A first step towards [SOURCE: Page_160]

attaining this objective is to provide a scientific base for discussion with decision makers on [SOURCE: Page_160]

the future management of wastewater in the Red River system. Potentially, through the 159 [SOURCE: Page_160]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_161]

application of the model to other river systems within the complex drainage network [SOURCE: Page_161]

characterizes Northern Viet Nam. [SOURCE: Page_161]

For the implementation of the Seneque-Riverstrhaler model and the simulations, [SOURCE: Page_161]

previously published values of OC degradation, FIB die-offs and sedimentation rates were [SOURCE: Page_161]

used. For example, we used a OC degradation rate of 0.015 h-1 as proposed by Servais et al. [SOURCE: Page_161]

(2007), and sedimentation of OC is taken into account in the model through a sedimentation [SOURCE: Page_161]

velocity of 0.006 m h-1 to model the fluxes of OC and an FIB mortality rate of 1.08 d-1, as [SOURCE: Page_161]

proposed by Servais et al. (2007a) was used. The values obtained in this work varied between [SOURCE: Page_161]

0.1 and 1.33 d-1 (mean 0.57 d-1 ± 0.29) and so it is probable that improvements could be made [SOURCE: Page_161]

using more site specific values. This is something that could be included in a future version of [SOURCE: Page_161]

the model. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Moreover, light intensity has been identified as one of the most influential factor [SOURCE: Page_161]

causing die-off of FIB (Chan et al., 2015). In this work, die-off rates were conducted in the [SOURCE: Page_161]

dark, which are probably appropriate for the Red River conditions where turbidity is high. [SOURCE: Page_161]

However, in future work, given the importance of light as a mediator of FIB survival, it will [SOURCE: Page_161]

important to take this into account, although, the high turbidities may render the estimation of [SOURCE: Page_161]

the water column light field difficult. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Very little work has been published on FIB in the developing countries of Southeast [SOURCE: Page_161]

Asia, with the notable exception of some work from a rural catchment in Laos (e.g. Ribolzi et [SOURCE: Page_161]

al., 2011; Causse et al., 2015; Ribolzi et al., 2016a). It would therefore be interesting to [SOURCE: Page_161]

investigate the dynamics of FIB and OC e.g. in the Mekong River and delta. The large, [SOURCE: Page_161]

urbanized delta of this transnational river provides a very interesting opportunity of studying [SOURCE: Page_161]

the sources and losses of FIB. Moreover, the presence of a large, shallow, flood plain lake [SOURCE: Page_161]

(Ton Le Sap) in Cambodia, upstream of the delta makes this a particularly interesting site to [SOURCE: Page_161]

study the dynamics of FIB and OC. Moreover, the lower Mekong remains one of the last 160 [SOURCE: Page_161]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_162]

unregulated, given the specific hydrodynamics of the Ton Le Sap – Mekong system, it would [SOURCE: Page_162]

be very interesting to study particularly as in many dams are planned in the future on this [SOURCE: Page_162]

system (Holtgrieve et al., 2013). Ton Le Sap has many floating villages that have release their [SOURCE: Page_162]

wastewater (of both animal and human origin) directly into the lake. Further, by including [SOURCE: Page_162]

specific markers of the origins of FIB (stannols or microbial biomarkers), it would be possible [SOURCE: Page_162]

to clearly identify the sources of the FIB and, potentially, OC and then further refine the [SOURCE: Page_162]

possibilities for management and contamination reduction. [SOURCE: Page_162]

At this stage, besides the acquisition of original data regarding the origins and fateof [SOURCE: Page_162]

faecal contamination, one of the major results of this work concerns the carbon metabolism of [SOURCE: Page_162]

the Red River. We calculated that the system is highly heterotrophic and so these model [SOURCE: Page_162]

results provide an important base for further work. Little data exists on the role of tropical [SOURCE: Page_162]

rivers and estuarine systems as a source or sink of CO$_2$ to the atmosphere and the work [SOURCE: Page_162]

presented here shows that the Red River is a large source of CO$_2$ to the atmosphere. It is [SOURCE: Page_162]

therefore important to conduct in situ measurements over a period of time to provide some [SOURCE: Page_162]

ground-truthing data for these calculations. [SOURCE: Page_162]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_197]

196 [SOURCE: Page_197]

NGUYEN Thi Mai Huong – Thèse de doctorat - 2016 [SOURCE: Page_198]

Abstract In many developing countries, poor water quality poses a major threat to human health and the lack of access to clean drinking water and adequate sanitation continues to be a major brake on development. The Red River is the second largest river in Viet Nam and constitutes the main water source for a large percentage of the population of North Viet Nam. This thesis presents the results from observations and modeling of both faecal indicator bacteria (FIB) and organic carbon (OC) in the Red River system, North Viet Nam. The objective of this work was to measure FIB numbers and OC concentrations in this system and then to model these parameters in order to investigate scenarios for 2050 when population in the area is estimated to have doubled. The dataset was then modeled using the Seneque/Riverstrahler model in order to investigate the dynamics and seasonal distribution of FIB and OC in the Red River and its upstream tributaries.A scenario, based on the predicted changes in future demographics and land use in the Red River system for the 2050 horizon, showed only a limited increase of FIB numbers compared with the present situation. This was particularly the case in Ha Noi even though the population is expected to triple by 2050. The OC inputs and the resulting heterotrophic respiration of this OC resulted in a system that was a strong CO$_2$ source. The model results also reflected the importance of land use, discharge and the dominance of non-point sources over point sources for FIB and OC in the Red River. This thesis provides some new information on FIB in the Red River as well as providing a base for discussion with decision makers on the future management of wastewater in the Red River. Keywords: Red River, Faecal Indicator Bacteria, Organic Matter, Seneque/Riverstrahler model, human impacts Tóm tắt Ởcác nước đang phát triển, ô nhiễm nước đặt ra mối đe dọa lớn đối với sức khỏe con người và thiếu nước sạch và vệ sinh môi trường vẫn tiếp tục là vấn đề chính cho phát triển kinh tế - xã hội. Sông Hồng là con sông lớn thứ hai tại Việt Nam và là nguồn cung cấp nước chính cho bộ phận lớn dân cư ởmiền Bắc Việt Nam. Luận án này trình bày các kết quả quan trắc thực tế và kết quả mô hình hóa về vi khuẩn chỉ thị phân (FIB) và cacbon hữu cơ (OC) ở hệ thống sông Hồng, miền Bắc Việt Nam. Mục tiêu của nghiên cứu này nhằm đo đạc thực tế giá trị FIB và OC trên sông Hồng và sau đósử dụng mô hình mô phỏng các thông số này cho kịch bản năm 2050 khi mà dân số ở khu vực này được ước tính để có tăng gấp đôi. Sử dụng mô hình Seneque/Riverstrahler mô phỏng động học và sự phân bố theo mùa của FIB và OC trong sông Hồng và các sông nhánh thượng lưu. Kết quả mô phỏng từ một kịch bản, dựa trên sự thay đổi trong tương lai về dân số và sử dụng đất trong lưu vực sông Hồng năm 2050, cho thấy chỉ giá trị FIB tăng rất ít so với mô phỏng hiện tại. Điều này là đặc biệt đối với trường hợp tại Hà Nội khi mà dân số dự kiến sẽ tăng gấp ba vào năm 2050.Nguồn cung cấp đầu vào OC và các quá trình hô hấp dị dưỡng OC đã tạo ra trong hệ thống sông một nguồn CO2lớn.Các kết quả mô hình cũng phản ánh mức độ quan trọng của việc sử dụng đất, lưu lượng nước và nguồn thải phát tán hơn so với nguồn thải điểm cho FIB và OC ở sông Hồng.Luận án này cung cấp một số thông tin mới về FIB ở sông Hồng cũng như cung cấp cơ sở khoa học cho các nhà hoạch định chính sách về quản lý nước thải của lưu vực sông Hồng trong tương lai. [SOURCE: Page_198]

Từ khóa: Red River, Vi khuẩn chỉ thị phân, Chất hữu cơ, mô hình Seneque/Riverstrahler, tác động của con người. 197 [SOURCE: Page_198]
